# signing-preview

[![CI](https://github.com/tyrasz/signing-preview/actions/workflows/ci.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/tyrasz/signing-preview/actions/workflows/ci.yml) [![Live verification](https://github.com/tyrasz/signing-preview/actions/workflows/verify-live.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/tyrasz/signing-preview/actions/workflows/verify-live.yml)

Turns any wallet signing request into a human-readable preview before the user signs. It covers Ethereum, Arbitrum One, HyperEVM (chain 999) and Hyperliquid's native HyperCore actions, with special handling for Arbitrum → Hyperliquid deposits; transactions on Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain are simulated too, with their network fees.

It takes a standard EIP-1193 request (`{ method, params }`), the same object every dApp sends to every wallet. It returns plain JSON that any wallet, extension or backend can render.

```
"Withdraw 50 USDC to Arbitrum"
Withdraws 50 USDC from Hyperliquid to your address on Arbitrum One (a 1 USDC fee is deducted).
  You receive on Arbitrum (approx.)   49 USDC
```

This project started in [tyrasz/transaction-lab](https://github.com/tyrasz/transaction-lab) but shares no code with it.

## Quickstart

**Use it in your project.** The package isn't on npm yet; it installs straight from GitHub, which builds it on install:

```bash
npm install github:tyrasz/signing-preview
```

```bash
npx signing-preview
```

The second command starts the HTTP API and demo, with mock data unless `SIGNING_PREVIEW_MODE=live` is set. Installed packages need Node.js 22 or later.

**Work on it.** Developing needs Node.js 22.18 or later, which runs the TypeScript sources directly.

```bash
npm install
```

```bash
npm start
```

Open http://127.0.0.1:8787. The demo is a mock dApp sending requests to a mock wallet that is wrapped with the interceptor. Every balance, price and account in the demo is **mock data** (see `mocks/data.ts`).

```bash
npm test
```

```bash
npm run typecheck
```

## Using it from any wallet or extension

There are three ways to integrate, all producing the same `Preview` JSON.

**1. HTTP API.** Works from anywhere: wallet backends, extensions, mobile apps.

```bash
curl -s localhost:8787/v1/preview -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{
  "request": { "method": "eth_sendTransaction", "params": [{ "from": "0xa11ce00000000000000000000000000000000001",
    "to": "0xaf88d065e77c8cc2239327c5edb3a432268e5831", "chainId": "0xa4b1",
    "data": "0xa9059cbb0000000000000000000000002df1c51e09aecf9cacb7bc98cb1742757f163df70000000000000000000000000000000000000000000000000000000005f5e100" }] },
  "context": { "account": "0xa11ce00000000000000000000000000000000001", "origin": "https://some.dapp" }
}'
```

| Endpoint | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `POST /v1/preview` | Body `{ request, context? }` returns a `Preview`. Malformed requests return `400 INVALID_REQUEST`. |
| `GET /v1/openapi.json` | The OpenAPI 3.1 document for this API. |
| `POST /v1/replay` | Body `{ chainId, hash, trace? }` returns a `Preview` of a mined transaction, run again where it ran (see [Replaying a mined transaction](#replaying-a-mined-transaction)). `404 NOT_FOUND`, `409 PENDING`, `422 UNSUPPORTED` (the chain's own transactions), `503 UNAVAILABLE` (no RPC for the chain). A transaction, block or receipt the node didn't have is asked for once more a second later before it is said not to be there: a public node behind a load balancer can be a moment behind the newest block. |
| `GET /v1/health` | Liveness check. |
| `GET /v1/samples` | The demo's mock requests. |

**OpenAPI.** [`openapi.json`](openapi.json) describes the whole API in OpenAPI 3.1: every request and response schema, error codes, and two live HyperEVM examples (a HyperLend borrow and a Kinetiq stake). Load it into Swagger UI, Postman or Insomnia, or generate types or clients from it. For TypeScript types (`components['schemas']['Preview']`, `paths['/v1/preview']`):

```bash
npx openapi-typescript openapi.json -o signing-preview-api.ts
```

For other languages, [OpenAPI Generator](https://openapi-generator.tech) builds clients for Python, Go, Kotlin, Swift and more; it needs Java.

The document is hand-written and tested against the code:
- `test/openapi.test.ts` validates it against the OpenAPI 3.1 meta-schema.
- Every sample request goes through the real HTTP server, and each response must match the `Preview` schema exactly. Undocumented fields fail the test.
- Every error response is checked too.
- The `Preview` format is versioned by its `schema` field (`signing-preview/1`). New optional fields can appear within a version.

CORS defaults to `*`, so pages, extensions (`chrome-extension://…`) and apps can all call it. Set `CORS_ORIGIN` to restrict it. The API never signs anything. Its outbound calls go only to the RPC and Hyperliquid URLs configured on the server; callers can't supply URLs.

**2. EIP-1193 interceptor (browser, no dependencies).** Wrap whichever provider you have: `window.ethereum`, an EIP-6963 provider, or a WalletConnect provider. Every signing request is previewed, and the request is only forwarded to the wallet if `confirm` resolves `true`. A rejection throws the standard EIP-1193 `4001` error, and other methods pass through untouched.

```js
import { withSigningPreview, remotePreview } from './dist/interceptor.js';

const provider = withSigningPreview(window.ethereum, {
  preview: remotePreview('https://your-host/v1/preview'),
  confirm: async (preview) => showMyDialog(preview),   // resolve true to sign
});
// Hand `provider` to viem/ethers/wagmi as usual.
```

The interceptor fills in `chainId` and `account` with the non-prompting `eth_chainId` and `eth_accounts` calls. If the preview service fails, `confirm` still receives an explicit "Preview unavailable" preview (for `eth_sign`, with its blind-signing **danger**, which a failed preview doesn't lessen); the request is never forwarded silently.

**3. In-process library.** For a wallet, extension or backend that runs the previewer itself, without the API. The package ships compiled ES modules with TypeScript declarations:

```ts
import { previewRequest, StaticTokenRegistry, HttpHyperliquidInfo, RpcSimulator, JsonRpcClient } from 'signing-preview';

const preview = await previewRequest(request, { chainId: 42161, account, origin }, {
  tokens: new StaticTokenRegistry(),
  hyperliquid: { mainnet: new HttpHyperliquidInfo() },
  simulator: new RpcSimulator({ 1: ETH_RPC, 42161: ARB_RPC }),   // optional
  rpc: new JsonRpcClient({ 999: HYPEREVM_RPC }),                 // optional: live protocol state (HyperLend, Kinetiq)
});
```

Or take the same set-up as the server's live mode:

```ts
import { liveDependencies } from 'signing-preview/server';
const deps = liveDependencies({ ARBITRUM_RPC_URL, HYPEREVM_RPC_URL });
```

| Import | Contents | Runs in |
|---|---|---|
| `signing-preview` | `previewRequest`, registries, simulators, ABI and signature sources, all types | Node and browsers |
| `signing-preview/interceptor` | `withSigningPreview`, `remotePreview` | Browsers (no dependencies) |
| `signing-preview/server` | `createPreviewServer`, `liveDependencies`, `openApiDocument` | Node |
| `signing-preview/openapi.json` | The OpenAPI document | anywhere |
| `npx signing-preview` | The HTTP API and demo | Node |

`dist/signing-preview.js` and `dist/interceptor.js` are browser bundles of the same code, included in the package, for pages that load scripts directly.

`npm run check:package`, which also runs in CI, packs the library and installs the tarball into a fresh project. There it imports it from plain JavaScript, type-checks a TypeScript consumer against the published declarations, and starts the `signing-preview` command.

**Publishing to npm.** This is the maintainer's call. Choose the package name (`signing-preview`, or a scope such as `@tyrasz/signing-preview`), remove `"private": true` from `package.json`, and run `npm publish`. The `prepare` script builds the library and bundles first.

## What is decoded

| Flow | Request | Shown |
|---|---|---|
| Arbitrum → Hyperliquid, **Bridge2** | USDC `transfer` to `0x2df1…3df7` | Deposit amount and the credited Hyperliquid account. **Danger** below 5 USDC (funds lost), for a non-USDC token, or on the wrong chain. |
| Arbitrum → HyperCore, **Circle CCTP** (Circle's guide) | 1. `ReceiveWithAuthorization` signature on USDC; 2. `CctpExtension.batchDepositForBurnWithAuth` | Amount, batch split, HyperCore recipient, perps/spot, fee cap, guaranteed minimum and expected credit (0.20 USDC forwarding fee). **Danger** for every mistake that leaves funds stuck (see [Circle CCTP accuracy](#circle-cctp-accuracy)). |
| Ethereum/Arbitrum → HyperCore, **CCTP V2** | `TokenMessengerV2.depositForBurnWithHook` / `depositForBurn` | Same checks; plain CCTP transfers to any other domain are decoded too. Mainnets plus Ethereum Sepolia and Arbitrum Sepolia (→ HyperEVM testnet). |
| HyperCore → Arbitrum (and other chains), **Circle CCTP** | `SendToEvmWithData` with USDC (Circle's "Withdraw USDC from HyperCore to EVM chains") | Destination as a **CCTP domain** (3 = Arbitrum, not an EVM chain ID), automatic vs manual delivery, the live per-destination fee (Arbitrum/Base 0.20, Ethereum 1.20, Solana 0.50 USDC; 0 without forwarding), amount received and withdrawable balance. **Danger** when the HyperEVM step would revert: amount not above the fee, invalid domain, or data over 1,024 bytes. |
| HyperCore → Arbitrum, **Hyperliquid Withdraw** | `Withdraw` (withdraw3) | Arbitrum recipient, the 1 USDC fee, amount received and withdrawable perps balance. |
| HyperCore → HyperEVM | `SendAsset` / `SpotSend` to a system address (Circle's "Withdraw USDC from HyperCore to HyperEVM") | "Move X to HyperEVM", credited to the same address. **Danger** on a system address that doesn't match the token. |
| HyperEVM → HyperCore, **Circle CoreDepositWallet** | `deposit` / `depositFor` / `depositWithAuth` on HyperEVM | Amount, HyperCore recipient and perps/spot. **Danger** for a plain token transfer to the CoreDepositWallet, which Circle says leaves the funds permanently stuck. |
| HyperEVM → other chains, **CCTP** | `TokenMessengerV2.depositForBurn(WithHook)` on HyperEVM | Amount, destination, recipient and whether delivery is automatic or manual. |
| Sponsored HyperCore deposits | `ReceiveWithAuthorization` to `CctpExtensionV2` | Recognised, with a caution that the recipient is bound into the nonce and can't be shown. |
| USDC gasless transfers | EIP-3009 `TransferWithAuthorization` / `ReceiveWithAuthorization` / `CancelAuthorization` | Amount, spender/recipient, validity window; warns that a transfer authorization works like sending the payment. |
| HyperCore **user-signed** actions | `eth_signTypedData_v4`, `HyperliquidSignTransaction` domain | All 17 types in `@nktkas/hyperliquid` 0.33.3: `Withdraw` (net of the 1 USDC fee), `UsdSend`, `SpotSend` (including HyperCore → HyperEVM via system address), `UsdClassTransfer`, `SendAsset`, `SendToEvmWithData`, `ApproveAgent`, `ApproveBuilderFee`, `TokenDelegate`, `CDeposit`/`CWithdraw`, `UserSetAbstraction`, `UserDexAbstraction`, `UserPortfolioMargin`, `LinkStakingUser` (danger: the staking account can later lock this account), `StakingLinkDisableTradingUser` (danger: irreversible), `ConvertToMultiSigUser`, `SendMultiSig`; unknown types list every field. Checks: imitation domains, testnet, expired or future timestamps, a token ID that doesn't match its label. |
| HyperCore **L1** actions | `Agent { source, connectionId }`, `Exchange` domain | **When the original action is supplied** (see below): orders (limit, trigger TP/SL, builder fee), cancels, modify, leverage, isolated margin, top-up margin, TWAP, scheduled cancel, borrow/lend, vault create/modify/distribute/transfer, sub-account transfers (USDC and spot), rename, rewards, referrals, display name, spot dusting, HyperEVM block size. Markets are resolved on main perps, spot, **HIP-3 builder DEXes** and **outcome markets**. Perp orders are margin-checked on their own DEX (skipped for unified/portfolio-margin accounts); spot orders are checked against spot balances. Deployer, validator, agent and outcome-share actions are decoded too, with warnings for oracle updates, trading halts, fee-recipient and permission changes, market settlement, HYPE-spending auctions and backstop-liquidator deposits. |
| HyperEVM, **Kinetiq** liquid staking | kHYPE, and asxnHYPE (an institutional deployment whose StakingManager and StakingAccountant run the same code as kHYPE's, checked by code hash; read under its own symbol): `stake`, `queueWithdrawal`, `confirmWithdrawal` / `batchConfirmWithdrawals`, `instantUnstake`, `redelegate` on the StakingManager | "Stake 10 HYPE for ≈ 9.7315 kHYPE", "Unstake 100 kHYPE: ≈ 102.759 HYPE claimable in 7 days", "Instant unstake … for ≈ X HYPE (0.1% fee)". With an RPC, live values: rate, fees, delays (7 days, or 36 hours when Kinetiq's buffer covers it), Kinetiq's own instant-unstake quote, and when each queued withdrawal becomes claimable. **"This will fail"** for a stake below the minimum (5 HYPE), an amount that isn't a multiple of 0.00000001 HYPE (HyperCore keeps 8 decimals), paused staking or withdrawals, a claim that isn't ready, an empty instant-unstake buffer or a quote below your minimum. |
| HyperEVM, **HyperLend** | Pool `supply`, `withdraw`, `borrow`, `repay` (and `…WithPermit`, `repayWithATokens`), collateral and e-mode switches, `approvePositionManager`, `liquidationCall`; the native-HYPE gateway | Plain-language actions ("Borrow 3,000 USDC from HyperLend", "Withdraw all your WHYPE"), "all" for max amounts, and the **health factor before → after**, from your live position, oracle prices and each market's liquidation threshold (e-mode aware). **Danger** below 1.1; **caution** below 1.5; **"this will fail"** when HyperLend would refuse (borrowing beyond the limit, leaving HF < 1). **Caution** for a position manager, which can switch your collateral and e-mode. Gateway calls are trusted only when the contract's verified source is a WrappedTokenGateway pointing at HyperLend's Pool. |
| HyperEVM, **HyperLend isolated pools** | The pairs in HyperLend's PairRegistry (Fraxlend-style, ERC-4626 for lenders; four on 2026-11-06: WHLP, xHYPE (two) and kmHYPE collateral; later ones read from the registry): `deposit`, `mint`, `withdraw`, `redeem`, `borrowAsset`, `addCollateral`, `removeCollateral`, `repayAsset`, `leveragedPosition`, `repayAssetWithCollateral`, `liquidate` | "Borrow 2,000 USDC, adding 100 xHYPE as collateral (HyperLend isolated xHYPE → USDC)", "Lend 1.7152 WHYPE (HyperLend isolated kmHYPE → WHYPE)", with the pool's maximum LTV. **Caution** when the proceeds go to another address and for a leveraged position; information when collateral backs someone else's loan. From hyperlendx/hyperlend-isolated and the addresses on docs.hyperlend.finance. All 8 direct calls to the pairs in 40,000 recent blocks (7 withdrawals, 1 deposit) decode, with the amount and token their receipts show, 8 of 8. |
| Credit delegation (HyperLend, any Aave-style market) | `approveDelegation` on a debt token; `DelegationWithSig` signatures | **Danger**: the delegatee can borrow in your name, against your collateral, and the debt is yours. Named as HyperLend when the debt token's Pool is HyperLend's and lists it. |
| HyperEVM → HyperCore | CoreWriter `sendRawAction` | All CoreWriter actions (IDs 1–13 and 15–17), with 1e8 price/size scaling. Notes that HyperCore runs the action asynchronously, so a failure there doesn't revert the EVM transaction, and checks the action against the sender's live HyperCore account: spot sends, lending supply and staking deposits against the free spot balance (less what orders hold), USDC class transfers and vault deposits against what's withdrawable, delegating and unstaking against the undelegated stake, undelegating against what's delegated to that validator and its one-day lock-up, and orders against the available margin. **Caution** when HyperCore will reject it. Actions that contracts send through CoreWriter inside a transaction (a vault, a router, a smart account's batch) are listed from the simulation's `RawAction` events, with the same checks against the account they run on. |
| HyperEVM → HyperCore | HYPE to `0x2222…`, ERC-20 to `0x20…<index>` | "Move X to HyperCore". **Danger** if the token isn't the contract linked to that index. |
| Ethereum / Arbitrum / HyperEVM | ERC-20 transfer/approve/transferFrom, `setApprovalForAll`, WETH, Permit2 `approve`, native sends, contract creation | Amounts with the token's symbol and decimals. Warns on unlimited approvals, zero-value transfers and transfers to the token contract itself. |
| Off-chain approvals | ERC-2612 / DAI `Permit`, Permit2 `PermitSingle/Batch/TransferFrom` | Spender, amount and expiry; flags unlimited or long-lived permissions and owner mismatches. |
| Messages | `personal_sign`, SIWE, `eth_sign` | Readable text; SIWE domain vs. requesting origin (phishing); `eth_sign` is always danger, except for the hash of a UserOperation supplied with it ([below](#useroperations-signed-as-a-hash-entrypoint-v06-and-v07)). |
| Off-chain swap orders | CoW Protocol `Order` (domain "Gnosis Protocol" v2), 1inch limit orders (domain "1inch Aggregation Router" v6), UniswapX orders carried as a Permit2 witness (Dutch, V2/V3 Dutch, priority) | "Sell 1,000 USDC for at least 0.3 WETH (CoW Swap)", "Sell 1 WETH for 3,500 USDC (1inch limit order)", "Swap 1,000 USDC for at least 0.295 WETH (UniswapX)" (the least each output can decay to, with fee outputs listed), with expiry and fee. **Danger** for an order that sells for nothing; **caution** when the proceeds go to another address, when the maker isn't the signer, and for a verifying contract that isn't the protocol's. Type definitions checked against CoW's source and 1inch's `OrderLib`, and the 1inch domain against the router's `eip712Domain()`. |
| Arbitrum-style inboxes | `sendL2Message` on any inbox (Arbitrum One's on Ethereum; Orbit chains' on their parent, such as chain 99901's on HyperEVM, the contract with the most distinct senders there in a 400-block sample) | "Send a transaction to chain 99901 through its inbox (to 0xb0b0…0002)": the signed child-chain transaction it delivers (to, value, calldata, nonce) and its signer, recovered from the signature. Information when it relays someone else's transaction. |
| Ethereum staking deposits | `deposit` on the beacon-chain deposit contract (`0x0000…7705fa`) | "Stake 32 ETH for validator 0xa7fb502d…cc35", with who can withdraw the stake: the execution address of 0x01 and 0x02 credentials ("you", or another address), or a BLS withdrawal key (0x00). **Caution** when withdrawals go to an address other than the sender (the stake is theirs when withdrawn, and the credentials can't be changed: how fake staking sites take deposits) and for BLS credentials; **danger** for a deposit the contract will reject: wrong lengths, under 1 ETH, not a whole number of gwei, or a `deposit_data_root` that doesn't match. The root is recomputed as the verified contract does (DepositData's SSZ root, with SHA-256): on all 163 deposits sent to the contract in 7,000 recent blocks it matched. The validator's BLS signature isn't checked. |
| Relay | Relay's `RelayDepository` (the same contract on Ethereum, Arbitrum One and HyperEVM): `depositNative`, both `depositErc20` forms, and `execute` | "Deposit 2,400 USDT to Relay", with the request ID and who is credited; the three-argument `depositErc20` deposits the whole allowance, read from the token. Relay's solver fills the request on the destination chain from Relay's off-chain quote, so the preview says the destination, recipient and amount aren't in the transaction (Relay's API, `api.relay.link`, which would resolve them, isn't reachable from this environment). `execute` runs calls signed by Relay's allocator from the depository: "Relay payout: Send 2,724.72 USDC to you", money in when it pays the sender. **Caution** when a deposit is credited (and refunded) to another address. From the verified source on Sourcify; 171 of 171 recent Ethereum calls decode (113 deposits, 58 payouts). |
| Binance Web3 Wallet | Its swap and bridge router on Ethereum (`0xb300…028d`, an EIP-2535 diamond whose facet's source isn't published) | "Swap 1,500 USDT for at least 1,486.73 USDC (Binance Web3 Wallet)", with the fee taken from the output and the aggregator call it wraps (LI.FI, 1inch, Rango, SWFT or Binance's own). Bridges read as the wrapped LI.FI bridge ("Bridge 0.001 ETH to Arbitrum One via across (LI.FI, Binance Web3 Wallet)") or as "Send 20 USDT through RangoDiamond". The layout (fee receiver, target, input token, amount, fee and output token, minimum, call) was read from real calls and checked against their receipts: in 56 of 56 the sender paid exactly the amount shown; in 28 of 28 swaps with a token output it received at least the minimum shown; the fee matched to the basis point in 5 of 5. Marked partial, as the code is unpublished. **Caution** for a fee of 1% or more (danger at 10%) and for no slippage limit. Arbitrum's diamond runs different facet code, so it isn't decoded. |
| LI.FI | LI.FI's diamond (Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain): every bridge facet (`startBridgeTokensVia*`, `swapAndStartBridgeTokensVia*`) through the `BridgeData` they all take first, and GenericSwapFacet V3 swaps | "Bridge 250 USDC to Arbitrum One via across (LI.FI)", "Swap and bridge 0.1 WETH to Solana via relay (LI.FI)", "Swap 0.1 WETH for at least 290 USDC (LI.FI)": the bridge, destination, amount, source swaps, integrator and recipient. **Caution** when the funds go to another address; information for non-EVM destinations (the recipient is in the bridge's own data) and destination calls. Structs from lifinance/contracts; real transactions on Ethereum decode (bridges through Relay and Across, swaps). |
| Uniswap's older routers | V2 Router02, V3 SwapRouter and SwapRouter02 (Ethereum, Arbitrum, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain, where Uniswap deployed each): V2 and V3 swaps, exact input and output, `multicall` with `unwrapWETH9` | "Swap 100 USDC for at least 0.03 ETH on Uniswap", "Buy 0.1 WETH for at most 400 USDC on Uniswap", summing a multicall's swaps, with pool fees and deadline. **Caution** when the output goes to another address or nothing limits slippage. On 100 sampled Ethereum swaps, the output token matched what each receipt paid the receiver. Other contracts called with these functions (forks, which HyperEVM's main DEXes are) get the same headline under their verified name when their verified ABI has the function; without one, "on a Uniswap-style router (0x…)", marked partial and cautioned, since only the contract's code decides what the call does. |
| Swap and bridge routers | 1inch Aggregation Router v6 (`swap`, the `unoswap` family, limit-order cancels; Ethereum and Arbitrum), OKX's DEX router (Ethereum and Arbitrum: `dagSwap*`, `smartSwapByOrderId`, `unxswap*`, `uniswapV3SwapTo*`), KyberSwap's MetaAggregationRouterV2 (Ethereum, Arbitrum, HyperEVM), MetaMask Swaps and Bridge routers, Across SpokePool deposits (`depositV3`, `deposit`; Ethereum, Arbitrum and HyperEVM, addresses from Across's deployment list) | "Swap 100 USDC for at least 99.8 USDT on 1inch" (for pool-only swaps the output token is read from the pools on-chain, and the last pool's unwrap flag makes it ETH: on 56 sampled 1inch and OKX swaps and 38 KyberSwap swaps, the output token matched what each receipt paid the receiver), "Swap 3,500 USDC with MetaMask Swaps (oneInchV6FeeDynamic)", "Bridge 0.5 ETH to Arbitrum One through Across" with the minimum received there and the recipient. **Caution** when the output goes to another address, when nothing limits slippage, and when an Across refund would go to someone else. These contracts' sources are verified on Etherscan but not Sourcify, so without an Etherscan key they were unreadable. |
| **Seaport** (OpenSea) order signatures | `eth_signTypedData_v4` of `OrderComponents` or `BulkOrder` (Seaport 1.1–1.6; 1.6 on Ethereum, Arbitrum and HyperEVM) | "List BAYC #42 for 9.75 ETH", "Offer 5 WETH for any BAYC", "List 3 items on Seaport": what you give, what you receive, and what else the buyer pays to whom (OpenSea's fee, royalties), with validity and conduit. **Danger** when an order gives items and nothing (or less than a millionth of a token) comes back to you, alone or hidden in a bulk listing: the NFT-drainer pattern. **Caution** when most of the payment goes elsewhere, for "any token" offers (the filler picks which of yours), validity over 180 days or forever, an offerer other than the signer, and a contract that isn't an official Seaport. The digest shown matched Seaport 1.6's own on Ethereum. |
| SeaDrop mints | OpenSea's SeaDrop (Ethereum, Arbitrum, HyperEVM): `mintPublic`, `mintAllowList`, `mintSigned`, `mintAllowedTokenHolder` | "Mint 2 BAYC for 0.02 ETH (SeaDrop)": collection, quantity, the price per NFT (the stage's, or the public drop's read on-chain), fee recipient. **Caution** when the value sent is more than the drop's price, and when the NFTs are minted to someone else. The most-used contract by distinct senders on Ethereum after USDT, USDC and the Universal Router in a 25-block sample. |
| Seaport transactions | `fulfillBasicOrder` (and `_efficient_6GL6yc`), `fulfillOrder`, `fulfillAdvancedOrder`, `fulfillAvailable(Advanced)Orders`, `match(Advanced)Orders`, `cancel`, `validate`, `incrementCounter` | From the sender's side: "Buy BAYC #42 for 10 ETH" (fees included), "Sell BAYC #42 for 4.875 WETH" (accepting an offer, fees taken out), sweeps of several orders, matched buys judged as a whole, the token actually chosen for a collection offer, cancellations ("Cancel all your Seaport orders"). Validating an order publishes it, so it gets the same drainer checks as signing one. **Caution** when the items bought go to another address. Checked on 25 recent Seaport 1.6 transactions on Ethereum. |
| **ERC-4337 UserOperations** | `eth_signTypedData_v4` of a `PackedUserOperation` (EntryPoint v0.8 and v0.9, domain `ERC4337`); `personal_sign` of a v0.6 or v0.7 operation's hash, with the operation supplied ([how](#useroperations-signed-as-a-hash-entrypoint-v06-and-v07)); transactions to an EntryPoint (v0.7–v0.9): `handleOps`, `depositTo`, `withdrawTo` | "Sign a UserOperation: Run 2 calls from your account", with the `callData` decoded as the account's own transaction (smart-account batches included), and the whole operation simulated as the EntryPoint runs it, validation, gas and paymaster included ([how](#useroperations-simulated-as-the-entrypoint-runs-them)); the account setup (already deployed, an EIP-7702 delegation, or a factory deploying it), nonce, gas limits, the most it can cost, and the paymaster that pays. **Caution** for an unknown EntryPoint, and when an EIP-7702 account's operation names a delegate the account doesn't have yet (it would only run with an authorization handing the account to that contract); information when the EntryPoint's nonce for the account is already past the operation's. The digest shown is the one the EntryPoint verifies: it matched v0.8's `getUserOpHash` on Ethereum for a plain account and for an EIP-7702 one (whose typed data, as viem builds it, carries the delegate in place of the `0x7702` marker). A bundler's `handleOps` lists each operation with its calls. |
| **Safe** (Gnosis Safe) multisig | `eth_signTypedData_v4` of a `SafeTx` (an owner's approval), and `execTransaction` | "Safe transaction: Send 1,000 USDC", "Run 18 calls from the Safe": the call (or every call of a MultiSend batch) decoded as the Safe's own transaction and simulated from the Safe; the Safe's own settings spelled out ("Add owner 0xb0b0…0002 (threshold 2)"). **Danger** for a delegatecall to anything but Safe's own libraries (MultiSend, MultiSendCallOnly, SignMessageLib, CreateCall; v1.3.0, v1.4.1 and v1.5.0 addresses from safe-global/safe-deployments; their official migration contracts are a caution): the pattern behind the 2025 Bybit theft, where owners signed a delegatecall that replaced the Safe's code. **Danger** for enabling a module; **caution** for owner and threshold changes, guards, fallback handlers, a gas refund to a named receiver, a signer who isn't an owner, and a call the simulation says fails; information when the nonce is already used. The digest shown matched a real Safe's `getTransactionHash` on Ethereum; 15 recent `execTransaction` calls there all decoded. A plain call (not a delegatecall) to MultiSendCallOnly is a **caution**: its batch would run as the MultiSend contract, not as the Safe (one real operation on Ethereum did this). |
| **Safe ERC-4337 operations** | `eth_signTypedData_v4` of a `SafeOp` (Safe4337Module v0.3.0 for EntryPoint v0.7, v0.2.0 for v0.6; the same addresses on Ethereum, Arbitrum One and HyperEVM, each checked with `SUPPORTED_ENTRYPOINT()`) | "Safe operation: Send 36,090 USDC": the module runs the operation's call with `execTransactionFromModule`, as the Safe itself, so the call is read exactly as a Safe transaction (batches, delegatecall dangers), and the whole operation is simulated through the EntryPoint and the module with the owners' approvals standing in for their signatures, the Safe's configuration compared before and after, with the validity window, gas limits, the most it can cost and the paymaster. **Caution** for a module that isn't Safe's, an `entryPoint` the module doesn't use (the signature wouldn't validate), and callData other than `executeUserOp(WithErrorString)` (the module rejects it); information when the Safe hasn't enabled the module, the operation has expired, or the EntryPoint's nonce is past it. Safe4337MultiChainSignatureModule (EntryPoint v0.9, verified on Sourcify) is read the same way; its `MerkleTreeRoot` signature, one signature for Safe operations on several chains that the message doesn't show, is **danger** unless the wallet supplies those operations ([below](#multi-chain-safe-signatures)). Checked on real operations: 12 v0.3.0 ones on Ethereum and 9 v0.2.0 ones on Arbitrum, rebuilt as a wallet shows them. All 21 digests equal the module's `getOperationHash`, and all 20 plain ECDSA signatures recover to an owner of their Safe. Replayed at the block before each ran, the 12 Ethereum ones simulate without a revert, with the token amounts each receipt shows leaving the Safe (apart from token paymasters' gas fees, taken under the approval the preview shows). |
| **ZeroDev Kernel session keys** | `eth_signTypedData_v4` of Kernel v3's `Enable` (domain `Kernel`, 0.3.0-beta to 0.3.3): the account owner's approval of a session key (a permission: a signer and its policies) or another validator for the account | "Let session key 0xa538…7b56 act for your Kernel account, without limits", "Let session key 0x7fee…7c02 act for your Kernel account: 2 allowed calls": who holds the key (an ordinary key, a passkey, guardians), each of ZeroDev's policies in plain words (the calls it may make, with their arguments and value; its time window, rate and gas limits; who may check its signatures), the account function it may use, any hook or handler it installs, and whether it can also sign as the account. **Danger** when it can send any transaction from the account (a sudo policy, a validator for `execute` with no hook, or only policies and hooks this previewer can't read), when an allowed call is a delegatecall or reaches the account's own `execute` or admin functions (on the account, or on any contract), when its function is `executeUserOp`, and when it installs a handler that runs as the account, may become an executor, or anyone can call; **caution** otherwise ([details](#kernel-session-keys-enable-signatures)). |
| Smart-account batches | A call to your own account (an EIP-7702 delegated account or smart wallet): ERC-7579 / ERC-7821 `execute(bytes32,bytes)` (single, batch, batch with opData, delegatecall; "try" mode), `executeBatch((address,uint256,bytes)[])`, `execute(address,uint256,bytes)`, and the older parallel-array `executeBatch(address[],bytes[])` and `executeBatch(address[],uint256[],bytes[])` (SimpleAccount for v0.6, LightAccount) | "Run 3 calls from your account", with each call decoded as its own transaction from the account ("Allow Permit2 to spend unlimited DAI", "Swap 1,000 DAI for at least 994.999 USDC") and its warnings numbered. **Danger** for a delegatecall, which runs another contract's code inside the account. |
| **EIP-7702** account delegation | `authorizationList` in `eth_sendTransaction` (type-4), unsigned or signed | Which account is delegated to which contract (named when its source is verified), on which chain (`chainId` 0 = every chain). **Danger** when your own account would run another contract's code; information for authorizations signed by other accounts (a sponsor relaying them) and for revocations. The transaction is simulated with the delegations applied, so calls to a delegated account run its new code; not on HyperEVM, which has no EIP-7702. A call to your own account in the same transaction (a wallet upgrading the account and running a batch through it) is decoded against the new delegate's verified ABI. "Delegate your account to X" when nothing else happens. |
| Batches | `wallet_sendCalls` (EIP-5792) | Each call previewed, with warnings merged. The calls are simulated **in order in one block**, so "approve, then swap" isn't shown as failing, and balance changes are netted across the batch. **Danger** when a call in an all-or-nothing (`atomicRequired`) batch would fail, since then none take effect; **caution** for a non-atomic batch, where the other calls may still go through. A custom simulator without `simulateBatch` simulates only the first call and says why. |
| Uniswap **Universal Router** | `execute(commands, inputs[, deadline])` and `executeSigned` on Uniswap's official routers: 2.1.1, 2.1.2 and 2.2.0 (current), 2.0 (Uniswap v4), 1.2 and the original 1.0, on Ethereum, Arbitrum, HyperEVM, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain | One headline for the trade ("Swap 1 ETH for at least 3,150 USDC"), summing split routes and following chained hops through wrap/unwrap, then every command as a step: v2/v3/v4 swaps with fee tiers and per-hop minimum prices, Permit2 permits and transfers, wrap/unwrap, sweep, pay, fee portions, balance checks, sub-plans, v3/v4 position-manager calls, **Across bridging** ("…, then bridge to Base"), and the NFT-marketplace purchases of 1.2 and 1.0 (1.0's LooksRare, X2Y2 and Foundation purchases name the NFT and who gets it). `executeSigned` shows who signed the route. **Caution** when output goes to another address (on this chain or the bridge's destination), when nothing limits slippage (minimum received 0 and no per-hop prices), when the deadline has passed, or for a Permit2 allowance to an address other than the router; **danger** for a fee portion of 10% or more. Command tables differ between releases (0x10 is a Seaport purchase on 1.2 and a v4 swap now; 2.1 adds fields to every swap), so only known router addresses are decoded, each with its release's table: 1.0's was read from its verified source (it masks commands with `0x1f`, and `0x0e` is a placeholder there). 1.0 is little used now; on its traffic from early 2023 (300 Ethereum and 125 Arbitrum transactions) every transaction decoded with no unknown command, and on Arbitrum, whose RPC still serves those receipts, the headline's input and output tokens matched the receipts in 70 of 70 and 53 of 53. |
| Any other contract | calldata to a contract with a verified ABI (Sourcify, Etherscan or your own), including through proxies | Contract name, function and named arguments. Addresses are labelled ("you", token symbols) and amounts are shown in the right token's units. **Caution** for ownership, upgrade and role changes. See [Contract and token lookups](#contract-and-token-lookups). |
| | no verified ABI, but a known selector | The function from the built-in list or a signature database, marked **unverified** (`partial`). The match must re-encode to exactly the signed calldata, and the most trusted source decides when several fit (see below). |
| Plain-text calldata | data that is entirely printable UTF-8: `data:` URI inscriptions (Ethscriptions and similar), on-chain messages | The text, or for a JSON inscription its protocol operation ("Inscription: edmt emt-batch-transfer enat"), noting that an off-chain indexer decides its effect. Invisible and text-reordering characters are removed and flagged. Full when the recipient has no code; **caution** when it's a contract (it receives the text as input) or can't be checked. |
| Anything else | unknown calldata | `opaque`, with the selector shown. Simulated balance changes are added when a simulator is configured. |
| Simulated effects (any transaction) | `eth_simulateV1` logs and state; on HyperEVM, a local EVM over the chain's state ([how](#contract-and-token-lookups)) | Balance changes for native, ERC-20, **ERC-721 and ERC-1155** (e.g. "+1 BAYC #42"). Amounts are the balance differences read from state before and after each call, for every token the events or calldata mention, so share-based tokens are exact and wrapping ETH shows the WETH received (WETH emits `Deposit`, not `Transfer`). The native coin's balance is read too: OP Mainnet's, Base's and BNB Chain's nodes log no transfer for a `SELFDESTRUCT`, so an account delegated (EIP-7702) to code that selfdestructs lost its whole balance with nothing shown (measured 2026-11-12: 1 ETH gone, no log; now 1 ETH out). The gas a priced run's sender pays is left out of the change, as the network fee it is. **Permissions left in place**: ERC-20 and Permit2 allowances, single-NFT approvals and `setApprovalForAll`, read back from the tokens after the simulated transaction. One set *along the way* by another call (a router writing a Permit2 allowance, a smart account's `execute`) is flagged: unlimited allowances and single NFTs with **caution**, all-NFT approvals with **danger**. |

### Hyperliquid L1 actions: the one real limit

For orders, cancels and leverage changes, the wallet only signs the hash of the action. **No previewer can recover the action from the signing request alone.** The Hyperliquid web app avoids wallet prompts for these by approving a local agent key once (`ApproveAgent`, fully decoded here). So when one of these hashes reaches the user's main wallet, the default preview says the contents are hidden and advises caution.

A dApp or integrator that holds the action can pass it as `context.hyperliquid = { action, nonce, vaultAddress?, expiresAfter? }`. The previewer re-hashes it and decodes it **only if** the hash equals the signed `connectionId`. Otherwise it reports a **danger** mismatch.

### UserOperations signed as a hash (EntryPoint v0.6 and v0.7)

Smart accounts for EntryPoint v0.6 and v0.7 (SimpleAccount, LightAccount, Kernel, Biconomy and most others) have their owner sign the operation's bare hash: `personal_sign` of `getUserOpHash`, 32 bytes that say nothing. The wallet or SDK building the operation holds it, so it can pass it as `context.userOperation = { entryPoint, chainId?, operation }` (to the HTTP API, or from the interceptor's per-request `context` option), in the form bundlers' RPC carries it (v0.6 fields; v0.7 fields unpacked as `factory`/`paymaster…`, or packed). The previewer hashes it as that EntryPoint does and decodes it **only if** the hash equals the message being signed: "Sign a UserOperation: Send 15 USDC", read the same way as a v0.8 operation (calls, setup, gas, paymaster, simulation as the account). A different hash is a **danger** mismatch. The hashing was checked against the EntryPoints' own `getUserOpHash` on 30 recent v0.7 operations on Ethereum and 26 v0.6 ones on Arbitrum, all equal. For the 15 of those accounts with an `owner()`, the real signature was a `personal_sign` of exactly that hash by the owner.

### UserOperations, simulated as the EntryPoint runs them

An ERC-4337 operation (typed v0.8/v0.9, hash-signed v0.6/v0.7, or a `SafeOp`) is simulated whole, the way its EntryPoint runs it. The EntryPoint's code is replaced, by a state override, with the simulation code bundlers use: eth-infinitism's `EntryPointSimulations` for v0.7, v0.8 and v0.9, and for v0.6 its own `EntryPoint` with `simulateHandleOp` returning its result instead of reverting (a revert discards the operation's logs). `npm run build:entrypoints` (`scripts/build-entrypoint-simulations.ts`) builds them into `src/entrypoint-simulations.ts`, each with the compiler and settings that reproduce its deployed EntryPoint byte for byte (solc 0.8.17, 0.8.23, 0.8.28 and 0.8.28, all via IR at 1,000,000 runs), so an operation uses the gas the chain will charge it. It fetches its sources and compilers itself, pinned: the npm packages by their registry integrity, the Sourcify sources by a hash. CI rebuilds them with `--check` and fails if the committed file differs. v0.9's sources are the deployed contract's own, verified on Sourcify: its last published package, 0.9.0-rc.1, differs from what was deployed. The storage (deposits, nonces) and the SenderCreator stay the EntryPoint's own. A code override runs no constructor, so the override also sets what the EntryPoint's constructor would have: the EIP-712 name and version in the code (v0.8 on), and in storage the two things `EntryPointSimulations` otherwise sets only when a simulation starts, its SenderCreator and (v0.8 on) the EIP-712 domain separator operations are hashed with. An earlier bundle replayed in the same block, a plain `handleOps`, then runs as it did on-chain: before this, every v0.8 or v0.9 bundle ahead of the operation hashed with a zero domain and failed its real signatures. The larger replay samples found it: an operation whose USDC an earlier bundle in its block had already spent showed as succeeding. Checked: through the override, `getUserOpHash`, `senderCreator()` and `eip712Domain()` answer as the deployed EntryPoints do, on Ethereum and Arbitrum One. Two things in the simulation code differ from what bundlers run, and neither changes an operation's gas. Its `depositTo` doesn't waste gas on purpose. Its simulation-only check that the paymaster has code skips a paymaster field naming address 0, which the EntryPoint itself takes as no paymaster (such operations are included on-chain). `simulateHandleOp` then does what `handleOps` does for one operation: deploys the account through its factory if it's new, runs the account's `validateUserOp` (with the nonce and the prefund) and the paymaster's `validatePaymasterUserOp` (with its deposit), runs the calls (`executeUserOp` included) and charges for gas, the paymaster's `postOp` included.
- "Gas cost (simulated)": what the operation pays and who pays it (the account's deposit or the paymaster's), priced at the latest base fee. The base fee is passed as a block override: `eth_simulateV1` without validation otherwise runs at 0, and a token paymaster would charge nothing. The prefund the account sends the EntryPoint during validation is gas, so it isn't a balance change; a token paymaster's charge is, and is also shown as "Paymaster charge". The native coin the balance changes list is what the operation's calls send and receive, with the account's EntryPoint deposit counted as its own coin (the gas is taken from it, what's left of the prefund stays in it, and only the account can withdraw it): its balance and its deposit are read before and after the run, and what the two together changed by, plus the gas the account paid, is listed. Neither the account's balance nor the transfers a node reports tell the prefund apart from what the calls send (a local EVM reads the balance whole; `eth_simulateV1`'s transfer per call frame shows the prefund as the account sending the EntryPoint coin, as a deposit its calls make does): before, the local EVM's runs (HyperEVM's, and any chain's without `eth_simulateV1`) netted the two and dropped them together, so a send or a wrap of the native coin was listed only as asked for, and coin received was listed less the prefund. Coin the calls give someone else from the deposit (`withdrawTo` another address, `depositTo` another account) is listed as sent, which no event showed before; coin they keep in it (`depositTo` the account itself, `withdrawTo` it back) isn't. An account that is its own paymaster pays the gas from its own deposit, which is gas as it is without a paymaster. Its stake (`addStake`) isn't its deposit: it stays locked until `unlockStake` and a delay after it, so staking is listed as the coin leaving the account and taking the stake back as the coin coming in. A stake withdrawn to any other address (`withdrawStake`) moves neither the account's balance nor its deposit: it is listed as sent, from the EntryPoint's `StakeWithdrawn` event. The deposit is read in the lean pass too (BNB Chain's node, which splits the block's gas among the reads), which gives the run at least the operation's own limits: given half as much again as it used and 100,000, an operation with a call gas limit of 200,000 that used far less was refused by the EntryPoint (AA95, its gas left under its limits), and its deposit went unread. An operation previewed by its calls alone has its deposit counted the same way, with no gas (none is paid in that run): a deposit for itself isn't listed, a drain of it is, and the deposit is never listed as a token. Where the balances couldn't be read back (amounts from events), the coin moving between the account and the EntryPoint is left out, as the deposit's, but the deposit withdrawn to any other address is listed as sent, from the EntryPoint's `Withdrawn` event (no transfer of the account's shows it: the EntryPoint pays it), as a stake is; a deposit given to another account (`depositTo`) no event tells apart from the prefund, and it is still left out there. "Gas used (simulated)" is the gas the EntryPoint counts for the operation (`UserOperationEvent`'s `actualGasUsed`, `preVerificationGas` included): what a gasless operation, which pays nothing, still costs whoever sends it.
- **Caution** when bundlers would reject the operation, with the EntryPoint's reason ("the paymaster's EntryPoint deposit can't cover the gas (AA31 paymaster deposit too low)"; a nonce or prefund problem the checks above already name isn't repeated, and a nonce ahead of the account's only waits); when the paymaster's signature doesn't check out or its sponsorship has expired; when the account's own validity window has passed; when the calls fail (the operation is still included and pays for its gas); when the paymaster's `postOp` fails; when the operation installs an ERC-7579 executor module on the account (`userop_installs_executor`: an executor can have the account make calls with no signature from its owners), or a validator module (`userop_installs_validator`: whoever it names can sign the account's operations), but for the one validator of an account the operation deploys whose validation took the stand-in as the signer's (its root, the signer's key). A typed operation's `bytes` field given as odd-length hex is described as wallets sign it, with a 0 in front (viem and MetaMask do; `userop_odd_length_hex`); one that isn't hex at all isn't described past its account and nonce, nor simulated (`userop_unreadable`); and with no chain in its domain, its calls aren't decoded (`userop_no_chain`), not said to be none. The modules a whole run installs (the `ModuleInstalled` events the account logs, from its factory's setup or its calls) are listed as "Modules installed (simulated)".
- An operation with no calls (empty `callData`) is simulated whole too: the account's validation, its deployment where it has a factory, any paymaster's charge and the gas; its preview says what it does ("deploy 0x7622…3ece: it deploys the account through its factory … and makes no calls after: what the factory sets up in it (its owners, validators and any modules) controls the account from then on", or that it only runs the account's validation, uses up its nonce and pays for its gas). It used to be shown as "makes no calls", nothing simulated. Most are accounts deployed and nothing else: on BNB Chain, Kernel v3.1 accounts through ZeroDev's FactoryStaker (gasless, their root ZeroDev's ECDSA validator), 4 of 200 v0.7 operations in a survey of 2026-11-15 and 4 of 819 in 3,000 blocks after it (and of BNB Chain's v0.6, 2 of 201 in 20,000 blocks, one with no factory either); none of 1,076 Ethereum, 14,572 Polygon, 127 Arbitrum One and 248 OP Mainnet v0.7 operations and 852 Base v0.6 ones (300 to 3,000 blocks each). Replayed against their receipts on BNB Chain (2026-11-15), 7 deploy-only operations went from not simulated to run whole with the stand-in validated, gas 0.09–0.14% high (about what the replaced `ecrecover` adds where the ECDSA validator's first try, over the bare hash, passes through it), and the v0.6 operation with no factory (an account sponsored by a paymaster) from not simulated to validated, gas exact; the other 107 of 109 BNB Chain v0.7 operations replayed before and after came out the same, operation by operation. Where one can't run whole, its deployment alone is simulated (the EntryPoint doesn't call an account with no `callData`).
- The owner's signature doesn't exist yet. The simulation gives the operation a stand-in signature and replaces the `ecrecover` precompile (moved with `eth_simulateV1`'s `movePrecompileToAddress`; the fork simulator does the same) with code that recovers the stand-in as the signer, over the digest the signer's wallet will sign (the `personal_sign` form of the operation's hash, or the typed data's digest), and passes every other input to the real precompile. An account whose owner is signing validates the operation as it will once signed: accounts that only run calls they validated in the same transaction run as they will, an account that tries other forms of the hash first (Kernel's ECDSA validator) fails on those as it will, and a paymaster's own signature is checked for real. The stand-in costs exactly the precompile's 3,000 gas (on HyperEVM, which charges 15,000 for it, 15,000); any other signature passes through for about 200 gas more, which can't be given back. If the account checks a digest of its own, the stand-in is accepted over any.
- The stand-in takes the shape of accounts that wrap their owner's signature: Coinbase Smart Wallet (the signer's index among its owners, read from the account or, while its factory deploys it, from `createAccount`'s owners; and builds of it other wallets deploy through factories of their own, which no address names: taken for Coinbase's when their implementation answers as Coinbase Smart Wallet v1 does, its EIP-712 name and version, its replayable nonce key 8453 and the operation's EntryPoint, and the account is checked to run it: its code Solady's ERC-1967 proxy, as Coinbase's accounts are, or, while a factory whose `implementation()` is such a build deploys it, its address the one the factory's CREATE2 gives that proxy to the build with Coinbase's salt over the call's first two arguments, the owners and nonce, which only that proxy's creation code can produce (what a factory or a storage slot names isn't taken on its word); their owners read from the account or from that call, and the preview saying the implementation isn't Coinbase's own), ZeroDev Kernel v2.3, v2.4, v3.1 and its EIP-7702 delegate (v3.1 and the delegate by what validates the operation; the delegate's root is the account's own key, which its nonce names or else its root validator: an ECDSA validator's signature as it is or after `0xff`, as a shape may list several, tried in turn; a passkey's, below; and a permission whose signer is one of Bleap's cosigning modules (V2, or V1, the root its new accounts are deployed with), `0xff` and then the owner's stand-in and a second one, which the replaced `ecrecover` attributes to Bleap's cosigner, each recovered for the precompile's 3,000 gas; asked by anyone but the owner the module records for the account, the preview names that owner), Alchemy's Modular Account v2 (its implementations, two EIP-7702 delegates, and accounts its factory deploys in the operation, all with its `0xff00` prefix, but where the operation's nonce names a validation through Aave Labs' 2-of-2 multisig module: below), Alchemy's LightAccount (v2, and the later implementation used with EntryPoint v0.8) and MultiOwnerLightAccount v2, whose signatures carry a `0x00` type byte for an EOA owner (accounts their factories deploy in the operation too), and Kresus's KresusVault, a LightAccount build in the same form (`0xf9c0…c4d0` and its factory `0x5943…7017`, verified on Sourcify, Ethereum and Base; from the scheduled run of 2026-11-18, where a Base account the factory deployed in its operation reverted on the plain stand-in with InvalidSignatureType, replayed after: validated, gas within 0.1%), Biconomy Smart Account v2 (its own proxy's implementation slot read, or, while its factory deploys it with the ECDSA ownership module, that module; the module's signature and its ECDSA ownership module, abi-encoded), and Biconomy Nexus 1.2 when its default validator, K1MeeValidator, validates the operation (below). A wallet that knows its account's signature better can pass its own stub, the one it gives bundlers for gas estimation (`getStubSignature` in permissionless.js and ZeroDev's SDK, `getDummySignature` in Alchemy's aa-sdk): as the `signature` of `context.userOperation`'s operation. For a hash-signed operation (v0.6, v0.7) it is part of the operation supplied anyway; for a typed one (v0.8, v0.9) the typed data decodes on its own, and `context.userOperation = { entryPoint, operation: { sender, nonce, signature } }` only supplies the stub, used when the EntryPoint, sender and nonce (and callData, if given) are the typed operation's. The stub then goes to the account in place of the stand-in. The replaced `ecrecover` passes a stub it doesn't recognise to the real precompile, so a stub signed by a dummy key validates as it does in gas estimation: the account returns a signature failure rather than reverting. Where it does, the stub is run again with the stand-in in place of the ECDSA signature it carries (65 bytes, v 27 or 28, wherever it sits: after a type byte, before a mode byte, inside an ABI wrapper), and that run is taken where the account accepts it: the account reads the form its owner's signature will have and runs that path, the gas included (the request is what the signer signs; a stub the account refuses says nothing of it). Refused that way too, the stand-ins of the account's own form are tried, as for an operation with no stub, and the first the account accepts is taken (the preview says the wallet's stub was refused); only where none gets further does the preview say the account doesn't accept the stub as signed. A stub had left the preview worse off than none: on Base (2026-11-18), operations previewed with their own signatures' ECDSA parts dummied reverted in validation (`Panic(0x41)`, `InvalidSignature`) or were refused, 35–38% low, where with no stub their stand-ins validated; each now validates (within 0.05% and 0.5%). Measured with `npm run accuracy:4337 -- --wallet-stub`, which previews each operation with its own signature, the ECDSA signatures in it replaced by a dummy: on Sepolia, 51 of 51 operations so signed took the stand-in in their wallet's form, gas within 0.05% for 56 of 60 operations (the bare stand-in, on an earlier sample: 35 of 39, and one 9% low, an account whose wallet wraps its owner's signature in a type byte and an ABI encoding that costs 29,000 gas more to read than the bare signature it also takes).
- Passkeys: when a Coinbase Smart Wallet itself is asked to sign and it has a passkey owner, the stand-in is a WebAuthn assertion (webauthn-sol's checks: type `webauthn.get`, the operation's hash as the challenge, read from the EntryPoint's `getUserOpHash`; user present and verified), and the P256 verifier at 0x100 (RIP-7212 on Arbitrum, EIP-7951 on Ethereum) is replaced the same way, accepting that assertion and passing anything else to the real one. Kernel accounts whose validator is Paribu's passkey validator get the same webauthn-sol assertion. ZeroDev Kernel v3.1 accounts whose validator (the one their nonce names, else their root validator) is ZeroDev's WebAuthn validator get that validator's form of the assertion (`abi.encode(authenticatorData, clientDataJSON, responseTypeLocation, r, s, usePrecompiled)`, with the type and challenge where it looks for them), whoever is signing: a passkey is the account's only key. `usePrecompiled` is as the account's own last operation had it (looked for in its last 100,000 blocks, then, where the node answers that in one read, the 10,000,000 before: Arbitrum One's public node does), else as most accounts sign on its chain (below). Daimo's verifier gets a stand-in too: its code moves to another address, every input still runs through it (so verification costs what a real one does), and the stand-in's answer is 1. Checked on a live Kernel passkey account on Arbitrum at the latest state: validation accepts the stand-in on both paths. Through Daimo's verifier, the operation's gas comes within 3% of what the account's own signature costs there (the verifier's cost depends on the signature). Wallets differ, though: of 7 such accounts on Arbitrum, 5 signed with the precompile and 2 with Daimo's verifier, which costs about 320,000 gas more. A wallet that signs through Daimo's verifier on a chain with the precompile can pass its stub to get its gas right.
- OKX Wallet's smart account names the signing key in its signature. Its source isn't published; its checks were read from real operations, run in a local EVM while watching what the account hashes and calls. It keeps its keys as Uniswap's Calibur does, by hash, each with settings (an admin flag, an expiry, a hook), but stores only the hash and a type: an Ethereum key (the hash of its address) or a passkey (the hash of its public key). The signature carries the rest: the key's hash, a `validUntil`, then an Ethereum key's signature, or a passkey's public key, WebAuthn assertion and an optional Merkle proof (one signature for operations on several chains), over `keccak256(abi.encode(operation hash, validUntil, implementation))`. It runs its calls through `executeUserOp`, which reads the key's hash back out of the signature and applies that key's settings: only an admin key may make the account call itself. A key's type can also be a contract that checks its signatures: OKX's passkey verifier (`0xa921…9fe8`, an ERC-1967 proxy at that address on every chain here; unpublished, its checks read from an operation's validation in a local EVM) takes the key's hash, the account's digest and `abi.encode(passkey, cosignature)`: the passkey's part as a passkey key's, whose public key it hashes against the key's hash and whose assertion it checks through P256VERIFY, and an ECDSA signature over the same digest by a key it records for each account. Accounts behind OKX's longer proxy (797 bytes, the same code on OP Mainnet and Ethereum, known by its hash: its initializer, then a delegatecall to what its ERC-1967 slot names) are followed to the implementation their slot names (before, only proxies up to 199 bytes were, and those accounts got the plain stand-in, which they refused); other longer code isn't, whatever its slot holds.
  - With the bare stand-in, which names no key, first operations that add an owner failed in the preview (`NonAdminSelfCall`) where the signed ones succeeded: every outcome the larger runs below got wrong (20 on BNB Chain, 8 on Base, 3 on Ethereum).
  - The stand-in now has the account's form. If the signer's address is one of the account's Ethereum keys, it is that key's. Otherwise the real key can't stand (a passkey's public key exists only in its signatures), so a stand-in key is registered for the run with the type and settings of the key most likely to sign (a passkey before an Ethereum key, an admin before others, unexpired before expired; a passkey only when the account itself is asked to sign, a passkey wallet's way: asked by an address that is neither the account nor one of its Ethereum keys, where that key is a passkey, the stand-in is the passkey's real key, which the account refuses, and the preview says the account is signed by its passkey): in the account's storage, or, for an account the operation deploys (a node empties a new account's storage), through the account's own initializer, by code at the implementation's address that hands `initialize` the stand-in key's hash in the real key's place and passes every call on to the real code, moved aside. Validation then runs every check it runs on a real signature, and the calls run with that key's permissions.
  - Replayed on BNB Chain as blocks arrived, 37 such operations in two runs: every outcome right; with an Ethereum key the gas within 0.04% of the receipt's, with a passkey within 0.05% (one run a second late, 2.2% high), and for accounts deployed in the operation 0.11–0.16% high (the extra hop to the moved code, about 1,100 gas). Four of the Base operations the scheduled run had got wrong, replayed again: outcomes right, gas 0.16% high.
  - A key of OKX's passkey verifier's type gets the passkey key's stand-in (a stand-in key of that type and the key's settings) with a second stand-in as the cosignature, which the replaced `ecrecover` recovers as the key the verifier records for the account; the preview says the operation validates only once that key cosigns it too. Asked by that cosigner or by anyone else but the account (and its Ethereum keys), the stand-in is refused, and the preview says the account is signed by its passkey with that cosignature (the cosigner's alone being half of it). Only while the verifier runs the code its checks were read from (`0x47f0…a29a`) and records a key for the account; otherwise the stand-in is the last resort's (the key's real hash, in the form the verifier's known code takes), and the preview says why (the verifier's code isn't that, or it records no cosigner for the account) and that the refusal needn't mean the passkey can't sign; the account's other keys aren't stood in for in its place.
  - The account answers a signature it doesn't accept not with 1 (`SIG_VALIDATION_FAILED`) but with a value the EntryPoint reads as an aggregator. `handleOps` rejects that too ("AA24 signature error"), so validation data naming an aggregator now counts as a signature the account doesn't accept; before, such a stand-in was reported as validated.
- An EIP-7702 delegate (`0x13e0…9a5a`, and a second deployment: below) that only EntryPoint v0.6 may call takes Biconomy Smart Account v2's calls and signature form, abi.encode(the module's signature, a module), but checks the signature itself and never calls the module its operations name ("Multichain PassKeys Ownership Registry Module" 0.2.0). Its source isn't published, nor its registry's or module's; it was read from its code, the same on Ethereum, BNB Chain, Base and Arbitrum One. The module's signature carries two, and validation needs both: a cosignature over the operation's hash, which a registry contract checks against its verifying signers, and the account's own key's signature in the form of Biconomy's MultichainECDSAValidator, over the root of a Merkle tree with `keccak256(validUntil, validAfter, operation hash)` among its leaves (one signature for operations on several chains). Against the bare stand-in its validation reverted (AA23), so only its calls were simulated. The stand-in now has its form: the cosignature recovered as the verifying signer that cosigned every operation seen, and the account's own over a tree of two leaves, the operation's and another, as most of its operations are signed.
  - How often, through EntryPoint v0.6: on BNB Chain 58 of 1,951 operations in 25 hours (18 accounts) and on Ethereum 5 of 4,268 in 32 hours (one account), 2026-11-13 to 15; in an earlier scan, 68 of 2,610 on BNB Chain in 37 hours and 2 of 6,920 on Arbitrum One in 49 hours (2026-11-11 to 13), and 2 of 44,417 on Base in 8 hours (2026-11-13). None through v0.7 (of 65,192 on BNB Chain and 26,708 on Ethereum in the same hours), and it isn't deployed on Polygon. The 63 on BNB Chain and Ethereum were all cosigned by the same signer and sponsored by the same paymaster; their trees held the operation and one other (39), the operation alone (21), or two or three others (3).
  - Against their real signatures, run again on the latest state (the nonce and the paymaster's single-use mark reset; the latest operation of each of the 18 BNB Chain accounts, and the 5 on Ethereum, as sent by their own bundlers): before, every one reverted (AA23); now the stand-in validated for all 23, every outcome the real signature's. Its gas is 30–40 above the signed operation's for the 13 signed with one other, 390–431 above for the 8 signed alone, and 311–321 below for the 2 signed with two or three others: each element of the proof costs about 355 gas, at most 0.25% of these operations'. (The run with the real signatures pays 198 gas more for each of its two passing through the replaced `ecrecover` to the real precompile: counted out.)
  - Replayed against their receipts on the state they met, on BNB Chain as blocks arrived (2026-11-15; none came on the other chains while watched), 5 operations: before, each ran its calls alone (AA23), and two of those showed the calls failing where they had succeeded (one a swap, shown moving nothing); now each ran whole, the stand-in validated, every outcome right and the token movements exact where there were any (one within 2 base units). The gas paid against the receipt's, with the operation's real signature in the stand-in's place in brackets: at their block's own time 0.042% (0.040%) and 0.285% (0.289%) above; run a second after their block (BNB Chain's node won't simulate a block in its parent's second) 0.049% (0.07%), 0.169% (0.308%) and 25% (25.04%). That last bundle, replayed at its own time in the local EVM, matches its receipt: the 25% comes from running it a second late, with either signature, not from the stand-in.
  - A second deployment, `0x6640…831c` (another compiler's code, the same at its address on all six chains here), works alike, with a registry of its own (`0x82ea…e4dc`, the same on all six), which the delegate names (an unnamed getter, `0xa600845b`) and which takes the cosignature of its one verifying signer (`getVerifyingSigner()`), unless its owner switches it to take or refuse every one (a mode kept beside the signer, read by the unnamed `0x295a5212`); its operations name another module (`0xba41…967b`, the first's code with that registry in it), which it doesn't call either. It had no stand-in of its form: its validation reverted on the bare one (AA23, no revert data), so only its calls were simulated. On BNB Chain, 6 of 372 EntryPoint v0.6 operations in 40,000 blocks (about 5 hours, 4 accounts, 2026-11-15; the survey's scan before it, 8–9 of 485 from 5–6 accounts, 1.9%), each cosigned by its registry's signer, sponsored by `0xdeaa…34df` and signed alone, its tree the operation's leaf with no proof. The stand-in now has its form: the cosignature recovered as the signer the registry answers, read from the chain as the registry and its mode are (where they can't be read, the preview says the refusal needn't mean the account won't take the signer's), and the account's own over a tree of the operation alone. Asked by any key but the account's own, the preview says it takes the account's, whatever its registry does; with the registry switched to refuse every cosignature, or with no verifying signer, that it takes no operation, whoever signs, until the registry's owner changes that. A cosignature its registry refuses, this delegate pays nothing toward its gas for (it returns before paying its prefund, where it pays for an owner's signature it refuses), so without a paymaster the EntryPoint rejects the operation as unpaid (AA21): where the account can pay, the preview says that is the signature refused (the stand-in, or the wallet's signature), not the account's funds, and simulates the calls alone.
    - The survey's two examples on BNB Chain (`0xfb9f…9091`, `0x45cf…b21b`), whose blocks' state the node no longer serves, previewed on the latest state with their nonce and the paymaster's single-use mark set back: before, each reverted on the bare stand-in (AA23) and ran its calls alone; now each ran whole with the stand-in validated, the paymaster's validation too, and the second moved what its receipt did (19.663206529076146564 USDT out). Against each operation's own signature on the same state, the stand-in's gas is within 25 of it (the signed run's two signatures pass through the replaced `ecrecover`, 371 gas more in all, counted out); a tree with a proof element, as the first deployment's stand-in has, would cost about 355 more. Replayed against its receipt on the state it met, the one operation of the delegate that came while BNB Chain was watched (1 h 40 min, 2026-11-15; none in the 3 hours before), another account's (`0x49ac…147e`), with the code before and after: before, AA23 and its calls alone; now whole, the stand-in validated, the outcome right, the token movements exact (14.472349178320438515 USDT out) and the gas paid 0.079% (153 gas) above the receipt's, run a second after its block (above). The same runs replayed 9 of the first deployment's operations and 91 other BNB Chain v0.6 operations: each the same before and after.
- Alchemy's Modular Account v2 accounts validating through Aave Labs' `MultiSigValidationModule` (`0xcbec…a3e6`, `aave-labs.multi-sig-validation-module.1.0.0`, verified on Sourcify, Arbitrum One). The operation's nonce names the validation: below its options byte, the entity ID, whose module the account keeps in its own storage (read there), where the account runs Modular Account v2's code: delegating to its EIP-7702 delegate, or with the ERC-1967 proxy its factory deploys (Solady's; all 19 accounts validating so among 47 sampled on Arbitrum One have it), whose implementation slot is read (an account of other code with that slot, storage and module records may validate any way at all, and gets the `0xff00` form as before, with nothing said of a second signer). This one is 2-of-2: its two signers for the account (`getSigners(entityId, account)`) each sign the operation's hash as a message, and the signature is `0xff` and their two 65-byte signatures, 130 bytes, or the module reverts (`InvalidMultisigSignatureLength`). The `0xff00` stand-in is 66 bytes, so the account's validation reverted (AA23) and only its calls were simulated. On Arbitrum One, 123 operations of 95 accounts in a day (2026-11-15, all sponsored by `0x8868…35d3`; 0.87% of the survey's Arbitrum One v0.7 sample), each account's signers its own key and one key all 95 share. The stand-in is now the signer's in its place among the two (in `getSigners`' order, the order their operations are signed in: the module takes either, the other for about 170 gas more) and the other signer's, which the replaced `ecrecover` attributes to that key, each for the precompile's price. The preview says that the signer's key is one of the account's 2 signers and that the other, which it names, must sign the operation too before any bundler can include it. Asked by a key that is neither, the stand-in is refused and the preview names both. Any other Modular Account v2 validation keeps the `0xff00` form.
  - Replayed against their receipts on the state they met, on Arbitrum One as blocks arrived (2026-11-15), each with the code before and after: 8 operations of 8 accounts. Before, each ran its calls alone (AA23, `InvalidMultisigSignatureLength(66, 130)`); now each ran whole, the stand-in validated, every outcome right, and the gas paid 0.108–0.12% above the receipt's: 195 gas each, about what the paymaster's own ECDSA signature pays passing through the replaced `ecrecover` (above), not the stand-in, which costs what the two real signatures do (checked in the tests). None of the eight moved tokens. The same runs replayed 26 operations of Modular Account v2 accounts validating otherwise (most through Alchemy's SingleSignerValidationModule, the `0xff00` form) and 23 other v0.7 operations: each the same before and after. The survey's two examples, whose state the node no longer serves, previewed on the latest state with their nonce set back: before, AA23; now validated, one moving the same tokens as its receipt (4.45 minted, 4.45 burned) for 789,256–789,389 gas against the receipt's 789,515, the other's calls failing on the later state. Run with their own signatures on the same state, each used 338–356 gas more than with the stand-in, about what its two signatures pay passing through the replaced `ecrecover`.
- Send's account (on Base; `SendAccount`, verified on Sourcify, based on Daimo's) keeps passkeys by slot. Its signature is a version byte (`0x01`), a `validUntil`, the key's slot and that key's WebAuthn assertion, whose challenge is the version, `validUntil` and the operation's hash; any other version is refused before any check runs, so the plain stand-in left the passkey check out and its operations came out 11–30% low. The stand-in is that assertion as Send's app makes it (origin `https://send.app`, the authenticator flags its operations carry, the same length as theirs), by the key in slot 0 (else the slot the account's last operation used), valid for a day, accepted by the P256 precompile's stand-in: Send's verifier (behind a proxy, its implementation now unpublished) asks the precompile. As for a Coinbase Smart Wallet's passkey, it is made when the account itself is asked to sign (a passkey wallet's way); asked by an Ethereum key, which isn't one of its keys, the stand-in is the plain one, which the account refuses, and the preview says Send's account is signed by its passkeys.
- Trust Wallet's Barz (a diamond: each account the same 573-byte proxy; its facets verified on Sourcify, at the same addresses on every chain here; EntryPoint v0.6) validates through the facet installed for `validateOwnerSignature`: an Ethereum key's, which takes the plain stand-in, or `Secp256r1VerificationFacet` for a passkey, which takes `abi.encode(r, s, authenticatorData, clientDataJSONPre, clientDataJSONPost)` (the operation's hash, base64url-encoded, between the two) and verifies it in Solidity against the public key it keeps. The plain stand-in failed to decode (`AccountFacet__CallNotSuccessful()`, AA23), so its calls ran alone. With no precompile or verifier to stand in at, the stand-in is a real assertion by a passkey made up for the run, whose public key is set as the account's for it, in the form of the account's last operation's assertion (else Trust Wallet's, origin `https://trustwallet.com`): the account's own check runs whole. Its cost turns on the signature (each pair of bits of its scalars that are both 0, one in sixteen at random, skips about 850 gas), so the stand-in is a signature that skips as many as one does on average. Made when the account itself is asked to sign (a passkey wallet's way); asked by anyone else, the account's own key checks it and refuses it, and the preview says the account is signed by its passkey.
- Session keys' operations through Rhinestone's SmartSession (both deployments; any account whose nonce names it: a Safe through Rhinestone's Safe7579 adapter, which reads its validator from the nonce's top 20 bytes; Biconomy Nexus, after three bytes of its own, the first of which can be `0x01`, and a mode byte; Kernel, but not in enable mode installing it, which names it after the mode byte `0x01` and a type byte: below) are signed by the session's keys, not by the account's owners. The signature is a mode byte (`0x00`: a session the account has enabled), the session's permission ID, then what the session's validator takes; SmartSession runs the session's policies on the operation (for real), then the validator. The plain stand-in's `0xff` is no mode at all: validation reverted (Safe7579 reports a validator's revert as `ExecutionFailed()`, `AA23`), so only the calls were simulated. For a session whose validator is Rhinestone's OwnableValidator (a threshold of its owners' signatures over the hash as a message, in any order) and whose owners include the signer, read from the module (`getPermissionIDs`, `getSessionValidatorAndConfig`), the stand-in names that session and signs as the signer, with the other owners' stand-ins to the threshold, each recovered as its owner for the precompile's price. For a session whose validator is Biconomy's K1MeeValidator (below) and whose key is the signer, the stand-in names that session, in the form of what its key is asked to sign (below). Where the request can't name who signs (the signer asked isn't one of a session's keys, or the session's validator is another), the preview says so: that the account validates through SmartSession, as a session key's operation signed by its session's keys (`2 of 0x7e35…0eea, 0x866c…5dcb`; for K1MeeValidator sessions, the key, or the newest three of an account's keys and how many sessions it has, and that the validator takes the key's signature over the operation's hash or over a supertransaction's hash with the operation's proof), not by its owners, and that the signer asked isn't one of them. At most 1,024 sessions are read (newest first): where an account has more and none of those read is the signer's, the preview says the signer may hold one of the older ones, which weren't read, not that the account won't take its signature.
  - Biconomy's K1MeeValidator 1.0.3 (`0x0000…04fe`, its MEE 2.1.0 deployment; verified on Sourcify on Arbitrum One, Base and OP Mainnet) is the validator of most SmartSession sessions. As a session's validator (`validateSignatureWithData`, which SmartSession calls with the operation's hash and the session's config) its config's first 20 bytes are the session's key, and it takes one of its modes: 65 bytes over the operation's hash (as it is or as a message: no type, the modes' fallback); after a 4-byte type, simple mode (`0x177eee00`: abi.encode of the supertransaction's hash, the root of a Merkle tree of the hashes of its operations, on any chain; the proof of this operation's hash in it, OpenZeppelin's, each pair hashed in order; and the key's signature over the root, as it is or as a message); on-chain mode (`0x177eee01`, a transaction carrying the root that the key signs and sends) or permit mode (`0x177eee02`, an ERC-2612 permit with the root as its deadline). Every session operation seen was in simple mode with one proof element, the root signed as a message: 19 of 19 on Arbitrum One in two hours, 28 of 28 on Base in a day, 164 of 164 on OP Mainnet in 4.4 hours (2026-11-15). That is what Biconomy's SDK (`@biconomy/abstractjs` 2.0.2) has the key sign for MEE before 2.2.1: the supertransaction's hash, as a message, which the MEE node lays out per operation with its proof (from 2.2.1 the key signs the operation's hash itself, for a later validator). Each session has a key of its own (an account on Arbitrum One had 105 sessions and 105 keys), never the account's owner (K1MeeValidator's own owner for the account): a key held for the account apart from its owner's, a dApp's or an agent's, signing a hash that alone names nothing a wallet could check.
