  - The stand-in, when the signer is a session's key, is in the form of what the key is asked to sign, after SmartSession's use mode and that session's permission ID (an account's sessions are read newest first, 32 at a time through Multicall3, until one of the signer's turns up: an account on Arbitrum One had 105, and with only its newest 32 read, the key of its 73rd was told it wasn't one). Asked to sign the operation's hash (`personal_sign` or `eth_sign` of it, with the operation in `context.userOperation`): the plain form, 65 bytes, a stand-in `ecrecover` recovers as the signer over what the wallet signs and over the hash's other form, which the validator tries too, as no one's (as the key's real signature recovers someone else there), each for the precompile's price; the preview notes that the same operation sent through Biconomy's MEE node (simple mode: the key signing a supertransaction's hash, the operation carrying it and its proof) costs about 1,300 gas more to validate (on chain, below). Asked to sign a supertransaction's hash, with its operations ([below](#biconomy-mee-supertransactions)): simple mode, with that root and the operation's proof in it. Asked by anyone else, the same form with the signer's stand-in, which the validator refuses: the operation runs whole, the session's check failing, and the note names the key. In `test/k1mee-session.test.ts`, on the validator's real code: the plain form's run costs what the real signature's does (to within the replaced `ecrecover`'s forwarding of the real one, about 190 gas a call), simple mode with one proof element 2,117 gas more there (in the test's mock accounts, which copy the signature more than real ones: on chain the difference is about 1,300), and in simple mode the stand-in's run costs what a real signature's with the same root and proof does.
  - K1MeeValidator is also the default validator of Biconomy Nexus 1.2 (its implementation `0x0000…50ac`, verified on Sourcify; the validator an immutable of its code, on all six chains), which an operation names with validator 0 in its nonce (three bytes of MEE's, the mode byte `0x00`, then the validator), its owner (the one the account installed it with, `getOwner`: the account itself where none is set, as for an EIP-7702 account) signing for the account: behind the proxy its factory (`0x0000…f837`) deploys, a Smart Account v2 proxy upgraded to it, or as an EIP-7702 delegate (Ethereum). As an account's own validator its simple mode carries the operation's time bounds too: `0x177eee00`, then abi.encode of the supertransaction's hash, the lower and upper bounds, the proof and the owner's signature over the root; the leaf is MEE's hash of the operation's hash and the bounds, and the validator returns the bounds as the operation's validity (validAfter, validUntil). Every one seen (2026-11-15) was in simple mode, the root signed as a message, through Biconomy's MEE node, with 1 to 5 proof elements: on OP Mainnet 127 of 248 v0.7 operations in about 100 minutes, on Arbitrum One 21 of 1,325 in two hours, on Base 4 of 22,764 in a day, on Ethereum 17 of 19,900 in a day (16 of 6 EIP-7702 accounts), on BNB Chain 17 of 12,952 in eight hours (14 of 5 EIP-7702 accounts), on Polygon none in 20,000 blocks; none in on-chain or permit mode. Until then the account got the plain stand-in, signed by whoever the request named, and in simple mode its validator refused it. The stand-in, when its default validator validates the operation: asked to sign a supertransaction's hash with its operations ([below](#biconomy-mee-supertransactions)), simple mode with that root, the operation's proof and its time bounds (supplied without them, its leaf is its hash, which the validator doesn't check: refused, as the real signature would be, and the preview says so), and a note that the owner signs the supertransaction's hash, which the account's own validator checks with the operation's proof and time bounds; asked to sign the operation's hash, the plain form, with a note that the same operation sent through the MEE node costs about 1,700 gas more to validate with one proof element and about 330 more for each further one (operations replayed so against their receipts came out 1,676 to 1,709 gas under with one, 2,366 to 2,397 with three, 2,666 to 2,776 with four). The owner's signature in either is recovered as the signer over what the wallet signs and as no one over the other form, each for the precompile's price; asked by anyone else, the validator refuses it and the preview names the owner (or the owner the factory's set-up installs, for an account being deployed: NexusBootstrap's default-validator data), and asked with no signer named, the preview says the request names none (the stand-in is then the account's own). An account that keeps no owner is signed for only by an EIP-7702 account's own key, the account itself: a contract, its factory's proxy set up through NexusBootstrap without the default validator (initNexus, initNexusScoped, initNexusWithSingleValidator), has no key for its own address, so no signature validates through that validator; there the stand-in asked of the account itself (named or not) is recovered as no one, as any signature would be, and the preview says no signature validates (before, such an account was shown validating for the account itself). Accounts on a later Nexus (1.3.1, `0x0000…7e9d`, the same address on every chain here, verified on Sourcify; 7 operations of 2 accounts in those spans) validate through K1MeeValidator 1.1.0 (`0x0000…6e03`), which takes a supertransaction signed as EIP-712 typed data (MEE 3, [below](#biconomy-mee-supertransactions)); until 2026-11-19 they got the plain stand-in, which in simple mode their validator refused (2 of about 600 operations in Base's scheduled run of 2026-11-18, 0.9% low). Their stand-in is now in that form, and asked to sign a supertransaction's hash (MEE 2.1's form), their validator reverts reading it, which the preview says. In `test/nexus-k1mee-owner.test.ts`, on the real code of Nexus, its factory, its bootstrap and the validator (from chain): the stand-in's layout is a real operation's from Base word by word but the owner's 65 bytes, the real validator takes that operation's real signature (and returns its bounds), and simple mode costs 1,624 gas more than the plain form with one proof element, 313 to 315 more for each further one.
- ZeroDev Kernel v3's enable mode: an operation that installs a validator or a permission (a dApp's session key, mostly) and is signed by it. Its signature carries what it installs and two signatures, the owner's `Enable` signature ([below](#kernel-session-keys-enable-signatures)) and the new key's over the operation: a hook, then the validator's, hook's and selector's data and the two signatures (after a marker on Kernel v3.2 and later, when chain-agnostic). Kernel reads any other signature as that and reverts (`AA23`), so a preview of one simulated its calls alone. A dApp's SDK builds that signature, with dummies until it is signed, so the operation a preview is handed carries what it installs: the stand-in keeps it and puts stand-ins in place of both signatures. The owner's is over the `Enable` digest as Kernel computes it (its version's domain, chain 0 when chain-agnostic, the account's validation nonce, on v3.3 one more where that validation is installed at it), by the account's root: ZeroDev's ECDSA and multi-chain validators (the owner read from the validator, or from the set-up while the operation deploys the account; for a multi-chain owner's Merkle root, the operation's own root and proof where they prove the digest), its passkey validators (an assertion with the digest as its challenge), the validator that keeps signers by id (the operation's id; its owner from the set-up, from the operation's own signature, or the signer whose key has that id), an EIP-7702 account (its own key), and a root permission with ZeroDev's ECDSA signer or Bleap's cosigning one. The key's is over the operation's hash (Kernel's chain-agnostic one where marked), by what it installs: ZeroDev's ECDSA signer or validator (the key in its install data), its WebAuthn signers and validators, or Bleap's key and cosigner, after the policies' parts of the operation's own signature. Each ECDSA stand-in is one of its own, recovered as its signer only over what that key signs, as the operation's own signature shows (a real one recovers over one form) or else as wallets sign it (the digest itself, but on Kernel v3.2 its `personal_sign` form: 48 of 48 owners on Base; a key, the hash as a message), and over any other digest as no one's key, for the precompile's price too, as a real signature over a digest it wasn't made for: a validator's check of the bare digest before its `personal_sign` form costs what it will. A root or key of another kind, or an operation without enable data, runs as before.
  - Replayed on 2026-11-15 (`npm run accuracy:4337 -- --tx`, the operation's own signature as its context carries it): 177 operations in enable mode, 169 on Base from its archive state (33 from its last two days, the rest from the 1,000,000 blocks before), 6 on BNB Chain and 2 on Arbitrum One within minutes of their blocks. Every outcome right, every one run whole. The stand-in validated for 167: 85 multi-chain owners and 32 ECDSA validator owners installing ZeroDev's ECDSA signer, 27 Bleap accounts, 16 roots that keep signers by id (6 installing ZeroDev's WebAuthn signer), 5 EIP-7702 accounts and 2 passkey owners. The other 10 have a root or key it doesn't take (two root validators it doesn't know, a guardian validator installed, and a passkey signing for a root that keeps signers by id) and ran with their own signatures, as before. Gas within 0.05% for 155 of the 162 alone in their bundle and within 0.1% for all but one, a median 0.011% high; that one, 0.25% high, is a passkey owner verified without the precompile, by a P-256 verifier in Solidity, whose gas depends on the signature's values. Token movements exact for all 118 that moved any. With ZeroDev's dummy in place of each ECDSA signature (an owner's signature for the root that keeps signers by id, and a signature after policies' parts, kept as they are), as its SDK sends the operation before signing, the same 167 validate with the same gas; for the 16 roots that keep signers by id, that is with the owner's own signature. Before, the harness skipped them all; previewed with the dummies, 40 of the 41 most recent ran their calls alone (`EnableNotApproved`), 6 of those showing calls that fail where they succeeded, and the one whose owner is a passkey ran whole only because the dummies keep its real assertion. Ordinary operations are unchanged: 70 on Base, Polygon and BNB Chain (`--blocks`), and the 17 other operations of these Base accounts, replayed the same before and after.
  - Found by these replays and fixed: a stand-in asked over another digest went on to the real precompile, about 300 gas more each time (on the same 123 operations, a first version came out a median 0.11% high, 18 within 0.05%; now 0.011%, 117); and a Kernel v3.2 owner taken to sign the digest itself left the dummies' run one `ecrecover` short (6 on Base, 0.5–0.7% low).
- The operation runs at the gas price its bundle's transaction pays (`tx.gasprice`), taken as what the operation pays its bundler: the base fee plus its priority fee, up to its fee cap (on Arbitrum too, which charges tips: below); for an operation that pays less than the base fee (a gasless one, whose bundler pays), the base fee plus the node's suggested tip. Contracts read it: a pool that taxes swaps by the transaction's priority fee gave a different amount, or a failed swap, than a simulation at no price showed. A bundler can pay less than the operation offers (on Base, bundles paid a 0.001 gwei tip where operations offered 0.0015), so such a swap can come out a little better than previewed; the replay harness uses the price the bundle really paid.
- A paymaster's operation is simulated as sent by a bundler that paymaster's operations came through lately (found from its latest `UserOperationEvent`s, looked up alongside the stand-in's shape and remembered for ten minutes; the simulation's own address when there's none, or none within 1.5 s): a paymaster can check `tx.origin` or work differently for it. One on Arbitrum One charged 9,130 gas more as its own bundlers than as an address it didn't know, 4% of an operation's gas. One that only accepts certain bundlers and refuses the first (one busy on Ethereum and Arbitrum refuses others with `BundlerNotAllowed(address)`) is retried from the others, up to five; so is one that refuses it in its charge after the calls (`postOp`: on EntryPoint v0.6 a failing `postOp` rejects the operation, `AA50`; from v0.7 the calls are undone and the paymaster still pays). Coinbase's VerifyingPaymaster on Base (`0x8bf5…e105`, v0.6, verified on Sourcify) does that: its `postOp` reverts `BundlerNotAllowed(tx.origin)` when its data's `allowAnyBundler` byte is 0 and the bundler isn't on its list; its 82 operations in 2,000 blocks (2026-11-15) all had that byte 0 and all came from its one listed bundler, `0xbbd1…1d47`. Where that bundler's lookup took longer than the first run waits for it (1.5 s), the operation was simulated as the simulation's own address and shown as one bundlers would reject (`AA50 postOp revert`): 1 of the 13 through it in a survey's run. With the lookup made slow on purpose (2 s), 9 of 9 of its operations replayed against their receipts went from that rejection to run whole, the stand-in validated, gas 0.011–0.069% high; with the lookup as it is, 86 of 86 Base v0.6 operations came out the same before and after (12 of them through it). The operation's own account is never taken for one (an account that sends its own operations, as an EIP-7702 account can: run as it, it would be handed the bundler's balance to validate and spend). The lookup reads back 200 blocks, then 2,000, 10,000 and 20,000, then four older windows of 20,000 (or the node's cap): the paymaster of the cosigned EIP-7702 delegate's operations (above) takes only its own bundlers ("AA33 reverted: Invalid tx origin" for any other), and its operations can be hours apart (its last was 6,600 blocks back on Ethereum and 93,000 on Base when previewed; Ethereum's public node answers 10,000 blocks back, no more). With the lookup reaching only 20,000 blocks (2,000 on Ethereum, which refuses the wider read), such an operation was shown as one bundlers would reject.
- An account whose deployment, or validation, checks the bundler sending it is simulated the same way. OKX Wallet's accounts that one of its factories deploys on Ethereum (`0x335e…9fe3`, through an implementation at `0x29cc…0796`) only initialize when OKX's list has the transaction's origin (`isWhitelistedBundler(tx.origin)` on `0x9134…ac2c`; `InvalidBundler()` otherwise, which the factory wraps in its own error): simulated as the simulation's own address, an operation that succeeded on-chain, sent by `0xc868…686a`, was shown as one bundlers would reject ("AA13 initCode failed or OOG"). Now, when deploying the account fails (AA13), the operation runs again as each of up to five bundlers that recently sent that factory's deployments (from the EntryPoint's `AccountDeployed` events, whose factory isn't indexed: every deployment's log in the window is read and the factory's kept; back 200, 2,000 and 10,000 blocks and no further, and remembered as long as a paymaster's), and when an existing account's validation reverts (AA23) and its code reads `tx.origin` (an `ORIGIN` instruction that can run: not in push data or the compiler's metadata, nor in the bytes after an instruction that ends execution, up to the next `JUMPDEST`, where the compiler keeps its data), as each that sent its own operations (its `UserOperationEvent`s; not the account itself, for one that sends its own: run as it, it was handed the bundler's balance to validate and spend). The first that gets it past that failure is used for the rest of the simulation, and a note names it: "Simulated as sent by 0xc868…, a bundler that recently deployed accounts through this account's factory: sent by anyone else (the transaction's origin), deploying the account fails (AA13 …), so only bundlers it accepts can include this operation." With none, the rejection stands and says it failed sent by those bundlers too, if the node answered each run (a run it didn't answer, as under load, says nothing of that bundler, and the rejection is then the plain one). A bundler rejected exactly as the simulation's own address is (the same revert data, for the same gas) ends the runs, and the rejection says it failed sent by "the busiest of the 5 bundlers": the failure doesn't turn on who sends it. What that costs an AA13 that has nothing to do with the sender (medians of five previews, 2026-11-15): on Base, a factory with no deployments 2.1 s against 1.3 s without the lookup (4.6 s reading as far back as for a paymaster, 100,000 blocks in eight reads; three now), a busy one given data it rejects 2.0 s against 1.2 (2.5 s with all five of its bundlers run; one now); on Ethereum, a factory with no deployments 1.9 s against 1.2 (2.6 s). Where validation then reverts on the stand-in and only the calls are simulated, the account is deployed as that bundler as well (from it, through the SenderCreator's address). The lookup is made only on such a failure. A deploying account's validation isn't retried so (its code can't be read before it exists). How common this is (2026-11-15), testing each deployment at the state before its block (the factory called from the SenderCreator's address as its real bundler, and as two other addresses): 1,518 deployments by 25 factories (49 factory-and-chain pairs), on Ethereum 20 by 9 factories and on Polygon 9 by 5 as they came over about an hour (their public nodes keep about 100 blocks of state), on Base 1,341 by all 22 factories that deployed over 100,000 blocks, on BNB Chain 112 by all 7 over 9,000 blocks, on Arbitrum One 36 by 6 over 6,000 blocks and as they came. Every one deployed as the two other addresses as it did as its own bundler (two on Arbitrum One, by `0x85e2…df00` and `0xd703…42d5`, as one of the two: the node refused the other's call under load). The OKX factory deployed 4 of the 573 accounts deployed on Ethereum over 10,000 blocks (0.7%), all sent by `0xc868…686a`, and none on the other four chains over 20,000 to 100,000 blocks; its bundler was found in 1.2 s (two log reads, 200 and 2,000 blocks). The code checked is the one the account runs as read here: its own, its EIP-7702 delegate's or its EIP-1967 implementation's; a Safe proxy's singleton (in its slot 0) and an EIP-1167 clone's implementation aren't followed, so their `ORIGIN` isn't seen. Read so, of 1,500 recent operations' accounts on the five chains (300 each; 102 different codes running), one code reads `tx.origin`, an EIP-7702 delegate's (`0x5a7f…6f6d`, 1 account on Arbitrum One), and it doesn't refuse other bundlers in validation: its latest operation validated as the simulation's own address, before this change and after. A first version of the check also counted the implementation of 6 accounts on Base (`0x330e…5c13`), whose `ORIGIN` byte is only in data after its code (the "\x19Ethereum Signed Message:\n32" it hashes: "32" is `0x33 0x32`), as is a Polygon delegate's (`0xa253…33b7`); of a second sample's 94 codes (400 accounts on each of Ethereum, Base, BNB Chain and Polygon), the same EIP-7702 delegate, on BNB Chain, is the only one that reads it. Followed through their proxies, Safe's 1.3.0 and 1.4.1 singletons (L2 and not; more than half of Base's v0.7 senders run one: 58 of 94 and 55 of 85 in two samples of 300 blocks on 2026-11-15) do reach `ORIGIN`, but only in `handlePayment`'s gas-refund receiver (`refundReceiver == address(0) ? tx.origin : refundReceiver`), never in validation: not following them keeps the lookup off their `AA23`s. Replayed against their receipts before and after it, the two versions following the same blocks at once (725 operations: Ethereum v0.7 150 and v0.6 75, Base v0.7 and v0.6 100 each, BNB Chain v0.8 100, Polygon v0.7 100, Arbitrum One v0.7 100), every outcome was right with both and every operation both replayed came out the same (whole or not, the stand-in, gas and tokens), but for two on Arbitrum One that the node refused under load in the first run (run again, the same with both); with the limits above, 60 more (Ethereum v0.7 40, Base v0.7 20) came out the same as without them. None of them met an origin check: the OKX factory deploys a few accounts a day, and none came while this was measured. One came later (block 26,421,252, replayed 50 blocks on): before this change it was simulated failing (AA13) where it had succeeded; now it deploys as `0xc868…686a`, its validation reverts on the stand-in (AA23: no stand-in fits that account yet), and its calls alone, the account deployed as that bundler, came out with the receipt's outcome and its exact token movements.
- EntryPoint v0.6 and v0.7 know nothing of EIP-7702: an operation for them with an `initCode` is always a factory deploying the account, even when the account itself is the signer.
- When the chain's node fails the whole operation's simulation (an HTTP 429 or 5xx the client's own retries didn't get past, a timeout, a dropped connection, a block it won't run, or the run reverting without any of the EntryPoint's reasons), a failure that can pass is asked once more, 2 s on; still failing, the preview says so (**info** `userop_simulation_failed`: the node's failure, not the operation's or its account's) and goes on to the calls alone. So is a run as another bundler (a paymaster's or a factory's, above) that the node fails: the first run's rejection isn't shown as one bundlers would make, since the bundler the node failed could be one that is taken. It used to fall back to the calls alone saying nothing, which read as a stand-in the account didn't take: 3 of the 19 operations a survey of 2,025 on six chains didn't validate, whose stand-ins validated for every other operation of their kind: 2 on Polygon whose whole run came back with nothing, twice each (the node's error wasn't kept), and 1 on Arbitrum One, whose public node was answering HTTP 429 to most of the survey's requests. The replay harness counts them apart ("not whole, the node failed it") and asks once more, 10 s on, while the node keeps the block's state. Replayed before and after on the same blocks (2026-11-15), 117 of 117 Polygon v0.7 operations came out the same (no node failure met); on Arbitrum One v0.7, 31 of 34 did, and the 3 others met the public node's HTTP 429 in one run and not the other: before, one of those was shown as its calls alone with nothing said (its tokens counted as differing) and one not simulated at all; after, one was reported as the node's failure, the stand-in validating for the rest. With the node answering HTTP 429 to every whole-operation run (on purpose, for 3 Base operations), the preview before showed their calls alone with nothing said, and the harness counted them as calls only; after, each says the node failed it, and the harness counts them so.
- When the whole operation can't be simulated (validation reverts on the stand-in; a simulator that reports no logs), its calls are simulated on their own, as the EntryPoint makes them: the account deployed through its SenderCreator if it's new, then called from the EntryPoint (with headroom for the cold access a standalone call pays). Accounts that only run validated calls are then simulated directly from the account, when their code reads transient storage and their calls have the common `(target, value, data)` shapes. An account with no code (it gets an EIP-7702 delegation in the bundle itself) would run nothing, and the preview says so. The prefund is checked on its own too: **caution** when the account's EntryPoint deposit plus its balance (or the paymaster's deposit) is less than the most the operation can cost.
- Checked with `npm run accuracy:4337` ([below](#simulation-accuracy)) on recent operations, replayed as each new block arrived on the state each met: the block before, with the block's header, its earlier transactions (each with its own gas limit, price and access list, as it ran) and the bundle's own EIP-7702 authorizations. Run on a little over the gas each had used instead, the block's earlier transactions didn't always do what they had done: on Base three operations of an arbitrage bot (2026-11-16) showed succeeding, 8-13% over in gas, where on-chain they had failed short of their profit; on their own limits all three came out as their receipts, within 0.05%, and 45 others on four chains as before. Where a replayed operation fares otherwise than its receipt, the operation as it was mined (its own signature, its bundle's price) is run on the same state too, to tell a state that isn't the chain's from a preview's own difference. Latest runs (2026-11-10), each following new blocks for 20 to 25 minutes (on Ethereum after the 100 blocks already behind the head), every operation compared with its receipt: 728 on Ethereum and Arbitrum One, and 1,771 on Base, Polygon and BNB Chain in a first run of 15 minutes each:

  | Chain | EntryPoint | Operations | Outcome matches | Ran whole | Stand-in validated | Gas within 0.5% | Gas within 0.05% | Token movements matched |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | Ethereum | v0.7 | 300 (11 in bundles of several, 12 SafeOps) | 297 | 298 | 298 | 181/181 | 44 | 264/266 |
  | Ethereum | v0.8 | 114 (7 in bundles) | 114 | 114 | 114 | 57/57 | 6 | 98/98 |
  | Ethereum | v0.6 | 101 | 101 | 99 | 98 | 93/94 | 73 | 83/84 |
  | Arbitrum One | v0.7 | 175 | 175 | 170 | 166 | 94/99 | 34 | 133/133 |
  | Arbitrum One | v0.8 | 25 | 25 | 25 | 25 | 19/19 | 0 | 23/23 |
  | Arbitrum One | v0.6 | 11 | 11 | 11 | 11 | 5/5 | 4 | 10/10 |
  | Arbitrum One | v0.9 | 2 (both SafeOps) | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2/2 | 0 | – |
  | Base | v0.7 | 300 (14 in bundles, 147 SafeOps) | 300 | 299 | 290 | 49/49 | 12 | 157/157 |
  | Base | v0.6 | 300 | 299 | 300 | 300 | 283/290 | 164 | 147/148 |
  | Polygon | v0.7 | 271 | 270 | 268 | 268 | 207/207 | 147 | 74/75 |
  | Polygon | v0.8 | 300 (42 in bundles) | 300 | 300 | 300 | 244/244 | 201 | 232/232 |
  | BNB Chain | v0.7 | 300 | 296 | 296 | 269 | 23/23 | 6 | 175/177 |
  | BNB Chain | v0.8 | 300 | 300 | 300 | 300 | 97/97 | 37 | 288/291 |

  Gas is judged for operations alone in their bundle, simulated whole on their block's state; token movements count those within a gas-priced fee's rounding (most of the inexact ones: a paymaster charging in a token). The two v0.7 rows are the day's second runs; the others its first, run before some of the fixes below.
  - On the new chains (gas judged where the stand-in validated; on BNB Chain not for operations run a second after their block, most of them):
    - Found and fixed: a wrap of the wrapped native token (WETH9's `Deposit`, which has no `Transfer`) wasn't counted where balance changes come from the simulated events, so a BNB Chain operation wrapping 0.006 BNB and passing it on showed 0.006 WBNB going out as well.
    - Signatures no stand-in takes, so the account rejects it and its own check doesn't run: on OP Mainnet (13 operations in a later run, every outcome right), Biconomy Nexus through its MEE validator, its owner's (stood in for since 2026-11-15: below). Their gas isn't judged.
    - Two on Base had none until 2026-11-13, about 3% of its v0.7 operations (216 of 7,538 in six hours), 20 of 600 in a scheduled run, their gas 3–51% low. Quip's ShrincsWallet signs with a post-quantum one-time hash-based (SHRINCS) signature as well as its owner's ECDSA one; its stand-in is a real SHRINCS signature, by a key made up for the run whose commitment is set in the wallet's storage, on the leaf the wallet's key signs next (its lowest unused one), so its verifier walks every chain and path the real one does: 45 of 45 operations validated, gas within 0.004% (38–51% low before). The bullbit.ai wallet (implementation 0x330e…5c13, unpublished: its layout read from its operations) takes a passkey's WebAuthn assertion, checked with P256VERIFY, whose clientDataJSON it walks byte by byte: 30 of 30 validated, 27 within 0.1%; the other 3 carry the key Chrome adds to some (109 bytes, 30,296 gas) and come out 6–7% low, reported, not judged (for that wallet only: ZeroDev's and Safe's passkeys pay nothing measurable for it). A 150-operation Base v0.7 run after: the stand-in validated for 148 of 148 that ran whole, gas within 0.5% for all 140 compared. (A second build of ShrincsWallet, 0x6808…4fbc, the same storage and verifier, takes the same stand-in.)
    - Four more found the same day, each run whole after (fresh operations on Base and Polygon, gas within 0.2%): Splits' Smart Vault (a multi-signer account whose signature starts with its type, an enum: the stand-in's 0xff reverted with Panic(0x21)), now signed in its form by its signers, EOAs and passkeys, to its threshold (all but the last sign its light hash, of the account, nonce and calls with the signature's gas limits, the EntryPoint and the chain, the last the operation's: a passkey signing before the last asserts over the light hash, since 2026-11-19, when a vault of two passkeys on Base, both asserting over the operation's hash, had been refused; replayed after, validated, 0.016% under in gas); a Kernel v3 permission whose signer is ZeroDev's WebAuthn signer; a Kernel v3 being deployed through ZeroDev's FactoryStaker, its root validator and permissions read from its initialize(); and Kernel v3.2, which had no shape. Whether a ZeroDev passkey signature uses the P-256 precompile is the wallet's choice (its SDK's list of chains changed in December 2025, and wallets ship both): the stand-in ran Daimo's Solidity verifier on Base and Polygon, 335,000 gas, where the wallets used the precompile, so those operations came out 103–219% high, and on Arbitrum Blofin's wallet, which doesn't, 27–47% low. It is now read from the account's own last operation, else taken as most sign on its chain (measured: with the precompile Base 34 of 34, Polygon 8 of 8, Arbitrum One 141 of 147, Sepolia 19 of 24; without it Ethereum 17 of 17, BNB Chain 5 of 5): Base's and Polygon's 43 within 0.2%. And a Safe whose owner is a passkey's own signer contract (safe-modules' SafeWebAuthnSignerProxy, Picnic's wallet) got the ECDSA stand-in, which the contract's address recovered from, so Safe never asked it and its P-256 check (about 225,000 gas, in Solidity) didn't run: SafeOps 8–51% low on Ethereum, Base, Polygon and BNB Chain (15 replayed). The verifiers are read from the contract and it signs with a passkey's assertion, as for Safe's shared signer: those 15 within 1.3%. A Safe transaction's preview (execTransaction, run with the signing owner's stand-in) had the same gap, for both kinds of passkey owner, and gets the same stand-in: a Picnic Safe on Polygon sending nothing to itself, 97,401 gas before, 326,649 after. And an operation's calls run alone (when its validation can't be) on Kernel v3's `executeUserOp` reverted: Kernel runs the calls inside the execution hook validation stored for the operation's hash, which a run without validation hasn't, so it is set for the run as validation sets it: the hook the validator or permission the nonce names was installed with (`validationConfig`), none where it has none; calls that succeeded on-chain showed failing. (A first version set none for every account, and a hook that refuses the calls didn't run: on Base, all 12 such operations found ran under a hook, one of which refuses calls to modules, `ModuleCallNotAllowed`, and that call showed succeeding. A deploy's calls still run without a hook its `initialize()` installs: none of 65 recent FactoryStaker deploys on Base installed one.) And a Kernel v3's stand-ins are its ECDSA signature as it is and after 0xff (both seen on-chain); for a signer that isn't the account's, the first ran the whole operation with the signature refused and the second reverted in the ECDSA validator, and the second's rejection replaced the first's run, so the preview lost the operation's gas and showed its calls alone. A run that went through now stands over a later shape's rejection. Kernel's enable mode (an operation signed by a session key it installs) on an account it deploys was skipped then, as on one that exists; both run whole now (above). A Kernel v3 root validator that keeps signers by id (0x9334…04ba, unpublished: its layout read from its set-up and operations), whose signature is the signer's id and then its module's (an owner's ECDSA signature), rejected the stand-in on Sepolia (5 of 11 operations in one sample, all deploys through the FactoryStaker) and Polygon: it gets the id from the account's set-up, or as its last operation named it, and the 5 validated, gas within 0.05% (a later sample of 24: every one whole and validated). For an account that exists, the last operation alone wasn't enough: of 13 on Polygon previewed with a new operation, 8 refused the stand-in, 7 whose operations were older than the lookup reaches and one whose last was a passkey permission's; and on Base, of 18 operations replayed at their blocks, 3 whose latest earlier operation wasn't an owner's (an enable-mode one, a permission's) refused it (5.6–12.3% low). Its wallets name an owner's ECDSA key keccak256(signer module, owner) (all 1,183 such SignerAdded events on Polygon, Base and Sepolia; some Base accounts have several owners, each by its own id), so that id, the signer's own, is tried first and the last operation's after it: the 13 validate, and 17 of the 18 Base replays validated, gas within 0.21% (the other, signed through another of its signer modules, a 512-byte signature, still refuses it: 11.5% low).
    - Ambire's accounts (from the scheduled run of 2026-11-16, on Ethereum and Base: AmbireAccount `0x0f2a…c828`, verified on Sourcify), EIP-1167 clones whose implementation the preview didn't follow (it read ERC-1967 slots and EIP-7702 delegates only, so no stand-in shape for an account behind such a clone could apply). Their validation reads a signature's last byte as its mode, and the bare stand-in's v (27 or 28) as the Unprotected mode, which `validateUserOp` refuses (`SV_USED_UNBOUND`): the operation's calls ran alone. The stand-in now carries Ambire's Standard mode (`{r}{s}{v}` then 0x01, recovered over the EIP-712 digest of `AmbireOperation(account, hash)`), and an EIP-1167 clone runs its implementation's shape. Three of Base's, replayed after: each run whole, the stand-in validated, gas within 0.5%. The harness takes the signer from the operation's own signature over that digest (a key the account gives a privilege to, which it names in no other way).
    - Kernel v3.3 accounts deployed by a second `deployWithFactory` (factory `0xf928…7053`, unverified; its call takes a deployer's signature after the salt, `deployWithFactory(address,bytes,bytes32,bytes)`), whose root validator is a passkey's in webauthn-sol's form (`0xbbbe…5b1e`, already stood in for on deployed accounts): 72 of 2,541 EntryPoint v0.7 deployments on BNB Chain and 69 of 14,735 on Base in 200,000 blocks to 2026-11-17, none on Ethereum, Polygon or Arbitrum One. Only ZeroDev's FactoryStaker was known, so their deployments got the plain stand-in, which the passkey validator refused (0.6–0.9% low). Either form is now read by its call, its `createData` as Kernel's `initialize`: three of each chain's latest, replayed, validated, gas within 0.05% (five) and 0.5% (one).
    - Exa's accounts (Exactly's wallet, origin `https://web.exactly.app`; from the scheduled run of 2026-11-17, Arbitrum One v0.6): Alchemy's Modular Account v1 (`UpgradeableModularAccount` `0x0046…a61e`, verified on Sourcify) behind Solady's ERC-1967 proxy, whose functions validate through Exactly's `WebauthnOwnerPlugin` (published source, unverified; `0x9d65…d7d5` on Arbitrum One, `0x8f49…4ca0` on OP Mainnet, so it is known by its answer to `ownersPublicKeysOf`). Its signature is the signing owner's index (a byte), then for a passkey webauthn-sol's `WebAuthnAuth` over `abi.encode(toEthSignedMessageHash(userOpHash))`, or for an address owner its signature: the bare stand-in's first byte named no owner, validation reverted (AA23), and the operation's calls ran alone, its paymaster's USDC fee (20,081 of 499,020,081 moved) left out. The stand-in now reads the validation the operation's function names (`getExecutionFunctionConfig`) and, where that plugin keeps owners, signs as the account's passkey (asked by the account itself, as Exa's wallet asks) or as the address owner asked. Exa's operations were 21 of about 1,500 v0.6 bundles on OP Mainnet, a few a day on Arbitrum One. The failing one, previewed again on the latest state with its nonce set back: run whole, its passkey's stand-in accepted by the plugin's real check (its calls then failed there: its paymaster's sponsorship and its bridge quote had expired). Its real code, set up through `initialize` with Exa's plugin, takes the stand-in in the tests.
    - An account with session keys on Sepolia (from the scheduled run of 2026-11-18, v0.7; an unpublished implementation, `0x7e92…91c7`, behind ERC-1967 proxies, with session-key, social-recovery and freeze modules; its checks read from its operations' validation in a local EVM): its owner signs bare, over the operation hash's EIP-191 form, and a session key after a `0x01` type byte, as `abi.encode(signature, bytes32[] proof)`, an OpenZeppelin Merkle proof that the key may make the call (leaves `keccak256(keccak256(target ‖ selector))`) against the root the account keeps for that key, empty where that call is the only one allowed. The harness found no signature after the type byte, so it replayed the session keys' operations as their owner's, whose bare signature the account takes by a cheaper path: 4 operations 15.8% low, and others 7-9% in later replays. The stand-in now goes bare first, then as a session key's with an empty proof; a key allowed more than one call gets the proof from its own last signature (below). A session key's operation on a fresh replay: within 0.06% (the stand-in's ecrecover overhead), its owner's within 0.05%.
    - Refused every way, a stand-in for a named signer takes the form of that signer's own last signature on the account: the latest of the account's last six operations with an ECDSA signature in it that recovers as the signer, over the operation's hash or its EIP-191 form, with the stand-in in its place. The wrapper and whatever it carries (a type byte, a proof, a key's id) are then the wallet's own. It is kept only where the account accepts it, and the preview says the stand-in took that form. Seen working on SentinelAccountV4 (`0xbe4d…4190`, verified on Sourcify; Sepolia), whose owner signs after a `0x01` type byte: its operations, refused the bare stand-in, validate in that form (gas within 0.04%). Its `0x02` form, a Groth16 proof over the operation's hash, has no stand-in: its operations are previewed as the account refusing the signature (22.8% low on one).
    - Kernel v3 accounts whose validator is ZeroDev's WeightedECDSAValidator 0.0.3 (`0xed89…eeee`, verified on Sourcify; three accounts on Sepolia, from the scheduled run of 2026-11-18, refused the plain stand-in, 3.9% low): the validator takes the other guardians' approvals, 65 bytes each over the EIP-712 `Approve(bytes32 callDataAndNonceHash)`, as many as it takes with the signer's weight to reach the threshold, then the signer's own over the operation hash's EIP-191 form. The stand-in reads the guardians, their weights and the threshold from the validator (from the install data for an account its factory deploys) and puts the other guardians' stand-ins first, in the validator's order, each recovered as its guardian. With a delay set, only a proposal approved beforehand runs, and the stand-in is the plain one. One of those operations, previewed again on the latest state: refused the plain stand-in, validated with this one.
    - Frak's wallets (origin `https://frak.id`; from the scheduled run of 2026-11-18, Arbitrum One v0.6): Kernel v2.4 accounts whose default validator is Frak's MultiWebAuthNValidatorV2 (`0x0000…7b77`, verified on Sourcify), a passkey's. Kernel v2's accounts were all given the ECDSA validator's form (the mode `0x00000000`, then the owner's signature), and one being deployed through ZeroDev's Kernel v2 factory got the plain stand-in, which the validator refused (4.4% low). The stand-in now reads the default validator (`getDefaultValidator()`, or for an account the factory deploys, `initialize`'s), and for Frak's takes its form after the mode: a byte for the P256 precompile (as the account's last signature had it, else the precompile) or Frak's verifier contract, the passkey's authenticator id hash (the account's main passkey, or the first word of the validator's enable data), then the challenge's offset in clientDataJSON, r, s, the two lengths (3 bytes each), authenticatorData and clientDataJSON, which the validator checks for the user-present flag and the operation's hash as the challenge. That account, previewed again on the latest state: validated.
    - Alchemy's Modular Account v2 accounts behind an 845-byte ERC-1967 proxy (unverified, solc 0.8.15; three on Base in the same run), whose `SemiModularAccountStorageOnly` (`0x0000…b25a`, verified on Sourcify) reads a signature's first byte as an enum, so the bare stand-in reverted with `Panic(0x21)`: the preview didn't follow code that long to its implementation (only short proxies and OKX's 797-byte one, by its code's hash). That proxy is now known by its code's hash too, and the implementation takes Modular Account v2's 0xff00 form: all three run whole after, the stand-in validated, gas within 0.5%.

Operations are now simulated at the price their bundle pays (below): replayed again, the preview says the calls fail with `IG`, and its gas is within 0.005% of the receipt's. A following run of 641 operations on five chain-and-version pairs had every outcome right and every compared gas within 0.5%.

The preview now says so (`token_created_here`: the token doesn't exist yet, can't be named, and can land at another address), and the harness counts the two as one when neither existed before the block and the amounts match.

The stand-in validated for every operation that ran whole on Ethereum and BNB Chain (746) and for 239 of 244 on Base (the five: ShrincsWallet and the unpublished passkey wallet). No operation had to give up its block's earlier transactions.
    - The first scheduled run (2026-11-10, 5,280 operations on 14 chain-and-version pairs, before the fixes above) had 22 differing. What became of them:
      - Earlier bundles went missing from the replays. The block's earlier transactions ran with a gas limit a little over what each had used; an EntryPoint refuses a bundle whose gas left is under an operation's limits (AA95), whatever the operation goes on to use, so earlier bundles reverted in the replay and what they did was missing. On Arbitrum One, keepers race to fulfil the same Ostium price request: the loser, four transactions after the winner in its block, fails on-chain (`NotInitiated`), and showed succeeding. In a run before the fix 10 of 42 operations differed (8 outcomes); with earlier bundles given their own gas limit, 33 of 33 outcomes are right, a lost race included, and all 31 compared are within 0.05% of the gas.
      - When a node refuses a block's earlier transactions altogether (a full block's are too much for a public node), the replay now keeps the account's own and the other bundles before giving them all up, and an outcome that differs without them is reported, not failed: the replay didn't meet what the operation met (an Ethereum deposit failed for tokens gone by its turn, `TransferFromFailed`).

      - The six Base operations, replayed again on the current code: all match (two swaps taxed by their priority fee, a paymaster's fee rounding, the token created in the operation).
      - About 110 BNB Chain v0.8 operations had run as calls alone, their paymaster refusing an unknown bundler ("bundler verify failure"); with the bundler lookup's fixes a later run ran all 371 whole.
      - Two Ethereum operations' paymaster refused them (`UnrecycledBundle`): it only pays for bundles sent through its own contract, which relays them to the EntryPoint. Their calls are still simulated alone; the error is now named.
      - 18 operations weren't replayed for the node's rate limit (HTTP 429): the client now waits as long as the node asks, and the harness asks once more.
    - The scheduled runs of 2026-11-13 and 2026-11-14 had 6 differing, in five jobs. What became of them (replayed again where the node still served their state: Base's keeps weeks, BNB Chain's about an hour, Ethereum's 128 blocks and Arbitrum One's 30 seconds):
      - Base v0.6, the second operation of a bundle of two, a swap through a Metarouter that checks the gas left before its call: it failed in the replay (`InsufficientGasForCall`) and ran on-chain. Run in one transaction with the first, as mined, it succeeds; in a transaction of its own after it, it fails; given what the first touched as warm (USDC and the EntryPoint's slots), it succeeds, its gas 0.29% over the receipt's. A bundle's later operations now have their earlier operations' access list warm (above), and it matches; in fresh samples their gas came closer too (BNB Chain v0.8: 1.2–6.1% high, from 3.3–14%; Base v0.6: 0.9–2.1%, from 4.2–4.6%).
      - Base v0.6, an Aave borrow of 150 USDC: the variable debt token's `Transfer` (and `Mint`) logs the interest accrued since the account's last action with the amount (150.179495), where `balanceOf` had counted that interest already; the preview's 150 is the balance's change. BNB Chain v0.8, a token (`0xda18…fee1`) that pays a holder its pending rewards when it sends: an account sending 315.77 logged 315.75 coming back, already in its `balanceOf`. Both disagree with the events, and the balance across the block didn't settle it (other transactions in the block move the rewards' share). The harness now runs the mined operation on the preview's state (above): both match exactly, the BNB Chain one on a fresh operation of the same account (the original's state is gone).
      - Arbitrum One v0.7, an account running `execTransaction` on a Safe it owns, which sent 14,783 USDT: the preview showed the Safe's change, and the harness compared it with the account's own movements (none). Token movements are now compared account by account.
      - Ethereum v0.7, a gasless Modular Account v2 (EIP-7702) operation of a keeper running a 0x swap through a Safe module, its gas used 11.8% high; and Ethereum v0.6, an Aave collateral swap through ParaSwap that failed on-chain (no revert data, about 63/64 of its call gas used: out of gas) and succeeded in the replay, 7.3% under the receipt's gas. Their blocks' state is no longer served (the public node keeps 128 blocks; older reads need an archive token), so neither could be replayed again: not fixed, not explained. Both accounts send operations in bursts (the keeper about 50 in three hours on 2026-11-14, the other about 45 that day, every one but this one succeeding); neither sent one while they were followed for half an hour on 2026-11-15, and fresh samples of the same chain and versions (120 v0.7 and 25 v0.6 operations) had every outcome right and none differing.
      - Found while checking these, on Base v0.7: a SafeOp sending a token to a Safe it then deploys, and running a transaction on that Safe sending the token on. The third call's own decoding simulates that transaction alone, without the calls before it, where it fails (`ERC20InsufficientBalance`), and the preview said so, though the whole operation ran. A later call's Safe transaction failing on its own is now kept as a note when the batch, run in order, succeeds with its calls and the Safe logs `ExecutionSuccess` in it: a batch that succeeds doesn't say so by itself, as `execTransaction` can fail without reverting (`ExecutionFailure`: before Safe 1.3.0 always, from it with a `safeTxGas` or gas price), and an operation's calls can fail without its run reverting.
  - The three Ethereum v0.7 outcomes that differ are OKX Wallet passkey accounts running their calls through `executeUserOp`, which is handed the operation's signature: their signature names the key that signs, and the bare stand-in named none, so the calls reverted (`NonAdminSelfCall()`). The stand-in now takes the account's form (above). For any other account that reads its signature while running its calls, the preview still says the signed operation can fare differently, and the harness counts those as warned rather than wrong.
  - What the runs found, now fixed: Pimlico's paymaster refusing the simulation's own address (`BundlerNotAllowed`) when the lookup of its bundlers was refused under load (8 operations); a paymaster on Arbitrum One charging 9,130 gas more as its own bundlers, now simulated as one (2, 4% low); two EIP-7702 accounts whose stale ERC-1967 slot gave them Coinbase's shape; LightAccounts and Biconomy Smart Account v2s their factories deploy in the operation; a Zarb app Safe whose passkey owner (Safe's WebAuthn shared signer, verified by FCL's contract) the ECDSA stand-in skipped (2, 30% and 45% low: the second Arbitrum run's two failures). In the harness: Bleap's Kernel accounts whose root is the cosigning permission (their owner read from the module), Biconomy's signature wrapper, and a bundle 336 transactions into a full block whose earlier transactions left it too little of the node's 50M gas cap (AA95, now replayed without them).
  - Not replayed: session keys' operations (SmartSession; 20 on Arbitrum v0.7; since 2026-11-15 only those through a session validator other than Rhinestone's OwnableValidator and Biconomy's K1MeeValidator: above) and, since these runs, Modular Account v2 operations carrying a deferred action, neither of which a wallet signs as an operation hash (Kernel operations in enable mode, which these runs skipped too, are replayed now: above); and operations whose block's state the node no longer served by their turn (14 on Arbitrum, 3 on Ethereum). Still simulated as calls alone or with the stand-in rejected: Biconomy Nexus operations on a later Nexus, through K1MeeValidator 1.1.0, whose supertransaction hash is EIP-712's (Nexus 1.2's owners signing through its default validator, K1MeeValidator 1.0.3, are stood in for: above), Modular Account v2 validations reverting on the stand-in (other than Aave Labs' 2-of-2 module's: above), and a Kernel permission whose signer isn't Bleap's. HyperEVM's public RPC serves only the latest state, so its operations can't be replayed at the block before them; the fork simulator's path is covered by the tests.
- A `SafeOp` runs whole through the Safe's 4337 module: the signing owner's signature (or, if the signer isn't an owner, the first owner's) is the ECDSA stand-in over the SafeOp's digest, any other owners the threshold needs approve on-chain (Safe's `approvedHashes`), and the Safe's configuration is compared before and after. A passkey owner through Safe's WebAuthn shared signer (the Safe keeps the passkey in its own storage) signs as a contract instead: a WebAuthn assertion in the form Safe's WebAuthn library decodes, and the P-256 verifiers its passkey names (the precompile, or a verifier contract such as FCL's) replaced so that they run their real check on it and then accept it. On Arbitrum One, a Zarb app Safe whose passkey is checked by FCL's verifier in Solidity had come out 30% and 45% low with the ECDSA stand-in (about 228,000 gas of verification left out); the passkey stand-in adds 234,000 back. A Zeal Wallet passkey owner (Zeal's `Signer`, verified on Sourcify, the same address on every chain here) checks its passkey itself, with FreshCryptoLib's P-256 code in Solidity and no verifier to replace, so it signs a real WebAuthn assertion instead, by a passkey made up for the run whose key the signer's check is given in place of its own, in the form (origin and fields) the passkey last signed the Safe's operations in, since the signer's origin check costs up to 290,000 gas more or less by origin: a Zeal SafeOp on Ethereum that the ECDSA stand-in had put about 74% low (on its state rewound, the node having pruned its block's) came within 0.54% of the same operation run with its real signature, and a deploying one 43% low within 0.07% (Zeal's passkey-signed SafeOps were 3 of 7,873 through Safe's 4337 modules on the five chains scanned on 2026-11-13, all on Ethereum, EntryPoint v0.6). A Safe being deployed, or one with no passkey-signed operation within the node's reach of logs (Ethereum's public node serves about 10,000 blocks), signs in the form of the origin `https://passkey.zealwallet.com`, the second of the six the signer's origin check scans for: of 144 Zeal signers created on Arbitrum One and Base in the five months to 2026-11-17, 71 signed their first operation from that origin (67 in that form), 71 from Zeal's Android app (the first origin), whose check costs 16,000 gas less (369,817 to 385,898 gas for a signer's `isValidSignature` on Arbitrum One), and 2 from the browser extension. The extension's form, the default before, took 609,038: an Android-signed SafeOp on Arbitrum One, by a Safe whose operation before it was over a year back, came out 52% high in it, and an Android passkey's deploy on Ethereum 23% high. Zeal's four deploying SafeOps on Base in a month, replayed: within 0.5% (the two signed in that form), 1.8% high (one from the Android app) and 24% low (one from a browser whose clientDataJSON Chrome had lengthened with a member `other_keys_can_be_added_here`, which every origin's scan pays for). The made-up key stands in for every Zeal signer the run reaches, not just the signing owner's (an operation whose calls check another Zeal passkey would see it refused); a deploy takes the implementation Zeal's factory names now, and falls back to the ECDSA stand-in if that isn't the one stood in for. Recent Safe4337Module v0.3.0 operations on Ethereum, replayed at the block before them, came within 0.1% of the gas the receipts charged; with every owner approving on-chain instead, they were 0.2–0.5% off, an approval costing less than the signature check the Safe will run. On Arbitrum One, a live EntryPoint v0.9 operation through Safe4337MultiChainSignatureModule, replayed as its block arrived, ran whole through v0.9's simulation code: its outcome as the receipt's, its gas within 0.08% (2.2% low with every owner approving).
- `test/erc4337-sim.test.ts` runs the real simulation code on an in-process chain through the fork simulator: an owner's validation passing as signed, a token paymaster's charge, a signer the account doesn't accept, failing calls, an unfunded paymaster, a paymaster that only pays for its own bundler (run as the bundler its recent operations came through, looked for as far back as the node answers), a factory whose accounts only deploy for its own bundler and an account whose validation only takes its own (run as the bundler their recent deployments, or its operations, came through; with only bundlers it doesn't take, the rejection stands and says so, but not of bundlers whose runs the node didn't answer; a factory's lookup reaching 10,000 blocks back; an account that sends its own operations not run as sent by itself), a nonce ahead of the account's, the patched v0.6 EntryPoint, typed v0.8 and v0.9 operations, the passkey stand-in, the cosigned EIP-7702 delegate's on its deployed code (validated, for the gas a real signature in its form uses, and a real operation of it validated as signed), its second deployment's the same way (its registry's signer read from the chain: the stand-in follows a change of it, and the operation the old signer cosigned is refused, said to be the signature, not the account's funds; the registry switched to take every cosignature, validated, or to refuse every one, or with no signer, refused for that reason; a key other than the account's refused for that, certain whether or not the registry can be read), and a Modular Account v2 on Alchemy's deployed implementation validating through Aave Labs' deployed 2-of-2 module (`test/fixtures/mav2-multisig.json`: asked by either signer it validates and the note names the other, by anyone else it is refused naming both; real signatures in either order, for the stand-in's gas in the module's own; a real Arbitrum One operation validated as signed; an account of its own code with the same slot, validation and signers, validating anything, gets no such note). `test/erc4337-gaps.test.ts` runs, on code read from the chains (`test/fixtures/erc4337-gaps.json`), a BNB Chain operation that only deploys a Kernel v3.1 account through ZeroDev's FactoryStaker (whole, the validator it installs listed) and an operation with no calls and no factory; Coinbase's VerifyingPaymaster refusing the simulation's own address in `postOp` (AA50) and then run as its bundler, and a v0.7 paymaster doing the same; an executor module installed; and the node failing the whole run (asked once more; then said to be the node's). `test/kernel-enable-mode.test.ts` runs a Kernel operation in enable mode the same way (a mock of Kernel's enable mode with ZeroDev's ECDSA validator and signer, as their sources check): with the SDK's dummies and with real signatures, the stand-ins' run costs exactly the gas the real signatures' does; it also checks the stand-in's layout for ZeroDev's ECDSA and passkey validator roots and the root that keeps signers by id, and that every path through the `ecrecover` stand-in costs the precompile's price. `test/erc4337-standins.test.ts` runs a Send account (its validation, with Daimo's WebAuthn checks; asked by the account, and by an Ethereum key, whose note says who signs), a Safe7579 account's 2-of-2 SmartSession session (as the session's key, and as someone who isn't one, whose note names the session's keys) and a Nexus account's session whose nonce starts with `0x01` (not Kernel's enable mode) whole the same way, checks Send's stand-in against a real signature's layout, and checks the stand-in Bleap's V1 module gets on a deploying Kernel and in enable mode. `test/k1mee-session.test.ts` puts Biconomy's K1MeeValidator's deployed code (`test/fixtures/k1mee-session.json`) at its address: a real Arbitrum One session operation's signature validates there as SmartSession calls it (over another hash, or for another key, it doesn't), the simple-mode stand-in has that signature's layout, and Nexus accounts' K1MeeValidator sessions run whole: the operation's hash signed by the session's key (among one session, four, or forty with the key's among the oldest, the sessions read newest first until it turns up, 32 to a Multicall3 call where there is one), in the plain form for a real signature's gas, simple mode costing 2,117 more in its mock accounts; a supertransaction's hash with its two operations, each in simple mode with its proof for a real signature's gas, by `personal_sign` and by `eth_sign`; one operation with its proof (the root covering more: a danger); operations that don't make up the root (a danger, nothing run); a leaf with MEE's time bounds; operations whose accounts take no signature of the root here (an ECDSA account checking the operation's hash, an account's own K1MeeValidator) refused, not run over any digest; as someone else, the note names the key, or the newest keys and how many there are.
- `test/passkey-standins.test.ts` runs, at their own addresses, the code read from the chain (`test/fixtures/coinbase-copy.json`, `okx-passkey-verifier.json`, `barz.json`; the OKX and Barz accounts with the storage their calls read): the giga1 build of Coinbase Smart Wallet deploying and deployed, its passkey owner's stand-in validated with the note that the implementation isn't Coinbase's own, and another factory's account not taken for one when its implementation's name, replayable nonce key or EntryPoint differ, or the account isn't at that proxy's address; nor an account a factory naming the build deploys as other code, longer code keeping the build's address in its ERC-1967 slot, or a short one that isn't Solady's proxy; an OKX account behind the 797-byte proxy whose key is OKX's passkey verifier's, the stand-in laid out as its real signature and validated through the verifier's real code with its cosignature, asked by the account; asked by another address or by the verifier's cosigner, and with its key as a type-2 passkey asked by another address, refused, the note saying who signs; with the verifier upgraded or recording no cosigner, the last resort's stand-in, the preview saying why and that the refusal needn't mean the passkey can't sign; a Barz passkey account, whose real operation's own signature validates there, its stand-in in that signature's form validated by its Solidity P-256 check against the stand-in passkey's key, within 2% of a test passkey's real assertion's gas, and refused when another key is asked; and a ZeroDev passkey account's `usePrecompiled` read from an operation 2,500,000 blocks back through one wide read.

### Multi-chain Safe signatures

Safe4337MultiChainSignatureModule lets a Safe's owners sign once for operations on several chains: they sign the root of a Merkle tree (EIP-712 `MerkleTreeRoot`, with a domain that names no chain) whose leaves are the operations' SafeOp digests, each on its own chain, and every operation is later submitted with a proof of its leaf. The message is only the root, so by itself the preview is **danger**: "the message doesn't say which operations it covers, on which chains, or how many".

The wallet that built the tree can pass its operations as `context.safeOperations = [{ chainId, operation }, …]` (each `operation` the SafeOp message; at most 64 over the HTTP API). The previewer hashes each as the module does and rebuilds the root, under the layouts tree builders use (OpenZeppelin's merkle-tree, and level by level), with the leaves in the order given or sorted. With the module's pair hash (the smaller node first), a root that matches commits to exactly those operations: no other operation has a proof to it. Then the preview is "Sign Safe operations: 3 operations on 2 chains", each operation previewed as the SafeOp it is on its own chain (calls, warnings, simulation), and every operation's balance changes listed, each with its chain (`chainId`: the signature names none, so the request's chain says nothing of where funds move). Operations of more than one Safe are a **caution** naming them (`operations_multiple_accounts`): an app can add an operation of a Safe of its own that lists the signer as an owner, and what that Safe receives isn't yours, so only what each Safe sends (and what the connected account receives) is listed, what the others receive described with their operations. Operations that don't make up the root are a **danger** mismatch: the signature covers others that aren't shown.

### Biconomy MEE supertransactions

Biconomy's MEE (through its SDK, `@biconomy/abstractjs`) has a key sign once for operations on several chains: the supertransaction's hash, the root of a Merkle tree (OpenZeppelin's `SimpleMerkleTree`, its leaves sorted) whose leaves are the operations' hashes, signed as a message (`personal_sign`), as its SDK has it signed before MEE 2.2.1 (K1MeeValidator 1.0.3, the validator of most session keys' operations, above, is MEE 2.1.0's). The MEE node submits each operation with the root, the proof of its hash in the tree and the signature (K1MeeValidator's simple mode), and the validator checks the signature over the root and the proof. A session key's operation (through Rhinestone's SmartSession) is a leaf as its hash; one the account's own K1MeeValidator checks, as MEE's hash of its hash and its time bounds. The message is a hash that names nothing, so by itself the preview is a binary message that may be anything.

The wallet holding the supertransaction (its quote) can pass its operations as `context.meeOperations = [{ chainId, entryPoint, operation }, …]` (each `operation` as bundlers' RPC carries it, for EntryPoint v0.6 or v0.7, with `lowerBoundTimestamp` and `upperBoundTimestamp` for an operation whose leaf is MEE's hash; at most 64 over the HTTP API). The previewer hashes each as its EntryPoint does and rebuilds the root under the layouts tree builders use (as for a multi-chain Safe root, above). If they make it up exactly, the preview is "Sign a supertransaction: 2 operations on 2 chains", with an info that the signature authorizes these and nothing else, each operation previewed whole on its own chain with the stand-in in simple mode, that root and the operation's real proof in it (its validation costing what the MEE node's submission does), and every operation's balance changes listed, each with its chain (`chainId`), as for a multi-chain Safe root (above, operations of more than one account included: an app can add an operation of an account of its own that names the signer's key as a session key, which the root's signature validates too; before, its incoming tokens were listed against yours, and a drain read as a balanced swap at **info**). Each operation's run is read for the other operations' accounts on its chain too (their tokens, from its events), so what leaves your account is listed whichever operation moves it: an app's operation, run after one of yours that grants it an allowance, can take your tokens with it. "You sign as an owner" is said only of an operation whose account took the stand-in. Operations on one chain are simulated in the order they run (as supplied, but an account's operations under one nonce key in their nonce's order), each on the state the ones before it leave: a wrap and then a send of what it wrapped, each run alone on the latest state, showed the send failing. That is done for the first 8 of a chain (each runs after all those before it, so the work grows with the square of their number: 32 of one account on one chain took 12 times as long as each alone); past them, each is simulated alone. An operation run after others it doesn't need (it needs only its account's earlier ones under the same nonce key: MEE gives each operation a key of its own) can be submitted without them by anyone holding the signature, so it is simulated so too (alone, or after only the ones it needs), and where it then sends more, that is a **danger** (`mee_operation_alone_differs`). One whose calls fail where operations on other chains may run first (a bridge), or where it was simulated alone past a chain's first 8, is a **caution** that they may change that (`mee_operation_may_depend`). An operation with time bounds shows them ("Valid from", "Valid until"): a **caution** when it can't run yet (simulated on the state now) or has no end (`upperBoundTimestamp` 0), an **info** when it has ended (K1MeeValidator refuses it) or lasts more than a day. An operation that can't be hashed (a chain ID that isn't positive, a field past its type, time bounds past K1MeeValidator's uint48) is a malformed one, the **danger** mismatch below; such operations used to fail the preview (HTTP 500), which the interceptor turned into a "Preview unavailable" **caution**, `eth_sign`'s danger included. Only K1MeeValidator takes the root's signature in that form: a session's operation, or a Nexus account's whose own (default) validator it is, the stand-in then carrying the operation's time bounds too, which the validator returns as the operation's validity (above); any other (an account that checks the operation's hash) is shown with the account not accepting the stand-in, which there isn't tried over any other digest, as it is for a signature of an operation's own hash (tried so, a first version showed such an operation validating, which the root's signature can't validate). A wallet that holds only some of them can give each its `proof` (the hashes that take its leaf to the root): those are previewed the same way, with a **danger** that the hash covers operations that aren't shown (`mee_supertransaction_partial`). Operations that neither make up the root nor carry proofs that reach it are a **danger** mismatch (`mee_supertransaction_mismatch`), and nothing is simulated. `eth_sign` of the hash, which K1MeeValidator also takes, is previewed the same way. A signature in an operation is left out: the key's over the root doesn't exist yet.

MEE 3 (Biconomy's K1MeeValidator 1.1.0, the default validator of Nexus 1.3.1; both verified on Sourcify) has the key sign the supertransaction as EIP-712 typed data instead (`eth_signTypedData_v4`): primary type `SuperTx`, either `SuperTx(MeeUserOp[] meeUserOps)` or a struct whose fields are items of any struct type, each operation a `MeeUserOp(bytes32 userOpHash, uint256 lowerBoundTimestamp, uint256 upperBoundTimestamp)`, under a domain of the account's name alone (`EIP712Domain(string name)`, `"Nexus"`: no chain, version or account, which the validator rebuilds from the account's `eip712Domain()`). Each operation carries the outer type's hash, every item's hash and its own index among them, its time bounds packed in one word, and the key's signature (`0x177eee00`, then those, laid out as a real operation on Base has them). The validator checks that its own item (its hash and time bounds) is the one at its index, rebuilds the digest from the items and checks the owner's signature over it, then returns the bounds as the operation's validity. The typed data shows only the operations' hashes, so their operations come from the wallet as above (`context.meeOperations`; their time bounds are the items'): each one whose hash is an item is previewed whole on its chain, the stand-in in that layout with the supertransaction's items; an item no supplied operation matches is listed by its hash with the same `mee_supertransaction_partial` danger, as is an item that isn't an operation (its type named; this preview doesn't read it), and a supplied operation that isn't an item is said not to be previewed (`mee_operation_not_in_supertransaction`). The preview also notes that the domain binds no chain or account (`mee_supertransaction_domain`): one signature serves every account of the signer's named so, on every chain, each operation's hash binding it to its account, chain and EntryPoint; and a domain named other than `Nexus` is said not to be one a Nexus account takes. Its digest is checked against what the validator rebuilds before anything is previewed as a supertransaction: typed data that doesn't hash so (a domain with a chain, say) is previewed as any other. An item's time bounds are shown as the validator takes them, each cut to 48 bits (signed beyond that, the item's numbers aren't when it runs: `mee_bounds_truncated`; beyond the 128 bits its signature carries each in, it can never be found among the items: `mee_bounds_unusable`). An operation that is more than one item (other bounds) can be submitted under any of them: it is simulated under one valid now where there is one, and the preview lists each (`mee_operation_repeated`). A supplied operation that isn't previewed is named by its place in `context.meeOperations`, with why (not an item, the same operation as another supplied, or one whose hash can't be computed here: another EntryPoint than v0.6 or v0.7, malformed). A `SuperTx` with no operation among its items is previewed as any typed data. (These from an independent review of the first version, which took bounds whole, so an item of 2^48 + now was shown as not runnable for millennia where it ran at once.) In `test/nexus-mee3.test.ts`, on the real code of Nexus 1.3.1 and the validator (from Base), with an EIP-7702 account delegating to Nexus: the stand-in validates, a real signature over the typed data in the same layout validates for the same gas (to within the replaced `ecrecover`'s forwarding), and simple mode costs 4,589 gas more than the plain form with one item, about 190 more for each further one. Replayed against their receipts on Base, the two operations that had refused the plain stand-in validated, 0.017% under in gas. `npm run accuracy:4337` replays such an operation as the typed data its owner signed, where it is the supertransaction's only item (an array of one; with more, the other items are only hashes in its signature, so it is skipped).

### Kernel session keys (Enable signatures)

A dApp that wants to act for a ZeroDev Kernel v3 account without asking each time has the account's owner sign an `Enable` message (EIP-712, domain `Kernel`, its version the account's Kernel and `verifyingContract` the account). It names what to install: a validator (`validationId` 0x01 and the validator's address) or a permission (0x02 and a 4-byte ID: policies, then a signer, each with a flag that can leave out operations or signatures), the account function it may be used for (`selectorData`, nearly always `execute`), an execution hook, and the account's validation nonce. Nothing happens when it is signed. The key's holder sends it later inside its first operation (enable mode: the nonce's first byte is 0x01), and Kernel checks it against the owner (the account's root validator), installs what it names and runs that operation (simulated whole in a preview, with stand-ins for both signatures: [above](#useroperations-simulated-as-the-entrypoint-runs-them)). From then on the key sends operations from the account within its policies, and signs messages as the account (ERC-1271) unless a flag or a policy stops it. The format is Kernel's own source (v3.0 to v3.3, verified on Sourcify: `ENABLE_TYPE_HASH`, `_enableMode`, `_installPermission`); the modules' data, ZeroDev's SDK (`@zerodev/sdk`, `@zerodev/permissions`) and each module's verified source.
- **Who gets it**: an ordinary key (ZeroDev's ECDSA signer and validator, its multi-chain validator), a passkey (ZeroDev's WebAuthn signers and validators, by public key), guardians and their weights (ZeroDev's weighted ECDSA validator), or a key Bleap's cosigner countersigns. Any other signer or validator is named by its address, as unknown.
- **What it may do**, policy by policy: ZeroDev's sudo policy (no limits); its call policy, 0.0.1 to 0.0.5 (each allowed call: the function, named from the target's verified ABI or the built-in list, on which contract or, from 0.0.2, on any contract (0.0.1 matches target 0 and selector 0 as they are: the zero address, and data starting with four zero bytes), its argument rules as "to must be 0x1c57…0a52, amount must be at most 10 USDT", the value per call, and delegatecall); its timestamp (from, until), gas (in total, and the paymaster), rate-limit (with and without reset) and signature-caller (which contracts may check its signatures) policies; Bleap's ERC20TransferPolicyV2 (token transfers to one address). A policy flagged for operations only or for signatures only is read as such. An unknown policy or hook is named by its address, and no limit is claimed for it: it can only refuse more, so what the known policies allow is the most the key can do, and with no known policy limiting its calls the grant reads as unlimited ("…unless the policy 0x35a6…ff28, which this previewer can't read, stops it"). A rule on a word past a function's arguments is a caution, since the function ignores the data it checks. Long lists are cut short with how many more there are: the first 24 calls, guardians and policies, 8 rules, values and signature callers, and 24 token lookups.
- **Danger** when it can send any transaction from the account: a sudo policy (with a time limit or not), no policy that limits its calls, a validator for `execute` with no hook, or only policies and hooks this previewer can't read. Also **danger**: an allowed call that is a delegatecall; a call to the account's own `execute` or `executeFromExecutor` (the call policy checks only that outer call, not the calls packed inside it) or to its admin functions (`installModule`, `installValidations`, `changeRootValidator`, `upgradeTo`, `grantAccess`), on the account itself or on any contract (target 0, which takes in the account); `executeUserOp` as the function it may use (Kernel delegatecalls the account with the rest of the operation, the EntryPoint its caller: any of its functions); and a handler it installs for a function of the account that runs by delegatecall (its code runs as the account), runs by call (Kernel also makes it an executor when its own `isModuleType(2)` says so) or that anyone can call (a selector hook other than 0, address 1 being none; or any handler on Kernel v3.0, which installs hook 0 as none, where v3.1 and later let only the EntryPoint through). ZeroDev's recovery action, whose source isn't published, is read from its code: one function, `doRecovery(validator, data)`, which uninstalls the account from a validator and installs it again with new data, so, run as the account, it can replace the owner. **Caution** otherwise ("Whoever holds session key 0x2c5f…e581 can make only these calls from your account: transfer on USDC … until 2026-11-12T21:26:45Z, without asking you again"), with a caution of its own when it can also sign as the account: most can (ZeroDev's SDK leaves the flag that would stop it off), and their call and time limits don't apply to signatures, so a token permit or a Permit2 signature made with the key would move tokens whatever its calls are limited to. Information for its time window.
- And **danger**, from a second review of the preview: a message whose contents can't be read (whoever made it chooses what can't be read, and Kernel installs what it reads), an allowed call to a module's `onInstall` or `onUninstall` (modules keep each account's settings under its address, so the account calling them on its own validator or signer replaces its key), `uninstallModule` as its function or call (a hook-type uninstall reaches the root validator's `onUninstall`, and a validator-type one a root permission's signer's, deleting the owner's key), `uninstallValidation` likewise unless the account's owner is read as a validator (Kernel won't uninstall the root validation itself, but calls `onUninstall` on any other module named as a validator, a root permission's signer included, with the data given: the owner's key is deleted), and `doRecovery` read without an RPC (accounts with guardian recovery hand it to ZeroDev's recovery action). A rule value no word of its type has (an address with upper bytes, a bool of 2) is shown as such, not as the value it looks like. Both uninstalls were shown live on Kernel v3.1, v3.2 and v3.3 (Base and BNB Chain, real accounts with the owner's key overridden to a test key): a session key granted the function, by its own operation, deleted the owner's key, and the owner's next operation failed (AA24), with a root validator (ZeroDev's ECDSA validator, by `uninstallModule`'s hook type) and a root permission (Bleap's cosigner on Base, and ZeroDev's ECDSA signer, by `uninstallValidation` or `uninstallModule`'s validator type, the call made by the key or by the account to itself); `uninstallValidation` of the root itself, or of a root validator's module, and `uninstallModule`'s signer type with the root permission's id, were refused (`RootValidatorCannotBeRemoved`). `invalidateNonce` stays a caution: it revoked the key itself (its next operation refused, `InvalidNonce`), while the owner's operations still ran.
