Without an RPC, a grant of `uninstallValidation` is a danger, the owner unread (the passkey account's among the fixtures, whose owner is a validator: a caution with one). The two reviews found 20 ways the first version showed a takeover as less than danger, each fixed; the 189 real grants are, with an RPC, 53 danger and 136 caution (37 danger before), and none lower than before.
- Also flagged: a function that isn't Kernel's own with no handler in the message (whatever handler the account already has for it runs: a caution, and with an RPC the account's `selectorConfig` is read, and a handler there that runs by delegatecall, the recovery action, or an executor run by call, is a danger), an unknown hook, plain transfers to any address with a value limit (per call, with no limit on how many), a signer flagged for neither operations nor signatures (it can do nothing), chain ID 0 (Kernel v3.2 and later accept that on every chain), a type or domain that isn't Kernel's. With an RPC for the chain: whether the account runs Kernel v3 (a caution if it doesn't), its owner (a caution if someone else is asked to sign), and its validation nonce (information when the signature can't be used as signed; a validation already installed is refused again by v3.0 to v3.2 at the same nonce, and v3.3 takes it at the next, but ZeroDev's validators, signers and policies, all but its sudo policy, refuse a second install). A message that doesn't decode is still described, cautiously, as a Kernel grant whose contents can't be read.
- Checked live on 2026-11-13 against what owners actually signed. Among the EntryPoint v0.7's operations, 1,204 were in enable mode: 285 in 420,000 blocks on Base, 595 in 1,500,000 on Arbitrum One, 243 in 600,000 on BNB Chain, 57 in 300,000 on Polygon and 24 in 10,000 on Ethereum (the public node refuses older logs). From up to two per validation and chain, 189 Enable messages of 143 accounts were rebuilt from their operations' signatures (121 Kernel v3.1, 4 v3.2, 64 v3.3, 23 of them EIP-7702 accounts; 37 sudo grants, 4 validators). For 183, the preview's digest is the one the owner signed: the owner's ECDSA signature of it (57, ZeroDev's ECDSA validator), the EIP-7702 account's own key (23), a passkey's assertion with the digest as its challenge, verified against the passkey's key (86; for one account the operation itself replaced the key, and its earlier one, from the validator's `WebAuthnRegistered` event, verified), a leaf of the Merkle root the owner signed (11, ZeroDev's multi-chain validator), or Bleap's owner and cosigner (6). The other 6 have a root validator whose source isn't published. All 189 decode, none as unreadable. Previewed with an RPC, 53 are a danger (without one, 87: the 34 that grant `uninstallValidation`, whose owner can't be read then, are a danger too): the 37 sudo grants; 8 whose only policy and hook are ones the previewer doesn't know (their verified sources, NoSelfCallPolicy and NoSelfCallHook, refuse calls to the account itself, its modules and delegatecalls, and nothing else, so the key can move everything the account holds); 4 guardian validators installing ZeroDev's recovery action; and 4 installing a handler of their own run by delegatecall. The other 136 are cautions. Nine of them, one of each kind, are the test fixtures (`test/fixtures/kernel-enable.json`), each with what proves its digest.

### MetaMask delegations (ERC-7710)

MetaMask's smart accounts, and EOAs delegating to its EIP-7702 account, hand out permissions by signing a `Delegation` (EIP-712, domain `DelegationManager` version 1, `verifyingContract` the DelegationManager 1.3.0 at `0xdb9b…7db3`, the same address on every supported chain but HyperEVM). It names a delegate (or `ANY_DELEGATE`, 0xa11: whoever holds the signature), the account (the delegator), its authority (the account's own, or a delegation it holds that it passes on), caveats and a salt. Nothing happens when it is signed. The delegate later redeems it through the DelegationManager, which has the account make calls of the delegate's choosing, each checked by the caveats' enforcers. The preview reads:
- **Each caveat**, by its enforcer: all 37 of 1.3.0's deployment (its published list of addresses; each verified on Sourcify and read for the layout of its terms, except three MultiOperationIncreaseBalance enforcers that aren't on Sourcify, whose own `getTermsInfo` was called on Ethereum instead), plus an earlier ERC20StreamingEnforcer still in use. "AllowedTargetsEnforcer: calls only to 0xa0b8…eb48", "ERC20TransferAmountEnforcer: only transfer() calls on USDC, 100 USDC in all", "TimestampEnforcer: usable before 2026-11-18T21:28:22Z", "RedeemerEnforcer: redeemable only by …", the groups of a LogicalOrWrapperEnforcer (the redeemer picks one), and so on. An enforcer it doesn't know is named by its address, and the preview is marked partial.
- **Danger** when nothing restricts what is called: no caveats ("full control of the account"), or only caveats that limit amounts (value per call, a balance's change), when or how often it's used, or who redeems it. A LogicalOrWrapperEnforcer restricts calls only if every one of its groups does. Also **danger**: `ANY_DELEGATE` without exact calls or a RedeemerEnforcer. **Caution**: `ANY_DELEGATE` for exact calls, no expiry (no TimestampEnforcer or BlockNumberEnforcer end), an unknown enforcer, caveats that limit which functions are called but not on which contract (AllowedMethods, AllowedCalldata or exact calldata alone: `transfer` on every token the account holds), exact calls that nothing limits to one redemption (they can be made again and again), and authorized calls that would fail now. Exact calldata (ExactCalldataEnforcer and its batch form) is said to leave the target and value open. Information: a delegation to the account itself (only it can redeem it, and it can already make any call), one passed on, one already expired, and a signer who is an owner of the account rather than the account.
- **Exact calls simulated**: an ExactExecutionEnforcer, ExactExecutionBatchEnforcer or SpecificActionERC20TransferBatchEnforcer fixes the calls, so they are simulated as the account would make them, in order. Their asset changes, decoding and warnings join the preview ("Let anyone make one exact call from 0x99f8…8559: Send 200 USDT"), without the transaction's own fee and nonce checks, which are the redeemer's.
- **What it can move now**: for a token cap (ERC20TransferAmount, ERC20Streaming's most, ERC20PeriodTransfer's and MultiTokenPeriod's amount per period), the cap against the account's balance, as for permits.
- Checked on 2026-11-19 against 220 real delegations redeemed on Ethereum, Arbitrum One, BNB Chain, Base and Sepolia (`RedeemedDelegation` events). Each signature recovers, over the digest computed here, to its delegator (217, EIP-7702 accounts) or to the owner of a HybridDeleGator (3, Base). Every caveat was read. Most were exact calls usable once by a few named redeemers, or to anyone (deposits to Relay, token transfers), or delegations to the account itself; a few were full control.

### Safe transactions, simulated through execTransaction

A Safe owner's signature (`SafeTx`) and an `execTransaction` transaction are simulated the way the Safe runs them: `execTransaction` itself, so the Safe's own logic decides (guards, the gas refund, delegatecalls of any contract, MultiSend batches) instead of each call being run from the Safe's address. A signature doesn't exist yet when previewing, so the simulation gives the signing owner (or, if the signer isn't an owner, the first owner) the same ECDSA stand-in UserOperations get: a 65-byte signature that the replaced `ecrecover` attributes to them over the Safe transaction's hash, so the Safe's own signature check runs and costs what it will. The other owners the threshold needs (the lowest ones) approve the hash on-chain (Safe's `approvedHashes`, the approval its signature type 1 accepts, set with a storage override). Owners and threshold stay as they are, and the transaction is sent from the signing owner. An `execTransaction` transaction runs as sent, with its own signatures.
- In the same simulated block, the Safe's configuration is read again (owners, threshold, singleton, guard, fallback handler, modules) and compared with now. What a delegatecall does to the Safe then shows even when its calldata looks harmless: a singleton that isn't one of Safe's official ones is **danger** ("After this transaction the Safe runs the code at 0x…, which isn't one of Safe's own", the Bybit outcome); an official one (1.1.1 to 1.5.0, from safe-global/safe-deployments) is an upgrade, a **caution**; added or removed owners, a new threshold, guard or fallback handler are cautions and an enabled module is **danger**, unless the decoded calldata already says so.
- A batch whose later call runs a transaction on another Safe (one the batch funds, or deploys first): that call's own decoding simulates the transaction alone, without the calls before it. Where it fails there but the batch, run in order, succeeds (for a `SafeOp` run whole, with its calls succeeding) and the other Safe logs `ExecutionSuccess` in that run (and no Safe in it logs a failure: a Safe further down whose transaction fails without reverting leaves the ones above it succeeding), the preview says so in a note instead of warning that it fails. That a batch succeeds doesn't say it alone: `execTransaction` can fail without reverting, logging `ExecutionFailure` (Safe 1.1.1 and 1.2.0 whenever its call fails; from 1.3.0, which reverts with GS013 otherwise, when it has a `safeTxGas` or a gas price). Where the run reports no logs, or the Safe logs neither (Safe 1.0.0), the warning stays.
- After a takeover like Bybit's, the Safe no longer answers as a Safe (`getOwners` and `getStorageAt` are gone with its code), so its singleton is read with `masterCopy()`, which the proxy answers itself from slot 0; the rest of the configuration is then unreadable, and only the singleton is compared.
- Checked on 30 recent `execTransaction` calls on Ethereum, rebuilt as the SafeTx their owners signed and simulated at the block before they ran (without their signatures): all 30 outcomes match the receipts, all 15 that moved tokens move exactly the receipts' amounts for the Safe, and none reported a configuration change. On a real Safe 1.3.0, a delegatecall to Safe's SafeMigration reports "The Safe's code changes to Safe 1.5.0" (and "1.5.0 (L2)" for `migrateL2Singleton`).
- The Bybit theft, staged live on mainnet: an owner of a real Safe 1.3.0 (`0xf930…3063`) asked to sign a SafeTx that delegatecalls the contract used in the theft (`0x9622…7242`, still deployed) with the theft's own calldata, `transfer(0xbdd0…9516, 0)`, which writes the address into the Safe's slot 0. Besides the static delegatecall **danger**, the simulation reports "After this transaction the Safe runs the code at 0xbdd077f651ebe7f7b3ce16fe5f2b025be2969516, which isn't one of Safe's own (the Safe no longer even answers as a Safe)" as **danger**. `test/safe.test.ts` has the offline version.

### Can the transaction be included, and what does it cost

Simulation runs the call; the network also checks the transaction itself before including it, and `eth_simulateV1` runs with validation off. With an RPC for the chain, each `eth_sendTransaction` / `eth_signTransaction` preview reads the account's nonces, balance and the latest base fee, and adds:
- **Nonce**: **danger** when the request's nonce is already used by a mined transaction; **caution** when it belongs to a pending one (it replaces it, a speed-up or cancel); information when it leaves a gap, or when the account has pending transactions the preview doesn't include.
- **Fee cap**: **caution** when `maxFeePerGas` (or `gasPrice`) is below the current base fee, so the transaction waits.
- **Gas cap**: **danger** when the gas limit is above the most one transaction may have (16,777,216, EIP-7825) on a chain that refuses more: Ethereum, BNB Chain, Base and OP Mainnet. Measured on 2026-11-11 in what each chain mines: on Ethereum and BNB Chain the highest gas limit in 40 blocks was exactly that, and BNB Chain's node refuses a higher one; on Base and OP Mainnet none of 27,555 transactions in 500 blocks was above it. Arbitrum One and Polygon mine 2-3% of their transactions above it and aren't checked. From Glamsterdam on, what a limit holds above it pays for new state, so a chain whose blocks are Amsterdam's (Sepolia now) isn't either.
- **HyperEVM's two kinds of block**: small ones every second, of 3,000,000 gas (118 of 120 blocks on 2026-11-11), and big ones every minute, of 30,000,000; an account's transactions all go into one kind, which it chooses on HyperCore (`evmUserModify`; the node answers `eth_usingBigBlocks`). **Danger** when a transaction needs more than a small block holds (its gas limit, or the gas the simulation used) and its account is on small blocks: it won't be included until the account switches. On big blocks, an **info** note that it waits for the next one, and the network fee is at big blocks' own base fee (`eth_bigBlockGasPrice`).
- **Funds for gas**: **danger** when the balance doesn't cover the value plus gas limit × fee cap, which the network rejects; caution when it may not cover the estimated fee.
- **Network fee**: "≈ 0.000055 ETH (50,000 gas at 1.1 gwei); at most 0.00012 ETH": the simulated gas at the current base fee plus the tip (the request's, or `eth_maxPriorityFeePerGas`), capped at the fee cap. On Arbitrum One it is the same price, the tip included, on the L2 gas plus the gas ArbOS charges for the data posted to Ethereum (it used to be the base fee alone: Arbitrum took no tips, and now charges the tip a transaction names, up to its fee cap. On 2026-11-16, 49 of 77 user transactions in 25 blocks paid above the base fee, and their fees came to 1.41 times what the base fee alone gives; its node suggests no tip, so a request naming none is still priced at the base fee), from NodeInterface's `gasEstimateL1Component` (zero at the moment: it matched `gasUsedForL1` in 20 of 20 recent receipts). On Base and OP Mainnet (OP Stack) every transaction also pays an L1 data fee on top of its gas, often more than the gas itself on OP Mainnet: the preview adds it ("…, plus 0.0000006 ETH for the data posted to Ethereum"), from the GasPriceOracle predeploy's `getL1Fee` for the transaction serialized as the wallet will send it, unsigned (authorizations not signed yet get stand-in signatures of their size), and counts it in the most the transaction can cost and the funds it needs. Against receipts' `l1Fee` it was exact for transactions at the oracle's minimum size and otherwise a fixed 3 compressed bytes high (up to 2.3% on small transactions): the oracle allows 68 bytes for the signature, a little more than a real one compresses to.
- **Gas limit**: the request's own limit is passed to the simulator, so a limit too low shows as running out of gas, and the call is run again without it to say how much it needs ("out of gas: the gas limit (30,000) is below the 62,044 gas this call needs"). A node's "insufficient funds" or "intrinsic gas too low" is reported as the failure it is rather than as a simulator error.

**What a signature can move now.** A gasless approval or order moves nothing when it is signed, so it can't be simulated as a transaction, but what it would move if used now can be read. For EIP-2612 permits, Permit2 allowances and transfers, UniswapX orders, CoW orders and 1inch limit orders, the preview adds "Can move now": the amount the signature permits, capped by the signer's balance and, where a contract pulls the tokens on the signature's authority (Permit2, CoW's vault relayer, 1inch's router), by the ERC-20 allowance that contract already has. "5,000 USDC (your whole balance, and any you receive while the permission lasts)" for an unlimited permit, "nothing yet: Permit2 has no allowance for your USDT…" when the signature can't move anything until an approval exists. The signature's nonce is checked too: an EIP-2612 nonce behind the token's `nonces(owner)`, a Permit2 allowance nonce behind `allowance(owner, token, spender)`, or a one-time Permit2 nonce already set in `nonceBitmap` means it can't be used. Checked live on Ethereum with accounts that had just used Permit2 and CoW (CoW's relayer address read from GPv2Settlement's `vaultRelayer()`). Seaport orders get the same for each offered item: whether the offerer owns the NFT (or holds the ERC-1155 amount, or the ERC-20 balance) and has approved the operator that moves it (Seaport, or the order's conduit, resolved through Seaport's ConduitController), so a listing reads "AZUKI #7738 (you own it, and the order's conduit is approved)", "no: you don't own …" or "not yet: the order's conduit isn't approved…"; an order signed with a counter other than Seaport's `getCounter(offerer)` can't be filled (`incrementCounter` cancelled it). Checked live with a real Azuki owner.

**The price it pays.** Contracts read the price a transaction pays (`tx.gasprice`) and the base fee: a pool that taxes swaps by the transaction's priority fee (a DEX on Base adds this "MEV tax" to its swap fee), a refund paid at the gas price. A simulation at no price, which is how `eth_simulateV1` runs a call that names none, doesn't show them. So after the first run, the transaction runs again at the price it would pay now (the latest base fee plus its tip: the request's, or the node's suggestion; none on Arbitrum; up to its fee cap), with a gas limit (the request's, or twice what the first run used) the sender must be able to pay for at that price. If that changes the outcome, the sender's balance changes or its approvals, the priced run is the one shown, with a note: "What this transaction does depends on the fee it pays (a contract it calls reads the gas price): it was simulated at the price it would pay now, 0.0065 gwei (the base fee plus a 0.0015 gwei tip)." A node that refuses the priced run (a balance that can't cover its gas) leaves the first standing. An EIP-7702 transaction's price goes to the node as both fee cap and tip, which comes to the same: reth nodes (Ethereum's, Base's, OP Mainnet's and BNB Chain's public ones, and Sepolia's public endpoint when one of its reth backends answers: it is part reth, part Geth) build a call with an authorization list as a type-4 transaction, which has no `gasPrice`, and ran one priced with `gasPrice` alone at no price, so its sender paid no gas and was shown receiving its fee (a relayer's payout on Ethereum showed 0.000397 ETH for 0.000280; 2026-11-15). In replays of EIP-7702 transactions (`npm run accuracy -- --only-type 0x4`) 4 of 11 on Ethereum, 14 of 21 on Base and 2 of 4 on OP Mainnet had differed, each by exactly its fee (in a second sample, every one that seemed to match had gone unjudged, the node rate-limiting its balance reads), one of them 0.93% under on gas too (the price changes what it runs); priced so, none of the same blocks' transactions. It adds one simulation to a preview, 120–180 ms on the public RPCs of the six chains. A batch's calls (`wallet_sendCalls`) run again the same way, each with room over the gas it used, and the priced run is shown if it changes what any call does. So does a Safe's `execTransaction`, for a SafeTx being signed or one sent: a Safe's gas refund in the native token is paid at the lower of its own `gasPrice` and the transaction's, so at no price it showed nothing leaving the Safe. Checked live on Ethereum: a SafeTx sending 0.000001 ETH with a refund of up to 50 gwei showed 0.0000803 ETH leaving the Safe (the refund at the 1.33 gwei a transaction paid then), where it had shown 0.000001; a batch wrapping and sending WETH came out the same at both prices, so nothing was said. Whoever submits a signed SafeTx is taken to be able to pay for its gas (the owner standing in for them may hold nothing).

**Pending transactions.** A preview runs on the latest state, but the wallet's own transactions that aren't mined yet run first. Pass them as `context.pendingTransactions` (oldest first; `{ to, data, value }`) and the transaction is simulated after them, in order, in one block: a WETH transfer that fails on its own (no WETH yet) succeeds after a pending wrap, live on Ethereum. A pending transaction expected to fail is a caution.

## Preview JSON

```ts
{
  schema: 'signing-preview/1',
  method, kind: 'transaction' | 'batch' | 'typed_data' | 'message' | 'unsupported',
  network: { kind: 'evm', chainId, name } | { kind: 'hypercore', name, testnet } | null,
  title, summary,                       // plain-language headline and sentence
  details: [{ label, value, raw? }],    // raw = exact integer/hex when value is formatted
  assetChanges: [{ direction, account, amount, symbol, raw?, asset?, basis, standard?, tokenId?, usd?, usdSource?, chainId? }],   // chainId: a signature for operations on several chains
  approvals?: [{ kind, owner, spender, token, symbol?, allowance, raw?, tokenId?, expiration?, revoked, direct, basis }],
  warnings: [{ severity: 'info' | 'caution' | 'danger', code, message }],
  attention: 'none' | 'info' | 'caution' | 'danger',   // display hint, not a security verdict
  decoding: { status: 'full' | 'partial' | 'opaque', decoder, notes },
  simulation?: { status, source, gasUsed?, revertReason?, block? },   // block: the state it ran on, when one was named
  calls?: Preview[],                    // batches
  trace?: { source, agrees?, gasAgrees?, note?, calls: TraceEntry[], truncated?, totalCalls?, stateChanges? },   // with context.trace
  replay?: { hash, block, index, time, receipt: { status, gasUsed, fee? }, matches?, differences? },   // from POST /v1/replay
}
```

`assetChanges[].basis` says where each number comes from:
- `requested`: what the signed request asks for.
- `simulated_state`: balances read from the simulated state before and after the call (the default with a simulator).
- `simulated_events`: transfer events from `eth_simulateV1`, when the balances couldn't be read back.

None is a measured final balance. Fee-on-transfer or rebasing tokens, and state changes before inclusion, can differ.

`assetChanges[].account` is the account the change is to (for a UserOperation, the smart account, not the key signing for it), and `chainId` is set where one signature covers operations that can be on several chains (a supertransaction's or a multi-chain Safe root's, each change with its operation's chain, priced there, even when all are on one).

**Dollar values.** In live mode a change in the chain's native token or a built-in token carries `usd`, what that amount is worth now ("23,962.78"), and `usdSource`, where the price came from ("Chainlink ETH / USD"). A "Value now" detail sums them per direction: "out ≈ $7.54 (1 asset without a price not counted)".
- Prices are Chainlink's USD feeds, read with `eth_call` on the chain's own RPC (`latestRoundData()`), so no price service learns what is being previewed. On HyperEVM, which has no Chainlink feeds, they are Hyperliquid's mid prices (`allMids`) for HYPE, BTC, ETH and SOL, and USDC is taken as a dollar.
- Tokens are priced by address, never by the symbol a contract reports: a token calling itself USDC at another address gets no value. Wrapped and bridged forms take their asset's feed (WETH as ETH, cbBTC, WBTC and BTCB as BTC, USDC.e and USDbC as USDC, POL by the feed still named MATIC), so a depeg of the wrapper wouldn't show.
- A feed with no answer, or not updated for two days, gives no value (stablecoin feeds update daily unless the price moves). NFTs and every other token have none, and the sum says how many assets it left out.
- A reverted call's changes are the requested ones, valued the same way. Prices are kept for a minute and a preview waits at most 2.5 s for them; `SIGNING_PREVIEW_PRICES=off` turns them off, and `Dependencies.prices` takes any `PriceSource`.
- The 33 feed addresses (`CHAINLINK_FEEDS` in `src/prices.ts`) were each read on-chain on 2026-11-10: description, 8 decimals and a plausible answer. Live previews that day on Ethereum, Base, Polygon, BNB Chain and HyperEVM valued ETH, USDC, USDT, POL, BNB and HYPE transfers from those sources (11.894 ETH as $23,962.78), in the same 350–500 ms a preview took before.

NFT changes carry `standard` (`erc721`/`erc1155`) and the full `tokenId`. `symbol` reads like "BAYC #42", with long IDs shortened. Collection names come from the contracts and are easy to copy, so a received, named NFT gets an info note.

`approvals` lists the permissions the account holds **after** the transaction, one per token and spender, or per NFT:
- `kind` is `erc20`, `permit2`, `erc721` (one NFT) or `operator` (every NFT of a collection).
- `allowance` reads like "unlimited", "250 USDC", "BAYC #42", "all NFTs in the collection", "none" (used up or set to zero) or "revoked".
- `direct` is true when the approval is the call itself, such as `approve()`. Those are already flagged by their decoders, so only `direct: false` approvals add warnings.

Events alone can be stale: USDC, OpenZeppelin 5 tokens and Permit2 lower an allowance without an event when it's spent, and older OpenZeppelin tokens emit one on every spend. So `RpcSimulator` reads the real values back with a second simulation pass (`allowance`, Permit2 `allowance`, `isApprovedForAll`, `getApproved`) after each call, and `basis` is then `simulated_state`. A token without these view functions keeps the event value (`simulated_events`). Permit2 events are only accepted from the canonical Permit2 contract.

BNB Chain's public node (reth-bsc) splits the block's gas evenly among the calls that set none: 22 such calls got 3.2 million each of a 70 million block. (The others give each call what the block has left, capped at 50 million on Ethereum and Sepolia and 32 million on Arbitrum: measured on all seven, among them Ethereum's, Base's and OP Mainnet's on reth.) The seventy-odd reads around a swap then got about a million each, and Open Genesis on BNB Chain works a holder's balance out for 1.37 million gas: its read failed, and its events stood, which mislead (the token "sends" the sender what it holds and the sender sends it all and one more, netting to one unit where the balance went down by 4.56e18). The scheduled replays found it. A read that fails for a token the logs show moving, and a second pass whose calls don't come out as the first did (a call that set no gas may have run short among the reads), are asked again in a lean pass: only those reads (and, where the second pass didn't come out, the native coin's and a whole UserOperation's EntryPoint deposit), with each call given its own gas (what it set, else what it used and half as much again and 100,000), and every call must come out as before, with the same gas used, or the events stand. The read pass runs on the block the first run had (`eth_simulateV1` says which it simulated): a request not pinned to a block ran on whichever was the latest, and on Base and BNB Chain the latest had moved on by two blocks between two requests sent back to back. A call that set no gas must also have used the same gas in the read pass itself: on BNB Chain's node it can succeed on its share among the reads and still have gone another way (an aggregator that catches a sub-route running out), and its reads would describe that way. The local EVM's reads (the fork simulator) had a million gas each and failed the same way; they now have ten million.

On OP Stack chains a simulated block's calls count against the block's DA footprint (Jovian), each as the least a transaction counts, 100 bytes at L1Block's `daFootprintGasScalar` (400 on OP Mainnet, 40,000 of its 40,000,000): a replay of a block of about a thousand transactions, with the reads after its own, was refused ("transaction DA footprint exceeds available block DA footprint"), and its amounts were the events' alone. Such a refusal is now asked again with the scalar set to 0 for the run (16 bits of L1Block's slot 8, its operator fee's fields kept, where the slot holds the scalar the contract reports at that block), which only lifts that limit: on OP Mainnet's node, 1,001 calls in one simulated block were refused, and ran with it (2026-11-17). A replay's block starts with its L1-attributes deposit (`setL1BlockValuesJovian`, whose calldata ends with the scalar), which wrote the scalar back before the calls after it, so the first day's scheduled replays still lost the reads of 71 of OP Mainnet's 282: the deposit's last two bytes are set to 0 as well. Then the reads before a transaction in a full block used gas its block didn't, which the node counts with the transaction's own limit against the block's ("Block gas limit exceeded by the block's transactions"): the read pass is asked again with the block's limit raised by 200,000 gas a read (only `GASLIMIT` reads it, and a call that ran otherwise than in the simulation has its reads asked again apart), and is given the earlier transactions on the gas the simulation fitted them to. In six minutes of OP Mainnet after, 120 of 120 replays had their reads, every one exact.

On Arbitrum One the reads between two calls go in one call, to a reader placed by state override that makes each read a static call on 10 million gas, as the local EVM's are: its public node answers an `eth_simulateV1` of more than 30 calls, counted across its blocks, with HTTP 429 every time, and the reads are three calls a token for one call (its balance before and after, and whether it is an NFT collection), so any call naming ten tokens or more (the four built in, those its calldata and its events name) lost them. A transfer naming twelve tokens more took 51 calls, was refused ten times in 19.6 seconds, and was shown from its events alone ("RPC HTTP 429"); now it takes 3, in 0.4 seconds. On 24 mined calls of recent blocks run at the block before theirs (2026-11-16), the outcome and every amount came out the same as from reads of their own; 7 of the 24 had lost their reads that way (36 to 66 calls, 19.4 seconds of refusals each) and now read them in 3.

### The call trace

With `context: { trace: true }`, a preview also carries what the transaction does inside: every call it makes, in order, and the state it changes.

```ts
trace: {
  source: "a local EVM over Ethereum's state at block 26385887",
  agrees: true,                         // the traced run did what the simulation shown did
  calls: [{
    depth,                              // 0 for the transaction's own call; a call's own calls follow it, one deeper
    type: 'call' | 'staticcall' | 'delegatecall' | 'callcode' | 'create' | 'create2',
    from, to, label?, fromLabel?,       // "you", a known token's symbol, a verified contract's name, a precompile's
    text,                               // the call in a line: "USDC.transfer(0x0000000a…, 12711206443)"
    function?, args?, returns?,         // [{ name?, type, value }], decoded
    named?: 'verified' | 'standard' | 'signature',
    selector?, input?, output?,         // raw, when not decoded (cut when long)
    value?, gasUsed, error?,            // why a call failed; what it did is undone, the calls under it included
    events?: [{ index, address, label?, name?, args?, named?, topics, data }],
    node?,                              // the call ran on the chain's node (an Arbitrum Stylus program, or reaching one): why
  }],
  stateChanges: [{ address, label?, balance?: { before, after, change }, nonce?, code?, storage?: [{ slot, before, after }] }],
}
```

- **Where it runs.** No public RPC of the seven chains offers a tracing method (`debug_traceCall`, `trace_call`), so the trace is recorded in the local EVM (`ForkSimulator`: the chain's own rules over state fetched from the RPC as the call reads it). On chains with `eth_simulateV1` the simulation shown is still the node's; the trace is a second run of the same call. Both run in a named block: the chain's latest by number, and a header for the block after it (its time a block later, the same fee recipient and base fee), given to the node as block overrides and to the local EVM as its block, so that the two see the same state and the same block. `agrees` says whether the traced run had the same outcome and emitted the same events, in order, as the simulation shown; when it didn't, `note` says how, and the trace is a guide rather than a record. Checked on live traffic (2026-11-10, recent transactions re-sent from their senders at the latest block on Ethereum, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain): of 114 traced previews, 114 agreed. A trace took a median 1.1–2.2 s on the public RPCs, and up to 15 s, its budget, after which the preview comes without one.
  - A node that can't serve the named block (one that keeps seconds of state, or a backend a block behind) is asked again for its latest: asking for a trace never costs the simulation itself. The trace is then of a block a little earlier than the simulation shown, and its `note` says so. Arbitrum One's public RPC, which keeps about 30 seconds of state and rate-limits hard, is where this happens: of 24 previews there, all 24 were simulated and 23 traced (a median 11 s), 18 agreeing; the other five were transactions that had since been mined, failing on the node's latest block and succeeding on the earlier one traced. An RPC that keeps state longer doesn't have this.
- **Names.** In order of trust: the contract's verified source (Sourcify, then Etherscan if configured; asked for the twelve contracts called most, the code that runs, so a proxy's implementation); a built-in list of standard functions and events (ERC-20, ERC-721, ERC-1155, WETH, Uniswap v2 and v3 pools, Permit2); and the signature databases by selector alone (`named: 'signature'`: a guess, since different functions can share a selector; only spam-filtered candidates are used). What nothing names keeps its selector and raw data. Tokens are named from the built-in list and the token registry.
- **Events** are listed under the call whose code emitted them and numbered across the transaction. A delegatecall runs as its caller, so its events come from the caller's address. Events of a call that failed, or of calls under one, aren't listed: the chain undoes them.
- **State changes** are what the call left different, account by account: native balance, nonce, code (a contract created, an EIP-7702 delegation) and storage slots, each before and after. What a failed inner call wrote isn't among them. The gas payment isn't either: it is the network fee, shown apart.
- It covers transactions, each call of a batch (`calls[i].trace`), a UserOperation's whole run through the EntryPoint (validation, the calls, the paymaster) and a Safe's `execTransaction`. A trace has at most 400 calls listed (`truncated`, `totalCalls`).
- The demo page has a "trace" switch; in mock mode the unknown router's swap sample carries a canned trace.

### Replaying a mined transaction

`POST /v1/replay` with `{ chainId, hash, trace? }` (or `replayTransaction({ chainId, hash }, { trace }, deps)`) runs a mined transaction again **where it ran** and returns a preview of what it did: the decoded call, what moved, the approvals it left, its trace when asked for.

```bash
curl -s localhost:8787/v1/replay -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{ "chainId": 1, "hash": "0x…", "trace": true }'
```

- **Where it ran** means: on its parent block's state, after the transactions before it in its block (each with its own gas limit, price, access list, authorizations and blob hashes), in a block with its own header (time, fee recipient, base fee, randomness, blob base fee), at the price it paid. The chain's node runs the earlier transactions in the same `eth_simulateV1` request, and only the replayed one is reported.
- **From Glamsterdam on, nothing is run first.** A block of Amsterdam's carries its access list (EIP-7928): every balance, nonce, code and storage slot the block changed, each with the transaction that changed it. Where the node serves it (`eth_getBlockAccessListByBlockNumber`; Sepolia's does, about 60 to 300 KB a block in about 100 ms), the state the transaction met is read from it, exactly: each item's last change by the block's own opening calls or an earlier transaction, as overrides of the parent block's state, for the node's simulation and the local EVM alike. The note on the preview says so. A node without the method gets the earlier transactions run first, as before.
- **Checked against its receipt.** `replay.matches` is true when the run has the same outcome, the same events in order and the same gas; otherwise `replay.differences` says what differs and a caution warns that what is shown may not be what happened. The fee shown is the one paid (the gas at its price, an OP Stack chain's L1 data fee, a blob transaction's blob gas), and nothing is said about inclusion.
- **Its trace is checked against the chain's own record, from Glamsterdam on.** The block's access list has what each transaction changed. A replay's trace (the local EVM's run) is compared with the transaction's entry: every storage slot, balance, nonce and code, each as it stood after. `trace.stateAgrees` says whether they are the same, and the note says what differs when they aren't. Two things the list counts that a traced call doesn't are put back first: the fee (the sender's balance, and the tip in the fee recipient's) and the sender's nonce going up by one. On Sepolia, 2026-11-11: 40 of 40 replays through a block of 486 transactions agree, and three mined EIP-7702 transactions, one of them reverted (its authorization stays applied, and the trace shows that). The scheduled rules check of 2026-11-12 caught a regression here: 6 of 24 replays started a slot from the parent block's value, because prefetching what the node said a call touches read it over the block's earlier changes (and a simulation's storage overrides the same way). Fixed that day; then 72 of 72 Sepolia replays agreed with their receipts, the node's runs and the access list. The check found that a transaction's gas is bought before it runs: its sender's balance is lower by the gas limit at the price paid while it runs, and a contract there stores the balance it reads (the trace's slot was 0.0024 ETH high). The local EVM now takes that out before a run that has a gas limit and a price, and puts it back after, on every chain; and once a call of a batch has run, its fee is paid (the sender's balance) and its tip passed on, so the next call meets them as the next transaction would. The tip goes to the block's fee recipient, except on BNB Chain, which collects fees at its system address (`0xffff…fffe`) until the end of the block, and on Arbitrum, which pays accounts of its own (measured on each node with two priced calls in one simulated block; three programs in the rules check hold every chain to it).
- **The receipt decides between two runs.** Where the node's run isn't what the receipt says, the local EVM runs the transaction too, and if its run is what happened, that is the one shown (`simulation.source` says so, and a note says what differed). Two ways a node's simulation is known to go wrong are a block in its parent's second on BNB Chain and a gas limit over 16,777,216 from Glamsterdam on, both below. If neither run is what happened, the node's is shown with the differences.
- **Dollar values are as of its block** ("Value then"): the Chainlink feeds are read in that block's state. On HyperEVM, whose prices are the market's now, a replay has none.
- **The trace** is recorded in the local EVM, which starts from the state the earlier transactions leave rather than running them: each round of reads is one `eth_simulateV1` of up to four simulated blocks on the parent's state. The first runs the earlier transactions; the next two read balances and code through two small readers placed at unused addresses (the balance reader in a block of its own: BNB Chain's node splits a block's gas evenly among calls that set none, and beside 151 code readers the one reading 152 balances ran out); the last reads storage, each reader with gas of its own for its slots (at the chain's price of a cold read: on Polygon 5,460, where readers of 65 and 90 slots given 3,000 each ran out, 2 of 60 traces), by putting a reader in each contract's place (an override in a later block doesn't touch the calls of the first). A precompile is asked for none: the node runs it at its address whatever code is put there, and Ethereum's point-evaluation precompile answered the reader with an error, which left a rollup's blob transactions that call it without a trace (2 of 20 blob transactions in a run; both now traced, with the receipt's gas). Nonces have no opcode, so they are the parent block's, up by one for each earlier transaction the address sent and for each EIP-7702 authorization of its own that was good by then (BNB Chain's busiest sender delegates itself in several transactions of a block: counted by transactions alone, the trace of its second took the authorization for stale and was 12,500 gas off). Without this the local EVM had to run the whole block: a transaction 66th in its block took over a minute; wherever it is, it now takes a second or two, ten at most in the runs below.
- The demo page has a "Replay a mined transaction" box (live mode): a hash on the chain chosen, with the trace switch.
- **What isn't replayed:** the chain's own transactions (OP Stack deposits, ArbOS's, a BNB Chain validator's system calls, Polygon's state syncs), and a pending transaction (preview it as a request).
- **The node has to keep the parent block's state.** Most keep recent blocks only: PublicNode's endpoints answer `403 Archive requests require a personal token` after a few minutes, Arbitrum's public RPC after seconds. The preview then has no simulation, and says why. With an archive node the node's own run of an old transaction is the node's (right for its block); the trace's local EVM runs Ethereum's blocks under the fork of their time from the Merge on, and other chains' under today's rules, saying so (see [What the local EVM runs](#what-the-local-evm-runs-and-what-it-doesnt)). None of that could be checked here against an old block: no archive node was available. The readers use no `PUSH0`, so blocks from before Shanghai can be read.
- **HyperEVM's public RPC keeps none, and doesn't say so.** It answers every state read from its latest state, whatever block is named: by number or by hash, three blocks back or a million (a sender's nonce "before" its transaction's block came back already past that transaction at every depth tried). A replay there ran the transaction on today's state under its old block's header, and 0 of 12 matched their receipts. A nonce tells such a node from one that keeps to the block: an account that sent a later transaction (the replayed one itself; for a request previewed on a past block, one from the chain's latest blocks) can't already have that transaction behind it at the block asked for. Where it does, the preview has no simulation and says the node answers from its latest state; a node that serves HyperEVM's past blocks is replayed on as any other. One extra read a replay.
- **Arbitrum's node works out a transaction's L1 gas itself.** It takes its own estimate (70–126% of the receipt's) out of the gas limit before the call runs, as the chain took the receipt's, so a call that measures its gas left starts a few hundred gas off: a GMX keeper's execution fee (MultichainOrderRouter, 2026-11-18) paid its keeper an amount in WETH the receipt's didn't. Where a replay there doesn't come out as its receipt, it is run again on the node with its gas limit moved by what the node charged over the receipt's L1 gas (where that charge is between none and twice the receipt's), and shown if that run is the receipt's; else the local EVM, given the receipt's L1 gas, runs it, as before.
- **Base's blocks share seconds too, and its node doesn't mind.** Base makes about five blocks a second (32 of 40 shared their parent's second on 2026-11-11), and its node will simulate a block at its parent's time, as Arbitrum's does. A replay there runs at the block's own time, and a preview's next block is taken to be in the same second. (The replay had run such blocks a second later, as BNB Chain's node requires: 4 of 114 Base replays then differed from their receipts, by the gas of a write that happens once a second or by an event; at the block's own time, 114 of 114 match.)
- **BNB Chain's blocks share seconds.** Its blocks come several a second, and over half share their second with their parent (22 of 40 on 2026-11-11). Its node won't simulate a block that isn't later than the one before it, so the node runs such a block one second later than it was, and the preview says so. Most transactions come out the same. One that reads the time doesn't: its run on the node then differs from the receipt, and the local EVM, which takes any time, runs it at the block's own. If that run is what the receipt says, it is the one shown, and the preview says that instead. In a block of 73 transactions sharing its parent's second, 70 replayed: 70 match their receipts, 10 of them through the local EVM's run (before this, those 10 were reported as differing). A trace is of the run shown. The local EVM runs at the block's own time only in that case: the block's earlier transactions still run on the node a second late, and for a transaction that doesn't read the time itself, a run at their time is the better match (traced at the block's own time, 4 of 35 used different gas from their receipts: a pool that writes once a second wrote again). That left a transaction that reads the time and also follows one that wrote by it, where neither run is its receipt's (about one replay in a hundred). Before the local EVM's run, the node is now asked again at the right time: the second's earlier blocks (up to three) and this block's earlier transactions as one simulated block at the block's own time, on the state of the last block of the second before. Its NUMBER then reads that block's plus one, and the preview says so. Measured first on the node alone, over 337 transactions in such blocks on 2026-11-11: run a second late, 304 came out as their receipts; merged, 255 of the 260 that fit in one block (two earlier blocks of 50 million gas and more don't), among them every one the late run had wrong. Through the previewer afterwards: 155 of 155 replays in such blocks match their receipts (143 by the node's run a second late, 10 by the merged run, 2 by the local EVM's at the block's own time), and all 154 traces agree. (Running the second's transactions in the local EVM instead gives the receipt too, but fetching the state they touch took 20 to 54 seconds a replay.) The merged block is given its blocks' gas limits together: on one block's, the node refused a merge of 162 transactions from three blocks ("Block gas limit exceeded", 2026-11-18). On those three blocks' limits, BNB Chain's public node then refused it for its own budget ("Client adjustable limit reached", at 90,000,000 gas asked as at 210,000,000), so on that node such a transaction is shown as the run a second late, its first event (which logs the block's time) a second off; a node of one's own can run it.
- **BNB Chain's node adds up gas limits.** Its `eth_simulateV1` (reth-bsc's; Ethereum's, Base's and OP Mainnet's reth nodes don't) adds up the gas limits of a simulated block's calls and refuses when they pass the block's 70 million ("Block gas limit exceeded by the block's transactions"), and it takes no higher block limit. The chain counts the gas used, and BNB Chain's transactions are given several times what they use: in every one of 60 blocks followed, the limits passed 70 million somewhere in the block's last third, and no transaction after that point could be replayed (12 of 12 refused). When the node refuses such a transaction's replay, the block's receipts are read (once, and only then: a node that counts as the chain does is never asked for them), and the earlier transactions are asked again on less gas each: what each used, half as much again and 50,000, and an equal share of the room left, never more than its own limit. What less gas can change is a call running short, so every call that got less must have finished with room to spare (what it used and three eighths more, within what it was given: a call that runs short anywhere inside uses nearly all it has), or nothing is taken from the run. The local EVM's read of the state those transactions leave does the same. After it, 15 of 16 transactions past that point replayed, each matching its receipt, with 14 traces agreeing (the other's sender couldn't pay for it in a run a second late), and 12 of 12 in a later run; a run through all of each block: 104 of 104 match, 104 traces agree, gas the same in 99 of 99, none refused. Ethereum's and OP Mainnet's blocks pass their limits the same way (3 of 6 and 7 of 7 blocks followed), and their nodes run them as they are: 6 of 6 and 14 of 14 such replays match, and 8 of 8 on each once the receipts were read only on a refusal.
- **Checked live** (2026-11-10, public RPCs, transactions spread over a block a few blocks old, each with its trace): Ethereum 40 of 40 match their receipts, with 40 traces agreeing with the node's run (median 1.4 s, in blocks of 240–350 transactions); Base 38 of 38 (median 3.0 s); Polygon 40 of 40 (3.2 s); BNB Chain 40 of 40 (2.4 s); OP Mainnet 39 of 39 (2.5 s), 36 traced before the node stopped serving the block. In all, 197 of 197 replays matched their receipts and all 194 traces agreed. Arbitrum's blocks hold a transaction or two and its node keeps seconds of state: 3 of 3. A whole Ethereum block run as one batch (337 transactions) had every outcome, gas figure and event count equal to its receipts.
- What the runs found, and fixed: a **blob transaction** reads its blob hashes and the blob base fee (a rollup's batch poster checks both), so they are passed to the node (with EIP-1559 fees, the only way nodes take them) and to the local EVM, with the block's real blob base fee from `eth_feeHistory`. **Arbitrum's** header keeps the ArbOS version in the field other chains use for randomness: overriding it made the node run the block by the first version's rules, without `PUSH0`, so it is left alone there. **BNB Chain's** node refuses any simulated call that names blob hashes ("Transaction is not buildable", whatever fees go with them), so a block couldn't be replayed past a blob transaction in it: a node that answers so is asked again with such a transaction as the plain call it also is, at its price, and from then on at once (what it does to the chain's state is the same; only `BLOBHASH` reads nothing). **Polygon's** node logs every native transfer and fee payment from `0x…1010`, which no EVM run has: runs are compared without them. **Base** runs contracts natively behind the one-byte code `0xef`: its B20 tokens (Base's native token standard, since its upgrade of June 2026: a factory at `0xB20f…0000` makes tokens at `0xB200…` addresses, ERC-20 with roles, transfer policies, memos, pausing and permits, implemented in its node in Rust) and the two registries they consult (`0x8453…01`, which features are switched on; `0x8453…02`, the allow and block lists). The local EVM runs them from the Base Standard Library's Solidity references ([base/base-std](https://github.com/base/base-std), kept in `vendor/base-std` with its MIT licence: `MockB20Asset` and `MockB20Stablecoin` by the variant byte of the token's address, `MockPolicyRegistry`, `MockActivationRegistry`), compiled by `npm run build:b20` into `src/b20-reference.ts` and run on the chain's own storage. Base keeps them behaving like its Rust precompiles and storing state in the same slots, and they do: a token's name, symbol, total supply, balances, roles and policy slots are where the reference says on chain (the ERC-7201 namespace `base.b20`, at `0xc78b…4000`), and so are the registries'; called side by side with the live precompiles, the token answered the same on 168 of 188 calls and the registries on 359 of 372. It is base-std's `main` that matches, not its latest tag: v1.1.0's token takes a transfer to its own address, which the chain refuses, as `main`'s does (the registries' logic is `main`'s too). `main` is what Base runs from its upgrade of 2026-11-05 (block 53,556,127); before it, its tokens ran by v1.1.0's rules (from 2026-09-30) and v1.0.0's, so a block from before it isn't given the references, and a call to a B20 token there is refused as before. What still differed is patched where it is compiled (`scripts/build-b20.ts`): the factory's bootstrap privilege (open, in the reference, until a flag the chain never stores is set) is closed, a permit to spender 0 is refused, the references' own extra getters are gone, a call to no function reverts with its first four bytes, as the chain's does, the activation registry's admin is the chain's, and the policy registry's writes ask the activation registry whether it is switched on, and write their slots, as the chain's do (below). A permit's EIP-712 hashing is charged as the node charges it, keccak's price for the name, version, domain, struct and digest (a replay of a swap through a permit came out 346 gas low before; `DOMAIN_SEPARATOR()`, a bad signature, an expired one and a good one now cost what they do on the node), while hashing for a slot is never charged. Calldata a token's function can't decode reverts with the call's selector, as on the chain (the reference reverted empty, its only empty revert). A token keeps the chain's code, its marker: `EXTCODESIZE` reads 1 and `EXTCODECOPY` `0xef`, as on Base's node, and an account delegated to a token (EIP-7702) runs that marker as code, an undefined opcode that burns all its gas, as the node does; the reference runs only for a call at the token's own address. (As on the chain, the local EVM refuses a token or registry run as another contract's code, by DELEGATECALL, with `DelegateCallNotAllowed()`, and one sent value, whatever the function, with `NonPayable()`, charging either refusal nothing, as the node does.) Three things are handled outside the code. **Gas**: the node charges a token's call 100 and 6 a word of its input, then its storage reads and writes and its events (as an EVM's LOG) at an EVM's prices, the registry's storage it reads included, and nothing for computation; the local EVM gives the reference's call gas to spare and charges it exactly that, so a trace's gas is compared as any other's (before this, a transaction given just the gas it needed ran out locally, 2 of 66). A registry called itself is charged the same way (the chain also keeps the registries warm from a transaction's start, as precompiles; not their storage), a write to the policy registry its read of the activation registry's flag included (below), and a call whose charge passes the gas it was given runs out, as the node's does. **Calls**: the reference asks the policy registry with a call, and the policy registry's writes the activation registry, where the node's token or registry reads the other's storage itself, so those calls are left out of the trace. **Replays**: Base's node runs a B20 token whatever code an override puts in its place (`movePrecompileToAddress` too), so after a replay's earlier transactions its storage can't be read through a reader there (a preview's pending transactions run in the local EVM with the request, so what they do to a token is the run's own). Its balances, allowances, nonces and total supply are read through its own views, which the node runs after them (which one a slot is, the reference's own hashing of it says: an approval in the block before a swap spending it is seen). The rest is read as of the block before them, and where one of them changed the token by more than a transfer or an approval, or changed a registry (their events say so), the trace says it may not be what happened. **Creating a token.** The factory runs from its reference too (`MockB20Factory`, vendored with the rest). It writes a new token's state with two of Foundry's cheatcodes, `vm.etch` and `vm.store`, which the local EVM answers for the factory alone: the token's code becomes the chain's marker, and each store is a write to the token's own storage, priced as one. It is patched as the chain behaves:
  - it writes no `initialized` flag (a created token's slot for it is 0 on the chain), and answers `isB20Initialized` by whether the token exists;
  - an init call the token can't dispatch fails the creation with `InitCallFailed(i)`;
  - the token's bootstrap window is open to the factory's calls only while it creates the token (the local EVM marks it in the token's transient storage, from the factory's writing of the token's code to the end of its `createB20`), as the chain's factory, native code, calls a token only then: a transaction sent from the factory's own address is any caller's. In the window an init call skips its role's check (`mint`, `mintWithMemo`, `batchMint`, `burnBlocked`, the grants and revokes, `setRoleAdmin`, `pause` and `unpause`, the setters, the multiplier's functions and `announce`), but for `burn`, `burnWithMemo` and `seizeWithMemo`, which check it as the chain's do: such a creation fails `AccessControlUnauthorizedAccount` of the factory and `BURN_ROLE` or `SEIZE_ROLE`, after the pause check and before the function's own checks (a burn with no balance, a seize to the zero address), charged the role's read, and goes on to those checks once an earlier init call has granted the factory the role (and is refused again once one has revoked it). The reference let them through: a creation the node refuses succeeded here (56 to 25,787 gas apart) or failed for the balance or for the seize's own checks (up to 2,100 low), and those whose earlier init call had granted the role were 100 low, the warm read of the role skipped; patched. Measured on Base's node, each creation at its least gas and 1 under it (2026-11-15): 53 of 53 alike, from 30 (every role-gated function as an init call, an asset's and a stablecoin's, with the role, without it and with it revoked, paused, with no balance or too little, a seize that the exempt policy allows, each of `seizeWithMemo`'s arguments refused, `seize` without a memo, which the token hasn't, and inside `announce`), and every B20 set measured below again alike, as are Base's rules check (400 programs) and 27 of 27 B20 replays against their receipts.

  The node charges a creation its own way, measured beside the local EVM with each call's gas timed from a contract (a transaction's gas hides a cheap one under the calldata floor). A call to the factory costs 6 gas a word of input, and the factory is warm. It works out the token's address (42) once its arguments decode, before it checks the version and decimals (`getB20Address` is charged it too), and finding whether the token exists is an account read, warm or cold as EIP-2929 prices it (`isB20Initialized` the same). Writing the token costs 34,436 more for an asset and 32,236 for a stablecoin; a role it grants is charged one read of the role's flag, where the reference reads it twice; a stablecoin's currency is checked after all that (the reference is patched to check it after the existence check too, so that re-creating a token with a bad currency fails `TokenAlreadyExists`, as on the chain). In a static call the node's write fails, with no data; calldata short of a selector is echoed as four zero bytes, and parameters the reference can't allocate (`Panic(0x41)`) as the selector. With these, 141 calls of 20 kinds came out alike on the node and locally: assets and stablecoins, 6 or 18 decimals, names of 0 to 50 bytes, an admin or none, init calls that succeed, revert or can't be dispatched, and each way a creation fails. They agreed on outcome, return value, every event's data, gas, and what the new token answers afterwards (its name, symbol, decimals, supply, cap and roles). Eight early failures timed from a contract agreed too, as did two real creations replayed from the chain, their traces agreeing with the node's run and its gas. In a trace a creation is one call, with its events; the factory's inner calls are left out, as the chain's trace has none, and so is everything a token's reference calls (the registries, `ecrecover` for a permit, `announce`'s calls to itself). After a review on 2026-11-12 found 25 smaller differences (each confirmed on Base's node: the order of a stablecoin's checks, a warm token's existence check, a static creation, hashing a permit or domain separator called inside a creation or for a name of exactly 32 or 64 bytes, a permit whose signature verifies before it fails, role grants, the cheatcode address left warm, a precompile moved onto these addresses, a code override at them), each was fixed as the node does it. Measured again on its node after the fixes, 23 of the 25 agreed; the rest, and twelve more found by that re-measurement, were corrected the same day: a token function's own correction (`setRoleAdmin`, `announce`, `renounceLastAdmin`, `updateMultiplier`, `cancelUIMultiplierUpdate`, `batchMint`) is charged as it runs (at its first write, or as each recipient or event it stands for is reached), within the gas the call was given, so one given a little less than its charge runs out as on the node (and an `announce` whose calls fail after its id is written is charged as one that succeeds); what depends on how a call ends (a pending multiplier update cleared, `setRoleAdmin` on a token without admins) as it ends; an asset's multiplier is warm after its creation; a token moved away by a state override is ignored, as the node ignores it, and a registry a token calls stays the registry whatever precompile was moved onto its address. 21 more rows, gas windows included, then agreed on the node. An init call that grants the default admin role is refused on a token without admins and reads the admin count first, as on the node (the reference is patched: the factory's own grant of the initial admin goes a way of its own, once); every such row then agreed on the node, the three real creations replayed included. A view that computes with a pending multiplier update (`multiplier`, `toUIAmount` and seven more, and `newUIMultiplier`) is charged the node's second read of it, and `fromUIAmount` reads the multiplier before it overflows, as the node does (2026-11-13: every view under each pending state, live, taken effect or none, and in init calls and `announce`, alike on the node). A write given 2,300 gas or less runs out as on the node (EIP-2200's sentry, which the reference on spare gas never met: a second transfer, approve, grant or revoke in a transaction given just its charge ran out on the node and not here), a role's flag is charged its one read at its write, and transferFrom writes the allowance after its Transfer event, as the node does (2026-11-13: 118 of 120 rows, each the least gas a call needs and what it uses, alike on the node, from 62). The two rows left, and what measuring them found, are patched in the reference since, as the node does them: transferFrom and transferFromWithMemo check the allowance first and write it after the transfer and its Transfer event, so a static one fails at the balance's write with the policies and the balance read (it was 2,200 to 10,500 low, and said nothing where the node says the balance is short); `pause` and `unpause` decode their whole list before anything, so a feature out of range (4 or more) fails as calldata the token can't decode, the selector, with nothing read (it was 20,200 or more high, or refused for the role first), and they read and write the paused features once, whatever the list (a list of two given its least gas ran out here, at a write the node doesn't make). Measured on the node after, each call timed from a contract, at its least gas and 1 under it (2026-11-13): 147 of 147 rows alike, from 83 (static calls of each write, transferFrom's each way to fail, lists of features in and out of range, empty, repeated and unordered, with the role and without, inside `announce` and in init calls). A transaction sent from the factory's own address, which only a simulation can send, is any caller's on Base's node; the reference took it as the factory's, in its bootstrap window: a mint, burn, grant, revoke, pause or setter without the role went through here, refused on the node (`AccessControlUnauthorizedAccount`; a seize or `burnBlocked` failed here for its account instead), and so did a transfer under a policy that blocks the sender; a transfer the policies allow was 2,100 to 6,300 gas low (`transferFrom` up to 8,400, as many as the token's policy reads) and a function with its role 2,100 to 6,300, their reads skipped; and the reference's own `grantInitialAdmin`, which the chain's token hasn't, made anyone an admin. Sent to the cheatcodes' address, it wrote the token's storage or code, where the node finds an empty account; and to a registry with a precompile moved onto its address, it was given the registry, where the node runs the precompile (from a token's or a registry's address too). Now the window is open only while the factory's reference creates the token, the cheatcodes answer that reference alone, and a registry is kept for the calls a reference makes. Measured on Base's node, each a transaction from that address at its least gas and 1 under it (2026-11-13): 54 of 54 alike, from 9 (each role-gated function with the role and without, transfers under the token's policies and under one that blocks, `grantInitialAdmin`, the cheatcodes, creations sent from the factory's address, whose init calls run in the window, a contract put at its address by a code override, which is the factory on the node as here, and precompiles moved onto the registries), and every set above again alike. (The node runs a contract at the factory's address only with the factory moved away, which the local EVM refuses.) A call to a token address that has no token yet reverts empty, as on the chain. **The policy registry's writes** (its three creations, the admin's three functions and its three updates) read the activation registry's flag for it, `features[keccak256("base.policy_registry")]`, once their calldata decodes and before any check of their arguments, and are refused with `FeatureNotActivated(feature)` while it is off; its views don't read it. base-std's reference has no such gate (its own tests say the gate is the live precompile's alone): it is patched in, the flag read in the activation registry's storage through that registry's reference, so it is cold or warm as the node's read is (2,100 or 100). And the node writes a member's flag, a pending admin and the policy counter as whole slots, without reading them first, and reads the counter once, where the compiled reference read each before writing it, and the counter three times; patched so too. So a write was charged 2,100 too little cold and 100 warm, a creation 200 more than that (1,900 too little cold, 100 too much warm: the counter's two extra reads), a repeated account and `finalizeUpdateAdmin` 100 too much, a static `updateAllowlist` a cold read the node's write fails before, and `renounceAdmin` succeeded on 9,413 gas where the node needs 11,187 (its read of the pending admin fell before EIP-2200's sentry). A creation that writes the built-in policies (the counter under 2, which only a state override makes so on Base) is charged the node's marking the registry initialized, writing its code (200, a byte at a contract's code price), as the reference writes them: in a creation only (`finalizeUpdateAdmin` or `renounceAdmin` of policy 0 writes the same slot, for nothing more). And the node decodes the whole calldata first, so an array element out of its type's range (an account or a child policy id with its upper bytes set) is refused there, reading nothing, where the reference read the flag and the policies first, and created a policy before it met a bad account: patched to check every element first. Measured on Base's node after, each call timed from a contract at its least gas and 1 under it (2026-11-13): every write's success and each way it fails, static calls and calldata that doesn't decode, 55 rows each cold, warm and with the feature off, 12 sequences of calls in one transaction cold and warm, and creations with the counter at 0 and at 1, cold and warm: 217 of 217 rows alike (41 before); the token, factory and activation-registry rows measured beside them as before (transfers, approve, transferFrom, mint, static calls, creations: 18 of 18 alike, 17 before, the other the flag read again after a policy write, warm by then on the node). Those two found by a second agent checking the first's work, then measured the same way: an array element dirty in each of the five writes that take one, cold, warm and with the feature off, and `finalizeUpdateAdmin(0)`, 22 of 22 alike after (a dirty element 2,100 to 32,831 high before, and with the feature off `FeatureNotActivated` where the node answers the selector), and every set above again alike. And `isAuthorized` of a composite policy (UNION or INTERSECT, and so a token's transfer under one) was 300 high: the reference's index access read the children's length again for each child's bounds check and their shared slot again for the second, where the node reads each once, all before it asks the first child; patched to read them once (into memory), it agreed on two children and five over two slots, a short-circuit on the first, an inverted child and `compositePolicyChildIds` of five, cold and warm (13 of 13). A review the same day found what those rows missed, each confirmed on the node: the copy into memory read one slot past the last when the count was a multiple of four or none (four children are the most a write allows; a composite with none is one never created), so a token's transfer under a four-child UNION was 2,200 high (57,023 on the node, 59,223 here) and `isAuthorized` and `compositePolicyChildIds` of such a composite 2,100 (100 warm); the children are now read by a helper of the patch's, the length once and each slot of four ids once. `updateComposite` shrinking a list (four children to two or three) was 100 high, the reference clearing the old tail and then writing its slot again: it now writes each slot whole, the length, and zeros the slots past the new length (the node's write of the length gives it the old one, so the reference reads it after its first write, and a static `updateComposite` fails having read what the node's has: it was 2,100 high). And the node decodes a bool (`updateAllowlist`'s and `updateBlocklist`'s second argument, the only bool any of these functions takes) as any word whose upper 31 bytes are zero, true unless its last byte is 0: 2, 0x80 or 0xff is taken as true, the member written, and logged, as 1, where the reference's decoder takes 0 and 1 alone and refused them with the selector; a word with an upper byte set the node refuses as calldata it can't decode, the selector, reading nothing, as the reference does. The local EVM runs such a word as 1 (the trace's input is the call's as sent). Measured on the node after, each call timed from a contract at its least gas and 1 under it (2026-11-13): composites of 0 to 5 and 8 children, UNION and INTERSECT, each child asked or a short-circuit on the first, inverted children, one never created, asked, listed, written (4 to 2, 3 and the same four; 2, 3 and 1 to 4; 5 and 8 to 2; 0 to 2; created with 2, 3 and 4) and under a token's transfer, and bool words of 0, 1, 2, 0xff and with upper bytes set in both functions: 99 rows, alike cold and warm and the 79 registry rows with the feature off (46 alike before, cold), their events alike, and every set above again.

  The node's other new precompiles are refused. Replaying only Base transactions that reached a B20 token (`npm run replays -- --chain 8453 --logs-from 0xb2000000000000000000`) on 2026-11-12: 65 of 65 matching their receipts, every one traced, its trace agreeing with the node's run and its gas the same (33 of 33 in a run before; in a run before the gas was charged as the node charges, 64 of 66, the other two having run out of gas). Before, none of them could be traced (15 of 240 Base transactions in the scheduled replays of 2026-11-11 reached one).

### What the local EVM runs, and what it doesn't

The simulation a preview shows is the chain's own node's (`eth_simulateV1`) wherever the RPC has it, so it follows whatever rules the chain runs. The local EVM (every trace; the simulation itself where the RPC has no `eth_simulateV1`, as on HyperEVM) runs the rules it was checked against. It is not a complete implementation of every chain, and this is where it stops:

- **The rules it runs.** Osaka's opcodes and precompiles on Ethereum, Arbitrum One, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain, with each chain's measured differences; Cancun's on HyperEVM; Amsterdam's on blocks that are Amsterdam's (below). Measured on every node on 2026-11-11: none of the seven mainnets accepts Amsterdam's `SLOTNUM` opcode, a plain transfer costs 21,000 gas there, and Ethereum's node reports the fork active since 2026-01-07 with none scheduled.
- **Checked against each node** (`npm run rules`, and daily in CI). On every chain, about 1,300 small programs are run (1,325 on Ethereum, 1,252 on HyperEVM; 370 to 435 before the audit of 2026-11-16, below) on the node (`eth_simulateV1`, or `eth_call` where it has none) and in the local EVM, on the same named block, and must return the same bytes, emit the same events and use the same gas:
  - every opcode, after seventeen pushes (1 on top, then 2, 3, …), followed by code that returns the top of the stack and the memory size, so its result, its effect on the stack and its price all show;
  - 45 crafted programs for what that form can't reach: the previous block's hash, storage set, read warm and cleared (a refund), the same on gas limits that only hold if what a cleared slot gives back can be spent again, and across calls (a slot set in one call to a contract and cleared in the next, as an allowance is approved and then spent), transient storage, calls sent at a price (the sender's balance and the fee recipient's as the code reads them, the price itself), a creation by `CREATE` and `CREATE2`, calls with value to an account that exists and one that doesn't, `SELFDESTRUCT` with a balance and one that burns its balance as it is created, a creation of 25,000 bytes of code and one from 50,000 bytes of initcode (either side of the limits Amsterdam moves), Amsterdam's `DUPN`, `SWAPN` and `EXCHANGE`, what the code of an account that is delegated already (EIP-7702) reads as and calls into one (to a contract, twice, and to a precompile), a transfer, calldata;
  - each precompile with an input it accepts (a signature to recover, bn254 points and a pairing that holds, a KZG proof, BLS12-381 points and pairs in several counts, a P-256 signature made on the spot, `modexp` at three sizes) and each of `0x01`–`0x20`, `0x100`, `0x101` with none, called from a contract that reports the gas the call spent (called directly, a cheap precompile's price hides under the calldata floor);
  - five EIP-7702 authorizations, signed on the spot by keys made for the run: by an account that doesn't exist, one that does, one already delegated, one account twice, and one with the wrong nonce. What each costs depends on the fork (before Amsterdam 25,000 with 12,500 back for an account that exists; under it, the write and the state added). Not on HyperEVM, whose rules have no such transactions.
  - on Arbitrum One, 53 calls to its own precompiles: eleven reads (ArbSys's block number, chain id and version; ArbGasInfo's prices), which the chain answers for the local EVM and the local EVM prices; ArbSys's sends to L1, which the local EVM runs itself (below), with and without value, data and an argument, in a static and a delegate call and by callcode, their events compared too; ArbRetryableTx's `redeem`, `keepalive`, `cancel` and `getTimeout` of a ticket that doesn't exist; `0x73`, `0x74` and `ArbosActs`; thirteen calls ArbOS turns away for how they are made (below: a write in a static call or in a call made inside one, a delegate call, value to a method that isn't payable) and a pure method in a delegate call, which it runs; four errors that keep the rest of the gas, three with no data; calldata short of a ticket id to two of ArbRetryableTx's writes; `0x6a`, where Arbitrum One has no precompile, with value, in a delegate call and by callcode; and `ArbosTest.burnArbGas` of more than the call is given, in a call and in a static call.
  - on BNB Chain, 17 calls to its own precompiles (`0x64`–`0x69`), each timed from a contract: a BLS signature (one key, two aggregated, the wrong key, inputs it can't read) and a secp256k1 one (one that holds, one with `s` high, a byte too many), made on the spot by keys made for the run, and the light-block and double-sign checks and the two deprecated ones with inputs they can't read. Their first run (2026-11-15, on the code that answered them at a flat 2,000 gas) had 9 of the 17 differ: a BLS signature that holds cost 4,500 on the node with one key and 8,000 with two (2,000 locally), a secp256k1 one 3,000, and a BLS or double-sign check that reverts gave the rest of its gas back on the node where locally it took all of it. They are priced as the node prices them since (below), and all 17 agree.
  - on Base, 30 calls to its own contracts, which its node runs in Rust and the local EVM from their Solidity references, each timed from a contract: a live B20 token's reads and EIP-712 functions, calls that fail each way (no balance, to itself, a bad or expired permit, a function it lacks, calldata too short), value sent, its code as other contracts read it, an account delegated to it, the two registries (two writes to the policy registry among them), and creations through the factory (an asset with a mint, a stablecoin, decimals out of range, an init call the token lacks). So a change Base makes to them shows the next day. Their first run found the registries charged 100 too much when called themselves: like the factory, a registry costs 6 gas a word of its input and no more (`isAuthorized(0, …)` 18).
  - on Base, 35 calls to `NonceManager` and `TxContext` (below), each timed from a contract, and a transaction straight to each: a nonce read cold, warm and on another key, one stored and one too large for a `uint64` (given as state on both sides), one read after another transaction called the precompile, key 0, calldata each can't decode, a gas short of the price, value sent, run as another's code, `TxContext`'s three functions and the two of that type's it lacks. Before they ran locally all 35 were refused there; since, all 35 agree with the node, gas to the unit.
  - with each chain's own precompiles that a fork adds (BNB Chain's `MILLI_TIMESTAMP`), two calls to each: before the fork, an empty account on both sides.
