  - From an audit of 2026-11-16 that looked for what these didn't reach, on every chain's node, about 900 programs more a chain, in six families (`--family lifecycle`, `--only-context`, `--only-gas`, `--only-delegations`, `--only-edges`, `--only-combinations`): an account's life (creation onto an account that holds something, `SELFDESTRUCT` of a contract made before and in the same transaction, followed by the next transaction's reads), what the block gives a call (the history and beacon-roots contracts, the fee recipient's and fee vaults' balances after a priced call), gas at its boundaries (a call given exactly what it needs and a gas less, the calldata floor, a refund's peak), EIP-7702 delegations (to a delegation, to a precompile, cleared and set again, signatures that aren't valid), and opcodes' edges and combinations. Each audited difference was run again on a fresh block before anything was fixed. Of 18 confirmed, these are fixed and now in the check:
    - The calls a block starts with weren't made: EIP-2935's history held the hash of the block 8,191 before the parent's, and a read of EIP-4788's beacon root at the block's own time reverted (EigenLayer's `EigenPod.startCheckpoint` failed locally with `InvalidEIP4788Response` where the node's run succeeded at 71,644 gas). The local EVM now writes both before a block's first call, as the node's simulated block does: the parent's hash, and the root its node writes (zero on Ethereum and Sepolia, the parent's on OP Stack), or a replayed block's own. Replays give the node that root too (`beaconRoot`): an EigenPod checkpoint replayed on Ethereum read a zero root and used 71,644 gas against its receipt's 76,444, and now matches. Arbitrum's node writes neither in a simulated block, and the local EVM follows it there.
    - A contract created and destroyed in one transaction stayed (EIP-6780): the next call of a batch found its code, a `CREATE2` to its address collided (104,337 gas where the node's succeeded at 88,335), and the trace listed it as created. It is now removed at the transaction's end (under Amsterdam, EIP-8246, with its balance kept).
    - Fees went nowhere but the tip to `COINBASE`: OP Stack's `BaseFeeVault` is now paid the base fee, and Arbitrum One's fee accounts their parts (its infrastructure account the gas at ArbOS's minimum price, its network account the rest, the tip included, and the L1 pricer's pool the L1 gas where a replay's receipt says it), so a later call that reads them sees what the node's does.
    - Transactions the network refuses before they run, ran: a gas limit under the intrinsic gas (a transfer on 20,999 succeeded at 21,000) or under EIP-7623's floor, initcode over EIP-3860's limit, a creation with authorizations, a sender that can't pay for its gas at its price. They now fail without running, saying so, as the node's path says (on HyperEVM, where the local EVM is the simulator, the preview had said "success" for a transaction the chain refuses).
    - `ArbSys.arbBlockNumber()`, which the local EVM answers itself, answered on less gas than its price (802 against 803), where the node's call fails.
    - EIP-7702 authorizations a chain skips were applied: one with `s` over half the group order, or a parity of 27 or 28, delegated the account in the local EVM, the previews and the replays (code size 23, 81,362 gas, where the node skipped it: 49,127). None of 2,434 mined authorizations had either form.
    - Under Amsterdam (Sepolia): an authority's delegation bytes are charged once a transaction, against the account as the transaction found it (a list that cleared a delegation and set one again paid 35,190 twice); and the node path's stand-in for an authorization the wallet hasn't signed is corrected for what it can't reproduce (the 9,000 a write to another account costs, and the 23 bytes a first delegation does): 31,816 where the sender's own authorization takes 58,006 for a fresh account and 22,816 for one already delegated, now both the same as the sender's own.
    - ArbOS reads priced as their slots are: `getL1PricingSurplus` three, `getAllChainOwners` and `getBatchPosters` one and one an entry, `getPreferredAggregator`, `getLifetime`, `getCurrentRedeemer` and `getStats` none, ArbWasm's parameters 900, all 3 a word (each had been taken to read one). `ArbInfo.getBalance` and `getCode` are answered from the simulation's own state, at 1,500 and 2,900 and 3 a word of code: forwarded, they had answered with the chain's, without what the simulation changed. (`getL1PricingSurplus` and `getStats` still answer with the chain's latest block where the node answers for its simulated next one.)
    - HyperEVM's reads end at 0x…0814: 0x…0815 to 0x…083f are empty accounts on its node, cold, and had been warm forwarded reads here (2,622 gas against 1,122); in the check now.
    - A call given no gas limit has 30,000,000 in all, as HyperEVM's `eth_call` gives (it had 30,000,000 besides what it pays before its call). The other nodes give their own caps (32 to 160 million), which only code reading `gasleft()` sees.
    - Validity warnings for a priority fee above the fee cap, an empty authorization list, and a creation with authorizations, which every node refuses whatever the state; and an out-of-gas message that quotes what the call needs at its peak, before its refund (it had quoted what it used in the end: "below the 36,824 gas this call needs" for a call that ran out on 40,000 and needs 46,030).
  - Not fixed, from the same audit: under Amsterdam, a `CREATE` that fails fast (its creator at the nonce limit, or sending more than it holds) is charged the new account's state gas before the check, so inside a call given less than about 180,000 gas it runs out of gas where the node's pushes 0 (the library charges first; rare); a precompile moved by a state override (`movePrecompileToAddress`) is warm at both addresses here, where reth nodes leave the old address cold and geth-based ones the new one (the two clients disagree with each other, and the previewer's own stand-ins name both in their access list).
  - On 2026-11-11 all eight chains agreed on all of them, after the fixes below. Four differences are the node's way of answering and are listed as expected: Arbitrum's node takes its L1 data charge out of the gas a call is given (so `GAS` reads lower), and HyperEVM's `eth_call` answers on its latest block whatever block is named (`NUMBER`, the previous block's hash) and reports its large blocks' gas limit. On Arbitrum the node adds its L1 data charge to every call's gas, the same for calls with the same calldata in one block (119 or 120 gas for an empty call on the day; the block's first call pays about 6 more, so an uncompared call goes first): gas is compared there less that charge, for the programs sent no calldata.
  - What it found, beyond what replaying traffic had: **Polygon** returns one fixed address for `COINBASE` in every block (its headers name no miner, and it ignores an override of the fee recipient), has no `BLOBHASH` or `BLOBBASEFEE`, has an ordinary empty account where the point-evaluation precompile would be, and prices precompiles its own way: bn254 add 540 gas, multiply 12,600, pairing 67,500 and 51,000 a pair (Ethereum: 150, 6,000, 45,000, 34,000); `blake2f` 22 a round (1); BLS12-381 G1 add 1,050, G1 multiply 73,200, G2 add 1,620, G2 multiply 144,000, pairing 109,330 and 94,540 a pair, map to G1 15,400, map to G2 66,640 (375, 12,000, 600, 22,500, 37,700 and 32,600, 5,500, 23,800), with Ethereum's multi-scalar discounts; and a contract there may be 32,768 bytes long (24,576 on the other chains before Amsterdam), found when the 25,000-byte creation succeeded on its node and failed locally. **HyperEVM**, on Cancun's rules, follows an EIP-7702 delegation: a call into an account whose code is one runs the delegate's code (seen with such code given to an account; it mined no EIP-7702 transaction in 400 blocks). The local EVM does there too. And the authorization cases showed that **nodes built on go-ethereum refuse an authorization whose signature is written with leading zeros**, as wallets pad `r` and `s` (Arbitrum's and Polygon's nodes did): about one authorization in eight, and the whole simulation with it. Authorizations now go to nodes without them. **Arbitrum** has no `BLOBBASEFEE`, and its `BLOCKHASH` is a function of its own of the L1 block number (not that block's hash, nor the hash of the L2 block with that number): the local EVM now asks the chain for it. Its own precompiles charge 800 gas to open the chain's state, 800 for each storage slot a method reads and 3 a word given or returned (`ArbSys.arbBlockNumber()` 803, `ArbGasInfo.getPricesInWei()` 2,418), where the local EVM had charged a flat 2,000: a transaction asking for the block number came out 1,197 gas high locally (4 of 17 replayed against receipts; after it, 19 of 19 to the unit). The local EVM follows all of these; before, a zero-knowledge verifier's gas on Polygon came out several times too low.
  - `--fuzz N` adds N random programs (`--seed` to repeat a run; a difference prints what it takes): a contract and three helpers, each a short run of steps (a slot set to 0, 1 or 2 or read, transient storage, memory grown, an event, a call, delegate call or static call to a helper further down with all the gas or a set amount, a wei to an account that doesn't exist, or to a precompile, a creation (plain, with a constructor that writes, or colliding), a call into a delegated account, a read past the end of what a call returned), helpers ending by stopping, reverting, failing or destroying themselves, on one of several gas limits, tight ones among them. Its first 160 on Sepolia found the gas limits over the cap below (random programs stay under it since: a node's simulation is no guide to the chain there). After that, on 2026-11-11, 1,408 on Sepolia and 192 or more on each other chain agreed, but for two on Arbitrum One by 2 and 5 gas: its node takes the L1 charge out of the gas a call is given, so a call inside that fails with everything it was handed burns a little less, and a random program is allowed that charge there; one given its own gas limit that runs out on the node, where the local EVM's run fits that limit within twice the L1 charge, is listed as expected for the same reason. On 2026-11-12, after the day's changes to Base's and BNB Chain's rules, 900 more (150 on each of Ethereum, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon, BNB Chain and Sepolia, `--seed 1112`) all agreed. The scheduled run adds 48 new ones a day on every chain.
  - The same run compares each node's fork id with the one recorded as checked, and lists a scheduled fork and the precompiles only the node has.
  - **A fork before it happens** (`npm run rules -- --next`): every program again, in a block at the time of the fork a chain has scheduled (its `eth_config`'s `next`), which a node's `eth_simulateV1` runs under that fork's rules. So the local EVM can be ready on the day. On 2026-11-12 only BNB Chain had one scheduled, for 2026-12-08 (fork id `0x23d35e07`), and its `eth_config` lists one change: a precompile, `MILLI_TIMESTAMP` at `0x70`. Measured on its node in blocks at the fork's time: it returns the block's time in milliseconds as a 32-byte word (its seconds times 1,000, plus the milliseconds a BNB Chain block's mixHash carries, BEP-520), for 20 gas whatever the input, warm from the start, and runs out given 19. Before the fork the address is an empty account. The local EVM has it from that time on (`ChainProfile.timedPrecompiles`), with a block's own milliseconds in a replay and none in a simulated next block, as the node simulates one. At the fork's time all 370 programs agree with the node, the precompile's two among them; with its price set one gas off, the check said so. Its `eth_config` doesn't list all the fork changes: with BNB Chain's own precompiles among the programs (2026-11-15), the check at the fork's time found `VERIFY_DOUBLE_SIGN_EVIDENCE` (`0x68`) costing 16 gas more a byte of input from then on (26,000 for 1,000 bytes, 10,000 now; the other five the same). The local EVM charges that from the fork's time, and all 387 programs agree there. The fork is recorded as checked ahead (`checked.next`), so traces after 2026-12-08 carry no note that the rules changed. Measuring it showed something else: BNB Chain's node takes a `prevRandao` override as the block's milliseconds and answers opcode `0x44` with it, where a mined block answers with its difficulty (2, with mixHash milliseconds of 50, 400, 750 and 100 in four blocks). So its node is now given the difficulty as a difficulty override, and no `prevRandao`.
  - On a chain whose blocks carry their access lists (Sepolia), it also replays 24 recent transactions with a trace: each must match its receipt, use the node's gas, and change exactly the state its block's list records.
- **Glamsterdam.** Not live on Ethereum mainnet or any of the seven chains; live on Ethereum Sepolia since 2026-10-06 (measured: its headers carry a block access list hash and a slot number, `SLOTNUM` runs, a call costs 15,000 gas). What is in place, checked against Sepolia's node:
  - *The preview itself.* From Glamsterdam on a chain logs every native transfer as an ERC-20 `Transfer` from `0xffff…fffe` (EIP-7708), and its node no longer adds the pseudo-logs `traceTransfers` asked for. Read as a token, "Send 1 ETH" showed 1,000,000,000,000,000,000 of an unknown token at that address. Those logs are now the native coin moving. Without this, every preview that moves ETH would have been wrong on mainnet from the day of the fork.
  - *The local EVM.* A block whose parent's header carries a block access list hash runs the EVM library's Amsterdam rules (on Ethereum and Sepolia), with Amsterdam's transaction-level gas written here from the library's reference: a base of 12,000; 3,000 for the recipient unless the sender calls itself; 6,000 more when value moves; a creation's 12,000; access lists at 2,900 an address and 2,000 a key plus their bytes at the floor's price; a floor of 64 gas a calldata byte. A transaction's gas limit above 16,777,216 is kept aside for state (new accounts at 183,600, new storage slots at 97,920): what a call draws from it is added to its gas, and the `GAS` opcode never reads more than the 16,777,216 less the transaction's own charge. Mainnet will switch by itself when its headers do.
  - *Checked on Sepolia* (2026-11-11): 13 crafted requests (a call, a self-call, value, a new account at 204,600 gas, the calldata floor, access lists, a 100-byte creation at 360,712, a gas limit) and a storage refund cost the same on the node and locally, to the unit; 46 of 46 recent transactions had the same outcome and events, and the 40 that succeed the same gas; the first 30 transactions of a block replayed against their receipts: 30 of 30 match, 30 of 30 traces agree, gas the same in 30. And the receipt harness (`npm run accuracy -- --chain 11155111`, which replays each mined transaction at its exact pre-state): 34 of 34 with the receipt's outcome, token movements, native movements, approvals and gas, to the unit, on the node and in the local EVM alike. Its first run had the local EVM's gas off by one or two new storage slots in 2 of 28, transactions with a gas limit above 16,777,216: the state reserve above was the fix.
  - *EIP-7702 under Amsterdam.* Each authorization costs 7,816 gas, applied or not. One that is applied pays 9,000 for the first write to the authority's account in the transaction (nothing for the sender's own account, which the transaction writes anyway, nor for an authority's second authorization), and for the state it adds: an authority with no account yet, 143 bytes at 1,530 each; one that exists, the delegation's 23 bytes; one already delegated, nothing more. Checked two ways on Sepolia. Mined transactions by each kind of authority, replayed with a trace: new ones (494,493 and 494,481 gas), one that existed (461,917) and one already delegated (152,535) cost the same in the receipt, on the node and locally. And eleven made-up cases run on the node and locally (new, existing, delegated, clearing a delegation, two authorities, a wrong nonce, the same authority twice, the sender itself once and twice): 11 of 11 the same. Until then the local EVM charged the 9,000 for new accounts only, as the EVM library's reference does: an existing or delegated authority came out 9,000 low. A transaction whose gas, after what it pays before its call, can't pay for that state runs out of gas: all its gas used, nothing run, and its authorizations not applied, its nonce alone taken. A delegation sent on 29,724 gas (Sepolia, 2026-11-16: 19,816 before its call, 35,190 for the delegation) failed so, the account left without code and its nonce up by one; the local EVM had applied it and reported 55,006 gas, over the transaction's own limit. Now it fails as the chain's did, with the receipt's gas.
  - *What a cleared slot gives back.* A new slot's state is paid for (97,920 gas) and given back if the slot is cleared again in the same transaction: an allowance approved and then spent, a lock set and released. Where that was paid out of a call's own gas (any ordinary gas limit), the EVM library gives it back only within the call that paid; Sepolia gives it back to the caller that paid when the clearing call returns, and meanwhile lets calls further in spend it on new state. Found by the rules check's replays: a mined transaction that approved two tokens, then had a router spend both allowances and mint, used 413,342 gas on a limit of 554,177, and ran out of gas in the local EVM. Four programs pin the rule on Sepolia's node (the caller can spend the gas on anything; the call that clears can't; a new slot in that call can; a call that clears and then reverts gives nothing back), and the local EVM now follows it: all four, and that transaction, to the unit. After it, 60 of 60 replays spread through a block match their receipts with every trace agreeing, the gas the same in 54 of 54 compared and the state changes those the block's access list records in 60 of 60.
  - *A gas limit over 16,777,216.* From Glamsterdam on a transaction may have one, and what it holds over that pays for new state only: the code has 16,777,216 to run on, and a transaction that burns all its gas burns that much. A mined transaction shows it: it failed on a 70,000,000 limit and used exactly 16,777,216. Sepolia's node doesn't do this when it simulates (it gives the whole limit as ordinary gas: `GAS` reads 19,984,998 on a limit of 20,000,000, and that transaction's replay used 50,000,000), so here the node's simulation is the one that is wrong and the local EVM the one that follows the chain. Two programs in the rules check show it and are listed as the node's own departure. A replay whose node run doesn't match its receipt is shown as the local EVM ran it when that does (below); a request not yet mined, with such a limit on such a chain, is run in the local EVM too, and where the two differ the local EVM's run is shown, with a note (that transaction, previewed again: reverted, 16,777,216 gas). 40 of 40 other mined transactions with limits over the cap replayed the same either way.
  - *The limits Amsterdam moves.* A contract and its initcode may be longer. On Sepolia a creation of 25,000 bytes of code succeeds (38,468,879 gas, nearly all of it for the new state, drawn from what a transaction's gas limit holds above the cap on ordinary gas), and so does one from 131,072 bytes of initcode, where 131,073 fails; the local EVM does the same. The code limit is 65,536 bytes: the node refuses a creation of 65,537 for its size ("CreateContractSizeLimit"; of 131,072: "max code size exceeded: code size 131072 limit 65536"), before it is charged for the state, and the local EVM refuses the same ones (measured 2026-11-11). Below that the node's gas allowance for one simulation (50 million) runs out from 32,769 bytes of new code at 1,530 gas each, which says nothing about the chain; up to 32,768 the node's gas and the local EVM's are the same (50,353,988 for 32,768 bytes). `DUPN`, `SWAPN`, `EXCHANGE` and `SELFDESTRUCT`'s logs (a transfer, and a burn) are among the programs. Sepolia is served for this (`SEPOLIA_RPC_URL`).
  - *Replays start from the block's access list* (above), so how deep a transaction is in its block no longer matters. Before, the earlier transactions were run first, and a public node's gas allowance for one simulation ran out on Sepolia's 200-million-gas blocks from about the 20th to the 40th transaction. On 2026-11-11: 30 of 30 replays spread through a block of 100 transactions and 40 of 40 through one of 246 match their receipts, every trace agreeing and the gas the same wherever it is compared (66 of 66). Where a backend lacks the method the earlier transactions are still run, and a replay the node can't hold says so.
  - *UserOperations on Sepolia* (`npm run accuracy:4337 -- --chain 11155111`, EntryPoint v0.7, as the scheduled run does it): 57 of 57 with the receipt's outcome, 52 run whole, the gas paid within 0.05% for 41 and within 0.5% for the other 11 compared, token movements exact for 7 of 8 and within a fee's rounding for the other. Before the access list was used, 12 of 30 had been replayed without their block's earlier transactions and 3 not at all.
- **A chain that changes its rules is noticed.** Each chain's node is asked for its own account of its rules (`eth_config`, EIP-7910; kept ten minutes). A fork id other than the one the local EVM was last checked against puts a note on the trace. A precompile the node lists that the local EVM neither has nor forwards is refused rather than run as an empty account, and the missing trace says which: Base's `NonceManager` and `TxContext` at a block before its upgrade of 2026-11-05 (from it on the local EVM has them: below). (Base's `B20Factory`, `ActivationRegistry` and `PolicyRegistryPrecompile` run from their Solidity references: [above](#replaying-a-mined-transaction); Arbitrum's `0x73`, `0x74` and `ArbosActs`, refused until 2026-11-15, are forwarded with ArbOS's others: below; BNB Chain's `MILLI_TIMESTAMP`, from its fork of 2026-12-08, the local EVM has too: below.) Polygon's node lists no current fork and HyperEVM's has no `eth_config`, so neither check runs there.
- **Base's account-abstraction precompiles**, `NonceManager` (`0x8130…aa01`) and `TxContext` (`0x8130…aa02`), from the account-abstraction transaction type that EIP-8130 (keystore accounts) builds on, came with Base's upgrade of 2026-11-05 and run in the local EVM as Base's node runs them, measured on it (2026-11-15) by the gas a call from a contract spends. Both have no code and are warm from the start. `NonceManager.getNonce(address, uint256 key)` returns the account's nonce on that key, a `uint64` kept in the precompile's own storage at `nonces[account][key]` in ERC-7201's namespace `base.nonce_manager` (found by giving candidate slots a value in a state override: that one is what it answers), read from the chain like any slot; key 0 reverts with `ProtocolNonceNotSupported()`; it costs 6 gas a word of input plus the slot's read, cold or warm as an `SLOAD` (2,118, then 118 for the same key). `TxContext`'s `getTransactionSender()`, `getTransactionPayer()` and `getTransactionSenderActorId()` return the transaction's sender (`tx.origin`, not the contract that asks) for 3 gas: that is what they answer in any transaction but that type's own, and the local EVM runs no other (that type's `getTransactionCalls()` and `getTransactionGasLimit()` aren't on the node). Calldata either can't decode reverts with its selector; run as another's code they revert with `DelegateCallNotAllowed()`, sent value with `NonPayable()`, for nothing. A state override moving either away, or another precompile onto either, is refused (Base's node runs the one moved there). `NonceManager` being an empty account, a transaction that calls it leaves it to be removed as empty (EIP-161), storage and all: a nonce a state override gave it reads 0 in a later transaction of the simulated block on the node, and the local EVM now removes an empty account's storage with it too. How often traffic reaches them: in 2,000 consecutive Base blocks of 2026-11-15 (37,259 transactions) and 400 spread over the ten days since the upgrade (7,884), no transaction was sent to either or named either in its calldata, none was of a new type, and none of the contracts called (1,167 and 830, with the 391 and 232 implementations, clones and EIP-7702 delegates behind them) holds either address in its code. The public node serves no tracing, so a call from deeper in a transaction (a contract its target calls in turn) could only be looked for that way: none was seen.
- **Gas is compared.** A trace says whether its run used the same gas as the node's (`trace.gasAgrees`), when both succeeded: a difference means the local EVM's gas rules aren't the chain's for that call. Not compared on Arbitrum, whose node adds a charge for L1 data that depends on the call's bytes (the rules check compares it there, less that charge). Arbitrum's node charges no calldata floor (EIP-7623): 1,000 bytes of calldata to an account with no code cost 37,000 gas there, and the floor's 61,000 on Ethereum, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain (2026-11-15). The local EVM had charged Arbitrum the floor too, where neither the receipts nor the rules check could show it (both compare a transaction's gas there only when it sends no calldata); it no longer does.
- **Past blocks.** On Ethereum a block that was asked for runs under the fork of its time, from the Merge on; blocks before it aren't traced. Transaction-level gas older than Prague's is modeled only as far as the calldata floor, so an older creation's cost shows as a gas difference. On other chains past blocks run under today's rules, with a note when the block predates the node's current fork.
- **Refused, not misrun:** on Arbitrum, a contract creation that reaches a Stylus (WASM) program, or comes after a call to one in a batch, and any call that must go to the node for one where the node has no `eth_simulateV1` (a call made straight to a program, one that reaches a program from inside another contract, and the calls after either are the node's: below); contracts whose code is a marker for something the node runs itself (starts with `0xEF` and is no EIP-7702 delegation), apart from Base's B20 tokens, registries and factory, which run from their references (above; a B20 token is still refused at a block from before Base's upgrade of 2026-11-05, or of a variant with no reference), and Base's factory or a registry moved away by a state override; the node-only precompiles above; Ethereum blocks from before the Merge.
- **Opcodes a chain doesn't have.** Chains without blobs lack the blob opcodes: `BLOBBASEFEE` is invalid on Arbitrum and Polygon, `BLOBHASH` on Polygon too. The local EVM had accepted them; they are taken out for those chains.
- **Forwarded precompiles** (ArbOS's, HyperCore's reads, three of BNB Chain's) are answered by the chain with `eth_call` on its latest state: reads come out right, but a precompile call that changes state has no effect in the local run, and ArbOS's are charged as measured (800 to open the chain's state, 800 a storage slot read, 3 a word; the slots read are known for the methods contracts call most, and one is assumed for the rest; a revert is charged what the node's own call of it was, asked of the node, below). ArbOS's writes that real traffic makes are not left to that:
  - *What Arbitrum One's traffic writes.* Its public node has no traces (`debug_traceTransaction`, `trace_block` and `arbtrace_block` are refused), so ArbOS's logs say it: in the 350,000 blocks (28 hours) to 2026-11-15, 26 transactions sent a message to L1 through ArbSys (`L2ToL1Tx`), one of them a direct `withdrawEth` of 1.4 ETH and 25 from contracts (20 token withdrawals through the bridge's gateway router, whose `outboundTransfer` calls `sendTxToL1`); in a week, 3 transactions redeemed a retryable by hand (`redeem` sent to `0x6e`: a `RedeemScheduled` without a `TicketCreated`), next to 1,642 tickets redeemed as they were created. In the 350,000 blocks to block 524,432,756 (27.9 hours to 2026-11-15), the only other ArbOS precompile to emit an event was ArbRetryableTx (`0x6e`): a `TicketCreated` and a `RedeemScheduled` for each of 276 retryables, every one in the transaction that created it (a deposit from L1, redeemed as it arrives, in a transaction ArbOS makes, not one a wallet sends); none of `0x65`–`0x6d`, `0x6f`–`0x74` and `0xff` emitted one.
  - *ArbSys's sends run in the local EVM* (`sendTxToL1`, `withdrawEth`; `src/evm/arbos.ts`), as the node runs them: the value leaves ArbSys's balance (burned), the send is appended to ArbOS's send-merkle tree, read from and written to the storage of ArbOS's state account (`0xa4b05fff…`: its size at the first slot of its subspace, each level's partial after it, checked against `ArbSys.sendMerkleTreeState()`), and the call emits a `SendMerkleUpdate` for each level merged and the `L2ToL1Tx` with the send's hash, its leaf (what the call returns), the L2 and L1 block numbers, the time, the value and the data. Its gas, measured to the unit on the node: 800 to open ArbOS's state, 3 a word of arguments, the hash (30 and 6 a word of its 168 bytes and the data), the tree's size read and written (20,800), each level merged 7,717 (a read, a hash, a cleared slot, an event), the level the leaf lands on 20,800 (20,000 where it is a new top level, which isn't read first: once the tree's size reaches a power of two), the size read again (800), the `L2ToL1Tx` (1,875 and 8 a byte of its data), 3 for the word returned, and 800 more when value is sent: from 46,945 for an empty send. In a static call (or a call made inside one), a delegate call or by callcode, or with an argument missing, the node's call fails and burns all it was given, as the local EVM's does (a callcode had been forwarded, and succeeded). Before, the call was forwarded: no event, the value left at ArbSys, the leaf the chain's and not the run's, and about 1,610 gas where the node charges 46,945 to 71,420 in the calls measured, a static or delegate call succeeding where the node's fails.
  - *ArbRetryableTx's writes* (`redeem`, `keepalive`, `cancel`) of a ticket that exists are refused: a `redeem` gives the retry all the gas the call has left, and the chain runs the retry as a transaction of its own after this one, which the node's `eth_simulateV1` can't be shown doing without a live ticket (none was left in the week's 1,642). Of a ticket that doesn't exist they revert with `NoTicketWithID()`, as on the node, at its price (`redeem` 2,406, the others 1,606). With calldata short of a ticket id (36 bytes) ArbOS can't decode it: the call fails burning all it was given and the transaction goes on, on the node and now locally (where the whole simulation had been refused as reaching a ticket that exists).
  - *What a failed call costs.* ArbOS burns all a call was given only where it turns the call away before its method runs (Nitro's `precompiles/precompile.go`): a method it hasn't, arguments it can't decode, a method that writes in a static call (or in a call made inside one), one that isn't pure in a delegate call or callcode, value to one that isn't payable. An error the method itself raises keeps the rest, whether it has data to decode (`NoTicketWithID()`; `ArbosActs`' `CallerNotArbOS()`, 815) or none (`ArbAddressTable.lookup` of an address it hasn't, 1,603; its `lookupIndex` of 2^200, 803; `ArbAggregator.getFeeCollector` of an account that isn't a batch poster, 1,603). The chain's answer to the forwarded `eth_call` says neither which it was nor what it cost, so where it is a revert the node is asked again, from a contract that reports the gas the call spent, and the local call is charged that (all it was given where the node's call burnt all, or where the charge is more than it was given). How a call is made a plain `eth_call` can't show: those checks the local EVM makes itself, from Nitro's interfaces (which methods write, which are payable, which are pure; `ARBOS_METHODS`). `0x74` charges its 1,600 before its checks: a call it turns away reverts at 1,600 and keeps the rest. Measured before this: every revert had burnt all (`keepalive` of no ticket: 1,606 on the node, all the call's gas locally); then one with data kept the rest at a modeled price, and one without burnt all: `lookup`, `lookupIndex` and `getFeeCollector` above burnt all locally, `arbBlockHash` of a future block came out 1,612 (812 on the node), `ArbWasm.programVersion` of an account that isn't a program 1,606 (4,306). And a call turned away for how it was made ran as a plain call: `redeem` of no ticket in a static call, in a call made inside one or with 1 wei, `getTimeout` and `keepalive` in a delegate call, `arbBlockNumber` in a delegate call, `ArbAddressTable.register` in a static call answered or failed at a price where the node's call burns all, and `0x74`'s `isTransactionFiltered` with 1 wei or in a delegate call answered where the node's reverts at 1,600. A pure method runs in a delegate call on the node, and costs 3 a word (`mapL1SenderContractAddressToL2Alias`, 9; it had been charged 1,609). And `ArbosTest.burnArbGas` asked to burn more than the call has left burns all of it and succeeds (ArbOS ignores its running out there), on the node and now locally, where it had run out.
  - *`0x73` and `0x74`* are, by `eth_config`'s names, `ArbNativeTokenManager` and `ArbFilteredTransactionsManager`. On Arbitrum One every call to `0x73` (`mintNativeToken`, `burnNativeToken`, none) reverts with nothing and burns all it was given; `0x74` costs 1,600 whatever it is given, answers `isTransactionFiltered` (false for the hashes tried) and refuses `addFilteredTransaction` from an account that isn't a filterer, reverting with nothing but keeping the rest. `ArbosActs` (`0xa4b05`) refuses every method with `CallerNotArbOS()`. Each is forwarded so, where it had been refused. `0x6a` is no precompile on Arbitrum One (its `eth_config` lists none there): a call to it is one to an empty account, cold (2,600), on the node and now locally, where it had been forwarded with ArbOS's at 1,600 and turned away with value, in a delegate call or by callcode, failing where the node's call succeeds.
  - *Checked on Arbitrum One's node* (2026-11-15), each call alone on one block, sent from a contract that reports the gas it spent: an account's `withdrawEth` of 0.001 ETH, four `sendTxToL1`s (0, 1, 33 and 300 bytes, with and without value), a `withdrawEth` with nothing sent, in a static call and in a delegate call, `redeem` and `keepalive` of no ticket, `0x74`'s two methods, `0x73`'s `mintNativeToken` and `ArbosActs.startBlock`: 0 of 14 alike before (no events, the gas, the static and delegate calls, the reverts' gas, the last four refused), 14 of 14 after, events, return and gas to the unit (a call that burns all it was given less the node's L1 charge). Then 72 more (a few the same call in two runs), on how a call is made and what a failed one costs: sends of 1 to 1,000 bytes and odd encodings, by callcode, two in one call, one undone by a revert, on exactly the gas they cost and one less; methods of ArbRetryableTx, ArbSys, ArbAddressTable, ArbAggregator, ArbGasInfo, ArbWasm, ArbosTest, `0x74` and `ArbosActs` in static and delegate calls, by callcode, from inside a static call and with 1 wei; and their errors, with data and without: 40 differed before, 70 alike after. The other two, one call run twice, are `arbBlockHash` of a future block, whose gas now matches (812) but whose error names the block the simulation starts from (below). The rules check (`npm run rules -- --chain 42161`, block 524,430,969) agreed on all 416 programs, the sends' events among them; the 19 programs added for the calls above differed on the code before (block 524,432,608). Then, from a review (2026-11-15), 22 more: calldata short of a ticket id to `redeem`, `keepalive` and `cancel` (from a contract, and once straight from an account), `0x6a` called each way, and `ArbosTest.burnArbGas` asked for more than the call has (on 10,000 gas, in a call, a static call and a delegate call, and on all a call can be given), next to four that were alike already (`redeem` of a ticket that doesn't exist; `burnArbGas` of exactly what it has, of 2^64, and on less than its argument costs). The 18 the review found all differed before (the short calldata had the whole simulation refused as reaching a ticket that exists; `0x6a` was charged 1,600 where the node charges 2,600, and failed with value, in a delegate call and by callcode; `burnArbGas` ran out where the node's succeeds), and 21 of the 22 are alike after, to the unit; the other, `burnArbGas` on all a call can be given, succeeds spending all of it on both, what it was given differing by the node's L1 charge. The rules check, with seven programs added for them, agreed on all 423 (block 524,442,785). And `test/arbos.test.ts` holds ten calls recorded from the node on one tree: eight sends, merging none to three levels, and a static and a delegate call, which the local EVM matches; and the calls turned away and the failed calls above, the review's three among them, against the node's answers as measured.
  - Still forwarded as reads: ArbSys's `isTopLevelCall`, `wasMyCallersAddressAliased` and `myCallersAddressWithoutAliasing`, which the chain answers for a call of its own rather than for where the call is in the run, and `arbBlockHash`, which it answers for the block the simulation starts from, one before the simulated block (a block at or past it is refused with an `InvalidBlockNumber` naming that block, not the simulated one); and the rest of ArbOS's writes: owner-only `ArbOwner`, `ArbWasm`'s activation of Stylus programs (which the local EVM can't run anyway) and `ArbWasmCache`, which emitted no event that day, and `ArbAddressTable.register`, which emits none, so how often it is called wasn't seen. The point-evaluation precompile (`0x0a`, which rollup contracts call to verify blobs) is forwarded too: it needs a KZG library and trusted setup this package doesn't carry, and had thrown instead of running. It is a pure function of its input, so the node's answer is exact, at the precompile's fixed 50,000 gas.
- **Arbitrum's Stylus programs** (WASM, behind the code prefix `0xEFF000`). A call made straight to one, a request's own target, is handed to the chain's node (`eth_simulateV1`, on the block the preview's simulation uses, with the batch's calls before it and a replay's earlier transactions first): its outcome, return data, events and gas are the node's own run (a failed call's return data read from the node's `error.data`, where Arbitrum's node puts it, its `returnData` being empty), and the trace shows it as one frame marked as the node's (`TraceCall.node`), with all its events, since what the program called inside isn't known. The amounts are the node's too: in the same simulated block it reads the account's native balance and each watched token's balance before and after, and the approvals touched after. The reads on each side of the call are one call, to a reader that makes the native balance's read and every token's and approval's a static call, as the local EVM's reads are (each on 10 million gas); the reads after a call are the ones before the next. Made as calls of their own, as they were, the reads ran as transactions in that block: a contract read as a token (the call's own target is one) ran its code there, and the call ran on what it left (in a test, a contract that pays 1 DAI the first time it is called came out with no event, its read having paid). And they made the request too big: Arbitrum One's public node answers an `eth_simulateV1` of more than 30 calls, counted across its blocks, with HTTP 429 every time (30 ran and 31 never did, 15 and 15 ran and 15 and 16 didn't, 2026-11-16), and a call with ten more tokens watched took 37, and ran out of time after eight refusals. The native balance after is less the call's fee as well, which the node takes at the call's price for all the gas it used, its L1 charge included (at one, two and ten times the base fee alike): the fee being counted apart, as for the local EVM's runs, it is put back. The node adds its charge for L1 data to the call's gas; the same call is run again a block later with the program's code taken away, at the call's own nonce and with its value, its sender given the balance to send it (each counts in the charge: a probe to another address came out 176 gas short with 200 zero bytes of calldata, the same call a nonce later 10 gas over, and a probe sent with no value 35 gas short for a call sending 1 ETH, 44 for one sending 5,373 ETH), and what it used over its calldata's cost comes off, so the gas is the L2 part, as the local EVM's is. Measured on Arbitrum One on 2026-11-15 against the node's own run of each call: a deposit of 1,000 USDC into the one program with traffic (funded by a state override), the same deposit unfunded, `transferOwnership` by an account that isn't the owner and an unknown selector came out the same in outcome, return data, events and gas, to the unit (78,408, 63,569, 41,746 and 39,175; the two that revert with data, `TransferFailed()` and `0x30cd7471`, had come out with none, as "execution reverted", before the return data was read from `error.data`); with the block's base fee set, three calls of 4, 68 and 205 bytes the same L2 gas to the unit (39,175, 63,593 and 44,396), and `getOwner()` sending 1 ETH and 5,373 ETH (reverting: it takes no value) 39,187 as the node's (39,222 and 39,231 with a probe that sent no value); `getOwner()` priced at one, two and ten times the base fee moved no native coin (its fee had shown as 837,480,000,000, 1,666,920,000,000 and 8,283,000,000,000 wei sent to the program). Before, each was refused.
  - **A call that reaches a program from inside another contract** (a proxy whose implementation is one, a router or a multicall that calls one) goes to the node too, and so does **every call after either in a batch**, whose state the local EVM then doesn't have. The local run stops where it first reads the program's account, and is thrown away, with what it had begun of the call: the node runs that call whole. Each call goes with the batch's calls before it, but not one whose sender couldn't pay for it, which the local run leaves out too; the calls the node runs go together, in one request (below). Each runs on what every request of the batch sets; an EIP-7702 authorization the wallet has yet to sign stands in as its code, as in the node's own simulation. The calls before stay the local EVM's. The frame's note says why and names the program, and so does the result's source: "chain 42161's node, for the Stylus (WASM) program 0x…, which the local EVM can't run, reached from inside another contract", or "…, after call 2 of the batch, which reaches the Stylus (WASM) program 0x…". A read around a call that reaches a program (a watched token behind a proxy whose implementation is one) hands the call over too. Where it is a read, before the call, of the call's own target (which is always watched), the call goes through that contract: a call to a proxy whose implementation is a program is handed over at the read of the proxy's `balanceOf` (so on Arbitrum One, 2026-11-16), and its source says "reached from inside another contract (first by a read of a balance or a permission around the call)". Any other read says only that: "reached by a read around the call (a balance or a permission)", and the calls after it "after call 1 of the batch, a read around which reaches …", since the call itself wasn't seen to reach the program. On Arbitrum One (block 524,636,762), a call to such a proxy (74,981 gas) and a USDC `approve` with the proxy watched (55,221, its read reaching the program) each came out as the node's own run, its source saying which. The tokens the node read are listed as read, as a local run's are; a UserOperation's native coin is counted from them. A program reached on a value a speculative round guessed isn't taken for reached: the value is read, and the round run again.
  - Where a request names no gas limit, the call and its probe are given the local EVM's 30,000,000. The node prices a call's L1 data with its gas limit, and a call not given one gets the block's gas left, which differs between the call and its probe. Multicall3's 388-byte call was charged 1,161 for L1 data first in its block and 1,170 after three calls, and 1,170 at either place given 30,000,000; its L2 part had come out 6 gas over.
  - Measured on Arbitrum One on 2026-11-16, block 524,613,030 (base fee 0.02 gwei), against the node's own `eth_simulateV1` run of the same calls on the same block. Ten cases, all refused before, came out the same in outcome, return data, events and L2 gas, to the unit, in all 18 calls (16 run by the node, and the two approvals before a program's call by the local EVM), and in the amounts where any moved:
    - a forwarding contract (put in by a state override) calling the vault program's `getOwner()` (44,360), its `transferOwnership` for a caller that isn't the owner (44,760, reverting with `0x30cd7471`), and its `deposit` after an approval (the approval the local EVM's, 58,458; the deposit 44,976, reverting with `0x76419f97`, as the program now does on the node for any caller);
    - Multicall3's `aggregate3` of `getOwner()` and a 1 USDC transfer (93,558; Multicall3's 1 USDC out, read by the node);
    - the owner, given the forwarder's code, transferring ownership (51,659, with its `OwnershipTransferred` event), then `getOwner()` straight and through the forwarder, each returning the new owner;
    - the program's `getOwner()` first in a batch, then an approval and a nested call; an approval, the program's `deposit` and a 1 USDC transfer; the forwarder calling the program, then moving 1 USDC;
    - the call of a mined transaction that reaches a program through a proxy (0x14f5…acfa, below), on today's state: it reverts with the program's panic "exchange failed", 165,395 on both.
    - on blocks 524,621,092 and 524,624,399, with a Stylus ERC-20 (0x0060…2ba4, no decimals) and a program that takes value with no calldata (0x66da…de2e): a contract's call of the token's `transfer` (74,649, 3 out of the contract), the same from a holder's own account given that code, a `transfer` that reverts caught by the contract (50,669, the transaction succeeding), 0.001 ETH sent through to the program (56,493), 0.002 ETH sent with a call the token refuses (53,839, kept by the contract), the token's `approve` (69,365, the allowance 5 read back), a minimal proxy delegating to the token (74,981), an account delegated to the contract by an EIP-7702 authorization signed (87,149) and yet to be signed (87,149), priced at 0.02 gwei with a gas limit (the fee put back), a call that names sixteen tokens to read; and batches of seven, three and three calls, among them a `transfer` that runs only on what the calls before it left and a call its sender couldn't pay for, left out: each call the same as the node's own run in outcome, return data, events, L2 gas and amounts.
  - Time on Arbitrum One's public node, 2026-11-16: in the local EVM's run, a single call the node ran took 0.9 to 1.1 seconds (six cases, one setting an approval, which is asked again with it read). The calls the node runs in a batch are asked in one `eth_simulateV1` (a round's), as many of one account's in a row as fit in the node's 30 calls: the reads before the first, each call and the reads after it (the ones before the next), and each one's probe, so nine with nothing before them. Asked one by one, as they first were, a batch of four took 4 to 10 seconds and a batch of seven ran out of the local EVM's 20 both times; asked together, on the same block, the four took 0.8 seconds (one request), the seven 6 to 10 (one), and ten calls of two accounts (two requests) 10 seconds once and ran out of time once. What is left is the node's rate limit: after the burst of state reads a run begins with, it lets about one `eth_simulateV1` through every three to five seconds from one address (each answered after three or four HTTP 429s). A batch the node refuses whole (a sender that can't pay for its gas) is asked again call by call, so the one that can't pay fails as any call does. Through the preview, whose own simulation asks the node first, a batch of a nested call and an approval after it was traced within the 15 seconds a trace is given.
  - Still refused: a contract creation that reaches a program, or comes after a call that does, since its charge for L1 data can't be told apart from what its code uses (there is no code to take away); and any of these where the node has no `eth_simulateV1`. What isn't known: the storage a program wrote (a result the node ran has no state changes) and the calls it made. Running the WASM here would need Nitro's activation (its instrumentation, ink prices per WASM opcode and host call, memory and init costs) reproduced to the unit, and the public node has no `debug_traceCall`, `eth_createAccessList` or `trace_*` to read a frame back from.
  - How much that leaves, measured on 2026-11-15: of 1,103 transactions in 150 blocks across 6,000, none reached a Stylus program by its `to`, its events or an EIP-1967 implementation; of the 1,070 programs ever activated (ArbWasm's `ProgramActivated`), one emitted any event in about 2,000,000 blocks (six days), in 4 transactions, each a call made straight to it by an account (deposits and withdrawals of USDC, now run on the node); and of 106 recent transactions (40 blocks across 2,400; 99 distinct contracts and functions) put through the local EVM's trace, 105 traced, one was lost to the node's rate limit, and none reached a Stylus program. Looking for calls that reach a program inside, on 2026-11-16: 16 contracts had an EIP-1967 implementation set to a program (an `Upgraded` event naming one, in the 30,000 blocks after each activation), and 15 still delegate to one. In the 18,900,000 blocks to block 524,609,294 (about eight weeks, read newest first until 40 were found) they had 44 transactions with events, all to two of them. The latest was at block 515,148,822 (2026-10-15); none came in the 2,000,000 blocks to block 524,607,221. That latest, 0x14f5128e9a9da6b75b8288bf0d8a4abe54a8f6f411d1bc18d09509ee4100acfa, calls another contract, which reaches a program through such a proxy. It can't be replayed against its receipt: the public node no longer has its block's state ("missing trie node", six tries).
- **BNB Chain's own precompiles** (`0x64`–`0x69`, as its `eth_config` names them) are pure functions of their input, each priced and failing as its node's do (`ChainProfile.ownPrecompiles`). Measured on its node (reth-bsc 0.1.4, 2026-11-15) by the gas a call to each spends from a contract, and, where a failure takes all the gas a call is given, by the least gas a call straight to it needs to fail some other way than running out: `SECP256K1_SIGNATURE_RECOVER` (`0x69`) takes a compressed key, `r` and `s` (`s` low) and a 32-byte digest, costs 3,000 gas and answers the key's Tendermint address (RIPEMD-160 of its SHA-256); the local EVM runs it. `BLS_SIGNATURE_VERIFY` (`0x66`: 1,000 gas and 3,500 a key; `0x01` when the signature holds, nothing when it doesn't), `COMET_BFT_LIGHT_BLOCK_VALIDATE_PASTEUR` (`0x67`: 3,000 and 16 a byte of input) and `VERIFY_DOUBLE_SIGN_EVIDENCE` (`0x68`: 10,000, and from the fork of 2026-12-08 16 more a byte of input) are answered by the chain at those prices; given an input they can't read, `0x66` and `0x68` revert and give back the rest of the gas, `0x67` takes all of it. The two deprecated ones (`0x64`, `0x65`) fail whatever they are given. Until 2026-11-15 all six were answered by the chain at a flat 2,000 gas, a failure taking all the gas. What calls them: the code of three of the chain's system contracts does (TokenRecoverPortal at `0x…3000` calls `0x69`; StakeHub at `0x…2002`, `0x66`; SlashIndicator at `0x…1001`, `0x66` and `0x68`), and so does a light-client contract's (the proxy `0x433b…986B`, whose implementation calls `0x66` and `0x67`), which logged nothing in that window or in windows half a million, a million and two million blocks back. In the 200,000 blocks to 2026-11-15 (about 25 hours) their logs show 7 transactions that reach one, all TokenRecoverPortal's `recover` (which checks a Beacon Chain owner's signature with `0x69`), and no validator created or vote key changed at StakeHub and no evidence at SlashIndicator. And of 33,522 transactions calling contracts in 461 blocks sampled over 25 minutes (about one block in six), none reached any of the six: each block's transactions were run on the node twice, the second time with each precompile moved away and code that calls it in its place, so that one reaching it would use other gas. The latest of the seven, replayed with a trace: 219,496 gas in its receipt, on the node and now in the local EVM (218,496 before, the `0x69` call 1,000 low). Not seen: `0x67`'s price on an input it accepts (no valid light block was at hand; only where its failures start), and what `0x68` answers to evidence that holds. The BLS answers stay the chain's: computing them here would need a BLS library's edge cases (encodings, subgroups, infinity) checked against the node first.

### Simulating on a past block

`context.block` (a number, or one in hex) simulates a transaction or a batch on the state after that block, as the first transaction of the block that followed (its real header; for the chain's latest block, the one that would follow): what it would have done then, not now.

- `simulation.block` names the state's block, the notes say when it was, and the dollar values are as of that block.
- Whether it could be included now isn't the question, so the nonce, balance and fee checks aren't run. The priced second run uses that block's base fee.
- The decoder's own lookups (token names, a lending position it describes, a Safe's owners) still read the latest block. Signatures (typed data, messages, UserOperations, SafeTx) are previewed against the latest block, and say so.
- A node that no longer has the block's state is an error ("This chain's node doesn't keep the state of block N…"), never a simulation on the latest block instead. (A block named only so that a trace matches the simulation still falls back, as before.)
- The local EVM's caches of contract code and last-seen values are of the latest state: for a block that was asked for they are neither used nor written.

### Simulating with state you set

`context.stateOverrides` gives accounts a native balance, code or storage slots for the simulation, in place of the chain's: "what would this do if the account held 2 ETH", "…if this slot were set". A UserOperation's prefund check reads an account's balance from them too, as its simulation does.

```json
{ "request": { "method": "eth_sendTransaction", "params": [{ "from": "0x…", "to": "0x…", "value": "0xde0b6b3a7640000", "chainId": "0x1" }] },
  "context": { "stateOverrides": { "0x…": { "balance": "2000000000000000000", "stateDiff": { "0x09": "0x2a" } } } } }
```

- Keys are addresses (at most 32). `balance` is in wei (hex, or a decimal string), `code` is hex (at most 24,576 bytes), `stateDiff` maps slots to values (at most 256 per account, each padded to 32 bytes). Anything else is refused (`400 INVALID_REQUEST`), not ignored.
- They reach every simulator: the node's `eth_simulateV1`, the `eth_call` probe and the local EVM (so a trace runs with them too), under the request's own overrides (an EIP-7702 delegation's code).
- The preview carries a **caution**, `state_overridden`: it shows what would happen if the chain were in that state, not what will. The inclusion checks (nonce, balance, fee) still describe the real chain, as do an operation's prefund and nonce checks and a Safe transaction's nonce and owner checks.
- For transactions and batches, and for what a signature authorizes where it is simulated: a UserOperation (EntryPoint v0.6 to v0.9, the whole operation and, where that can't run, its calls alone), a `SafeOp` (likewise, through the Safe's module), a `SafeTx` (`execTransaction` itself, or the Safe's calls one by one) and the Safe run an `execTransaction` transaction's decoder makes. Each of these says so with the same caution, once (a batch says it once for all its calls). Other signatures (permits, orders, messages) aren't simulated: their previews note that the overrides weren't applied. They combine with `context.block` (transactions and batches only) and `context.trace` (a trace runs with them).
- **Beneath the stand-ins.** A signature that doesn't exist yet is stood in for (see [UserOperations, simulated as the EntryPoint runs them](#useroperations-simulated-as-the-entrypoint-runs-them)), and the stand-ins need state of their own: the EntryPoint's simulation code and the slots it keeps for itself (its SenderCreator, from v0.8 its EIP-712 domain separator); `ecrecover` replaced (the precompile moved), and the P-256 precompile or verifier answering a passkey's stand-in; a Zeal signer's stand-in code; the EIP-7702 delegation an operation is signed for; a stand-in key's storage in the account (or the execution hook Kernel keeps for an operation); a Safe's approvals for the owners its threshold needs; the bundler's or submitter's balance for the priced run. The caller's state goes beneath them, account by account and slot by slot: where both set the same field of an account (its code, its balance) or the same storage slot, the stand-in's is used, since validation can't run without it, and the caller's other fields and slots stay. So code set for `0x…01` or the EntryPoint isn't used (ecrecover still answers every signature but the stand-in the chain's way, and the EntryPoint runs its simulation code over the deposits and nonces set in its storage), while a balance, a deposit or a slot set for the account, its paymaster, the EntryPoint or the Safe is. The stand-in's shape is chosen from the account as it is on the chain.
- A Safe's nonce, owners, threshold and configuration, and the transaction's hash, are read in that state (`eth_call`'s state override), so the approvals stand in for the owners it has there, and what the transaction changes in the Safe compares like with like. (A node that refused `eth_call` overrides would leave the Safe unread, as one that isn't a Safe: a `SafeTx`'s plain calls are then run one by one, still with them.) Whether an account's code is there (a UserOperation's calls run alone: a new account deployed first, or no code to run) also goes by the code set for it.
- A multi-chain Safe root's operations each run with them, on their own chain.
- Checked live on Ethereum, Base and HyperEVM (2026-11-10): an empty account sending 5 of the native token fails ("insufficient funds") on the chain's state and succeeds with its balance set to 9, with the trace agreeing with the node's run.
- Checked live on Ethereum (2026-11-15, `eth_simulateV1` through PublicNode), each asking an account for more ETH than it holds: a `SafeTx` from a Safe with 0.0027 ETH sending 1 fails (GS013) and with the Safe's balance set to 2 sends it; a `SafeOp` through Safe4337Module v0.3.0 from the same Safe has its calls fail and, with the balance set, runs whole and sends it; a v0.7 UserOperation of an EIP-7702 account (MetaMask's DeleGator, 4.7 ETH) sending 10 has its calls fail and runs whole with its balance set to 20. Each run with the balance set carries the caution; code also set for `0x…01` and the EntryPoint changes nothing, and the Safe's threshold set to 2 has a second owner's approval stand in.

## Mock and live data

`npm start` uses mock data. To use real data:

```bash
SIGNING_PREVIEW_MODE=live ETHEREUM_RPC_URL=… ARBITRUM_RPC_URL=… HYPEREVM_RPC_URL=… BASE_RPC_URL=… npm start
```

Live mode reads Hyperliquid's public `/info` API and simulates on any chain that has an RPC URL configured: with `eth_simulateV1`, or, where the RPC lacks it, in a local EVM over the chain's state (every supported chain; what HyperEVM, whose public RPC has no `eth_simulateV1`, uses), with `eth_call` and a state override answering if that takes too long. Chains without one are previewed from the decoded request only. The RPC URLs also enable on-chain token metadata and proxy detection.

| Variable | Effect |
|---|---|
| `ETHEREUM_RPC_URL`, `ARBITRUM_RPC_URL`, `HYPEREVM_RPC_URL`, `BASE_RPC_URL`, `OPTIMISM_RPC_URL`, `POLYGON_RPC_URL`, `BSC_RPC_URL` | Simulation, `symbol()`/`decimals()` lookups and proxy detection on that chain (Ethereum, Arbitrum One, HyperEVM, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon, BNB Chain); a chain without one isn't simulated. Any node with `eth_simulateV1` works: the public ones the scripts default to (`EVM_RPCS` in `src/chains.ts`: publicnode's for Ethereum, Base, OP Mainnet, Polygon and BNB Chain, Arbitrum's and Hyperliquid's own) all have it. |
| `TOKEN_LIST_FILE` | A [tokenlists.org](https://tokenlists.org)-format JSON file, consulted after the built-in tokens. |
| `ETHERSCAN_API_KEY` | Adds Etherscan (API v2: Ethereum, Arbitrum and HyperEVM with one key) after Sourcify. |
| `SEPOLIA_RPC_URL` | Ethereum Sepolia, the testnet that runs each Ethereum fork before mainnet does (Glamsterdam since 2026-10-06): simulated like the other chains. |
| `SIGNING_PREVIEW_OFFLINE_LOOKUPS=1` | Don't call Sourcify, Etherscan, OpenChain or 4byte. The built-in signature list still works. |
| `SIGNING_PREVIEW_PRICES=off` | No dollar values (otherwise Chainlink's feeds through the configured RPCs, and Hyperliquid's mid prices on HyperEVM). |
| `SIGNING_PREVIEW_FORK_CACHE` | A file that keeps the HyperEVM simulator's caches (contract code, last values seen) across restarts. The CLI uses `~/.cache/signing-preview/fork-cache.json` unless set; `off` for none. |

`liveDependencies()` was run against Ethereum, Arbitrum One and HyperEVM, with Sourcify, OpenChain and 4byte, on 2026-11-04. Token metadata, proxy detection, verified-ABI and signature decoding and simulation all worked. A first preview of an uncached contract took 0.4–1.8 s. The `/info` client is checked live by `npm run verify:live`.

Any RPC works if it supports `eth_simulateV1`, or `eth_call` with state overrides. Verification used these public, keyless endpoints:
- Ethereum: `https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com` (PublicNode)
- Arbitrum: `https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc`
- HyperEVM: `https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm`

Public endpoints can rate-limit or change without notice; use your own provider in production.

## Contract and token lookups

**HyperEVM simulation.** The public RPC (`rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm`) has no `eth_simulateV1`, `debug_traceCall`, `trace_call`, `eth_createAccessList` or `eth_getProof`, and serves only the latest state. It does have the plain state reads and `eth_call` with state overrides. `RpcSimulator` notices the missing method once and, in live mode, simulates with **`ForkSimulator`** (`signing-preview/fork`), falling back to **`SimulationProbe`**.

`ForkSimulator` runs the transaction in a local EVM (`@ethereumjs/evm`, Cancun rules as HyperEVM uses) over HyperEVM's state, fetched on demand with `eth_getBalance`, `eth_getCode`, `eth_getTransactionCount` and `eth_getStorageAt`, as Foundry's fork mode does:
- It produces every log, so every token, NFT and approval the transaction touches is seen, with no token list. Amounts are read from the state before and after each call, so fee-on-transfer and share-based tokens come out exact; tokens the calls name are read too, which catches WETH-style wrappers that emit `Deposit` instead of `Transfer`. Approvals are read back from the tokens.
- HyperCore's read precompiles (`0x…0800` onward) are answered by the chain itself through `eth_call`, and charged what HyperEVM charges: 1,000 gas plus 33 per input and output byte, measured on mainnet with a contract reading `gasleft()` around calls to six of them. `BLOCKHASH` gives the recent blocks' real hashes. Revert data, gas (with refunds and warm/cold access as in a real transaction) and batches in order are exact.
- Fetching one value per round trip would be far too slow, so each run is speculative: unknown values read as the last value seen (shared across simulations) or zero, and everything the run needed is fetched in parallel JSON-RPC batches (20 per request, HyperEVM's limit) before it runs again. Only a run that used fresh values throughout is accepted. A long chain of dependent reads, such as Kinetiq's diamond router, takes about 10 s the first time and about 1 s once its values have been seen.
- Where the chain's node will say what a call touches (`eth_createAccessList`: every chain here but HyperEVM and, it turns out, Arbitrum One's public node), it is asked first, in one request for up to eight calls (the first four and the last four: a replay's own transaction is the last, and asked for the first four only, one behind four of its block's earlier transactions went without it and took nine rounds, 2026-11-15), and the accounts and slots it names are read with the senders, the targets and the tokens to be read before the first run, so fewer runs find something missing. A node that says it has no such method isn't asked again, and one that hasn't answered within 4 seconds is left at that (Sepolia's public node left a replay's request unanswered for the 15 seconds a state read may take, three times in six). Measured on 8 recent transactions each, traced from the block before theirs with no cache, without and then with it (2026-11-12): Ethereum 43 rounds of reads to 19 and 9.1 s to 4.7 s; Base 47 to 19 and 8.0 s to 5.1 s; OP Mainnet 85 to 17 and 43.6 s to 7.8 s; BNB Chain 26 to 17 and 3.3 s to 3.0 s; on Arbitrum One, whose public node rate-limits by request, a third fewer rounds but about as many requests, and about as long (17–20 s for four or five). Every one traced either way. Base's own contracts (B20 tokens, their registries and factory) aren't read ahead: their storage is read their own way after a block's earlier transactions. `SIGNING_PREVIEW_FORK_PREFETCH=off` (or `prefetch: false`) turns it off.
- A round doesn't run again what the last one got right. Between two calls of a speculative run that has read nothing it had to guess yet, the calls before are what every later run will come to, so their results and the state they left are kept, and the next round starts at the first call that guessed. The reads around the calls (each account's balance of each token watched, whether it is an NFT contract) are run on their own first, while the node's access lists are read, so a token behind a proxy (the token, its implementation, the account's slot in it: three rounds) costs no run of the calls. A view gets 10 million gas; one of code that can't return data (no `RETURN`, no call to other code) isn't run, and one that spent half of it answering nothing (in a run that had guessed no value yet) isn't run again, for that contract and function, in that simulation. Sepolia's blocks carry transactions to a contract that loops through 2.5 million gas whatever it is sent, and it is each one's target, so it was read as a token too: every view of it burnt its 10 million gas, 35 of a 39-second replay. On 13 Sepolia transactions replayed at their exact pre-state behind 3 to 8 earlier transactions of their block, most of those the loops (the fork simulator as `npm run accuracy` runs it, 2026-11-15), 4 had run out of the harness's 120 seconds and the rest took 1.7–131 s (median of all 13: 108 s); now all finish, in 0.9–14 s (median 4.4 s), with the receipt's outcome and gas, and the same token movements as before. On 24 replays on the other chains, behind up to eight earlier transactions (one on Ethereum ran out of its time either way, its node rate limiting the two runs at once), the same results, from about as many state reads in fewer HTTP requests: Base's median 15.6 s became 2.7 s, BNB Chain's 10.3 s 2.8 s, OP Mainnet's 29.4 s 10.4 s, Polygon's 7.1 s 4.7 s; Ethereum's and Arbitrum One's about as before (their slow ones a chain of reads in the block's first call, which every round runs).
- A state read that gets no answer (a request that timed out, a connection reset, a batch answered with some left out, a node of a load balancer a block behind: "block not found") is asked again, within the time budget, as a rate limit is: on BNB Chain's public node eleven replays of a run each failed whole on one request left 15 seconds without an answer (2026-11-14).
- It runs in the next block (latest + 1 s), so interest and time-based values are one second further on than a plain `eth_call` shows.
- If it hasn't finished within 3.5 s (`forkBudgetMs`), the probe is asked, reading the tokens the fork run has seen so far as well as its own. The fork run carries on: if it finishes while the probe is still out, its fuller answer is used; otherwise it fills the caches, so the next preview is fast.
- A token first seen in the first call's logs is read on the state before it on a second EVM, rather than by running everything again, and proxy slots are prefetched only for contracts never seen before.
- In live mode (`npx signing-preview` with `SIGNING_PREVIEW_MODE=live`), a background warm-up at start simulates a Kinetiq stake and every HyperLend reserve's data and oracle price in one batch. The first health-factor read of a HyperLend borrower then takes 9 rounds and 4.1 s instead of 25 rounds and 12.2 s. Without a cache file from an earlier run, it also replays one recent transaction to each of the 12 contracts most used by different accounts in the last minute of blocks (`warmFromTraffic`), two requests at a time. `liveDependencies(env, { warmUp: true })` does the same for your own server.
- The caches survive restarts in a file (`cacheFile`; `SIGNING_PREVIEW_FORK_CACHE` in live mode, `~/.cache/signing-preview/fork-cache.json` for the CLI), at most about 25 MB.
- Measured on 25 real HyperEVM transactions to 25 different contracts (2026-11-05), each simulated from the same starting cache: cold, the median first preview took 4.2 s and 11 of 25 finished within the 3.5 s budget; after the built-in warm-up, 4.5 s and 12; after the traffic warm-up, 2.0 s and 17. The slowest tenth (12–20 s) are the public RPC's rate limit at work (the same number of rounds, twice the requests from retries): a server with its own RPC doesn't have it. A later run, after the first call's tokens were read without another run and proxy slots were prefetched only for new contracts (12 other transactions): median 3.1 s cold, 2.0 s after the built-in warm-up and 1.6 s after the traffic warm-up, in 3 rounds instead of 4.
- Public RPCs rate-limit per IP. State reads back off and retry within the simulation's time budget, but a busy server should use its own RPC.

`SimulationProbe` (`contracts/SimulationProbe.sol`) is the fallback: one `eth_call` sets the probe as the code of the sending account and runs the transaction (or a whole batch, in order) through it, from that account.
- Around each call the probe reads the native balance, ERC-20 balances, allowances, Permit2 allowances, `isApprovedForAll`, and NFT ownership and approvals by token ID. The differences become balance changes and approvals with basis `simulated_state`.
- Without logs, only what is read is seen: the tokens the calls name (their targets, the addresses in their calldata, nested calldata and packed Uniswap paths), HyperEVM's built-in tokens, and tokens decoders name (HyperLend's hTokens and debt tokens). The preview says how many tokens were read.
- With code at the account, contracts may treat it differently. The probe answers ERC-1271 `isValidSignature` the way the account's key would (`ecrecover`), and accepts NFTs as an account without code does. A contract that refuses senders with code makes the probe fail; a plain `eth_call` then confirms the call works, and the preview says its balance changes couldn't be measured.
- An RPC that also ignores state overrides falls back to `eth_call` alone: success or failure and the revert reason, for single calls only.
- `src/probe-code.ts` holds the compiled bytecode; `npm run build:probe` rebuilds it with the pinned solc, and a test checks it matches the source.

How it was checked:
- `ForkSimulator` on Ethereum, whose RPC still has the exact state before recent blocks: 71 transactions replayed after the earlier transactions of their block, with the real block header. All 71 outcomes match the receipts, all 22 of the senders' token movements are exact, and gas matches to the unit in all 71.
- `ForkSimulator` on 120 recent HyperEVM transactions (at most two per sender), replayed at the latest state: where the replay still succeeds, it found every token movement in the receipts (4/4; the probe 3/4). Its outcome differed from the probe's once, on an Arbitrum-style inbox whose fee depends on `block.basefee`: a plain `eth_call` runs with a base fee of 0, while the fork simulator uses the real one.
- `ForkSimulator` against HyperEVM itself, at scale: 150 recent real transactions (at most two per contract, one per sender) run on the latest state through the local EVM and through the node (`eth_call` for outcome and return data, the probe for balance changes) in the same block context. In the 144 where the node's answer didn't change during the run, the outcome agreed in 144, the return data in 47 of 47, and the probe's balance changes in 8 of 9. The 9th is an amount that grows every block (by 1.864 × 10¹⁵ wei), read about 5.6 blocks apart; run back to back, the two differ by exactly one block's growth, the local EVM simulating the next block. The first run of this found the two bugs fixed since: HyperCore precompiles charged about twice their gas (2 of 146 transactions with tight gas limits ran out locally) and `BLOCKHASH` reading zero.
- `ForkSimulator` on HyperEVM: view calls through it return the same bytes as HyperEVM's own `eth_call`, including HyperCore precompile reads (token and perp info, spot balances, oracle prices), Kinetiq's exchange rate and HyperLend's account data across all its reserves (with the same timestamp).
- On Ethereum, whose RPC still has the exact state before recent blocks, 240 transactions from one-off senders were simulated at their parent block with the shipped code and compared with their receipts. 237 succeeded as on-chain (the other 3 depended on an earlier transaction in the same block). 231 of the 235 token movements were found, 230 with the exact amount, and no movement was reported that didn't happen. 8 of the 10 approvals were found; the other two were granted and spent in the same transaction. In an earlier run of the same comparison, the probe and `eth_simulateV1` agreed on 232 of 233 outcomes; the other depended on the block time.
- On HyperEVM, live (`npm run verify:live`, step 10, with each simulator): a Kinetiq stake of 10 HYPE matches Kinetiq's own `HYPEToKHYPE` quote to the wei, a USDC transfer moves exactly 1 USDC, and approve + HyperLend supply in one batch gives −1 USDC and +0.999999 hUSDC (Aave-style balances round). Before that, 8 constructed flows matched independently computed values, including a WHYPE unwrap, a CoreDepositWallet deposit, HYPE to HyperCore and a below-minimum stake.
- On real HyperEVM traffic (2,796 transactions from 300 blocks over the last two hours, 2026-11-05), compared with their receipts: in every transaction whose replay still succeeds (273 that moved the sender's tokens, 26 of them from occasional senders), the preview shows all of the sender's token movements, against 90.8% (62.1% for occasional senders) with the `eth_call` check alone. The RPC only has the state after these transactions, so many replays fail (a balance already spent, a nonce already used); a preview before signing doesn't have that problem.
- `test/fork-simulator.test.ts` runs `ForkSimulator` against contracts in an in-process EVM behind a HyperEVM-shaped node (`test/evm-chain.ts`): exact share-token amounts, batches, approvals without events, Permit2 signatures, NFTs, an EOA-only contract, a forwarded precompile, reverts, round convergence and the fallback to the probe.
- `test/simulation-probe.test.ts` runs the real bytecode in an in-process EVM (`@ethereumjs/evm`) behind a fake HyperEVM-like RPC: transfers, unwrapping, direct and along-the-way approvals (including a forged Permit2 signature), batches, NFTs, reverts, insufficient funds and a contract that refuses senders with code.

**HyperEVM decoding.**
- Reverts with **custom errors** are named from the verified sources: Kinetiq's facets, HyperLend's Aave `Errors` library, OpenZeppelin, and the smart accounts' and modules' that UserOperations meet, among them Rhinestone's SmartSession (both deployments, verified on Sourcify; the 24 errors its deployed code can revert with, as `PolicyViolation(permissionId, policy)`: a policy of the session refuses the operation). For example, "MustBeDivisibleBy1e10: the HYPE amount must be a multiple of 0.00000001 HYPE" or "HealthFactorLowerThanLiquidationThreshold".
- Protocol decoders read live state with `eth_call` themselves, so they don't need simulation.
- The read-only client retries briefly when rate-limited, and keeps results for 15 seconds within a preview.

**Diamond routers.** When a contract's ABI lacks the called function, the resolver asks the contract for its facet (EIP-2535 `facetAddress`, or `getFacetAddress` as on Kinetiq's router) and uses that facet's verified ABI.

**Tokens** are looked up in order, first hit wins:
1. The built-in list (`src/chains.ts`).
2. A token list you supply.
3. The token contract's own `symbol()`, `decimals()` and `name()`. Both `string` and legacy `bytes32` return values are accepted.

On-chain metadata is whatever the contract says about itself, so the preview marks it "self-reported", with an info warning. Two checks run against the built-in list:
- **Danger** `token_impersonation`: a self-reported token claims the symbol of a built-in token on the same chain but has a different address, e.g. a fake "USDC" in an airdrop or address-poisoning scam. Symbols are compared after stripping case and punctuation.
- **Caution** `token_symbol_lookalike`: the symbol uses non-ASCII characters, for example a Cyrillic "С" in "USDС".

A token received in a simulation that has no name to read and no code on the chain yet is one the transaction itself creates. It gets an info note, `token_created_here`: it can't be named before it exists, and when the transaction runs for real it can land at another address than the simulation's, since where a contract is created can depend on who sends the transaction, and when.

**Contract ABIs** come from Sourcify (no key needed), then Etherscan if configured, then any you add with `StaticAbiSource`. When the address is a proxy, the implementation's ABI is used as well. Proxy detection covers:
- EIP-7702 delegated accounts
- EIP-1167 minimal proxies
- EIP-1967 implementation and beacon slots
- the older ZeppelinOS and EIP-1822 slots
- Safe
- an `implementation()` getter

A decoded call must re-encode to exactly the calldata being signed, or it's discarded.

**Function signatures** for contracts without a verified ABI come from three sources:
- a built-in list of about 35 common functions, with real parameter names (batching, Uniswap V2/V3, Aave V3, ERC-4626, NFT transfers, admin calls)
- OpenChain
- 4byte

Anyone can register a name in those databases, so these previews are `partial` and carry a caution.

Checking that a guess re-encodes to the calldata is not enough on its own.
